# Title Page

<column-break/>

->[Image: The monogram of **Albrecht Dürer**]<-

->The page is dominated by a large, stylized letter **A**, characterized by a horizontal bar across its top and serifs at its feet. Nested within the open space at the bottom of the **A** is a smaller, cleanly drawn letter **D**.<-

<note>This "AD" insignia is the world-renowned monogram of Albrecht Dürer (1471–1528), the most influential artist of the German Renaissance. His signature was one of the first "brand logos" in art history, used to protect his woodcuts and engravings from unauthorized copies.</note>

**A D**
<note>This is the iconic monogram of Albrecht Dürer (1471–1528). By placing the 'D' inside the 'A', Dürer created a recognizable brand for his workshop. This monogram acted as a mark of quality and a deterrent against the many contemporary artists who attempted to forge his popular woodcuts and engravings.</note>

<summary>This is the title page of Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion," a foundational Renaissance text that applied mathematical rigor to the study of the human figure. Published in 1528, it represents the culmination of Dürer's life-long effort to provide a scientific basis for German art.</summary>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page (recto and verso). It begins the formal dedication of the work to Willibald Pirckheimer, a prominent Nuremberg humanist and Dürer’s lifelong friend. The signature mark "A ij" indicates this is the second leaf of the first gathering of the printed book.</meta>

<page-type>dedication</page-type>
<sig>A ij</sig>

<image-desc>A large ornamental drop cap letter 'D' decorated with swirling vine and foliage motifs.</image-desc>
To the honorable and well-respected Lord
Wilibald Pirckheymer, Councilor to his Imperial Majesty, etc.,
my favorable dear Lord and greatly helpful 
friend, I, Albrecht Dürer, offer my
willing service.

<image-desc>An ornamental drop cap letter 'W' in a Fraktur or Gothic style.</image-desc>
Although, Favorable Lord and friend, I do not doubt that some will presume to criticize this undertaking of mine, because I, as an unlearned man of little understanding and gifted with little art, dare to write and teach that which I myself have never learned or been instructed in by anyone else. <note>Dürer often used this "humble" persona; although he was a genius, he lacked the formal university education in Latin typical of scholars like Pirckheimer.</note> Nevertheless, because you have repeatedly encouraged and even partly pressed me to publish these books of mine, I have chosen to risk the danger of gossip rather than refuse your request. Although I hope that no one endowed with virtue and understanding will interpret it poorly that I bring to light so generously and for the common benefit of all artists that which I have achieved with great diligence, constant effort, and work—and not without significant loss of temporal wealth <note>original: "zeytlicher." Dürer refers to the time and money spent on his research that could have been used for profitable commissions.</note>—but rather that everyone will praise my good intentions and inclined will, and understand it in the best possible way. Since I have no doubt I will be doing a favor to all lovers of art and those who desire to learn, I must let envy (which leaves nothing unpunished) take its usual course and reply that it is much easier to criticize a thing than to invent it oneself. And it is certainly not untrue that if the books of the ancients who wrote on the arts of painting were still before our eyes, this undertaking of mine might be interpreted poorly, as if I presumed to find something better. <note>Dürer refers to lost classical texts, such as those by Apelles or Parrhasius, which Renaissance artists deeply mourned.</note> But since such books have been entirely lost through the passage of time, I cannot with any fairness be blamed if I—as the ancients also did—release my opinion and invention in writing, so that other knowledgeable people may also be given cause to do the same, and our successors may have something that they can increase and improve, so that the art of painting may in time once again reach its perfection. Yet no one is forced to follow this teaching of mine as if it were entirely perfect in all respects; for human nature has not yet so declined that someone else might not also invent something better. Therefore, everyone may use this instruction of mine as long as it pleases them or until they find something better; if not, they may well consider that this teaching was written not for them, but for others who desire to accept it. For it must be a very weak mind that does not trust itself to invent something further, but always lies on the old path, follows only others, and does not presume to think further. Therefore, it is fitting for every knowledgeable person to follow another in such a way that they do not despair of finding something better in time; for when that happens, there is no doubt that this art may in time once again reach its perfection, as it did in olden days. For it is obvious that the German painters are not a little skilled with their hands and the use of colors, although they have until now lacked the art of measurement, as well as <term>perspective</term> <note>original: "Perspectiva." The mathematical science of representing 3D space on a 2D surface.</note> and other such things. Therefore, it is to be hoped that if they also achieve these, and thus combine practice and art with one another, they will in time yield the prize to no other nation. But no image can ever be perfect without right proportion, even if it is made as diligently as is ever possible; although it is not necessary to make all, and especially very small images, according to measurement, as that would require too much effort. Yet if one [knows] the measurement right and...

<summary>Albrecht Dürer dedicates his treatise to his friend Willibald Pirckheimer, defending his decision to publish his findings on art theory. He argues that while German painters are technically skilled, they lack the mathematical foundations of proportion and perspective used by the ancients.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, Willibald Pirckheimer, artistic theory, German art, perspective, proportion, Renaissance humanism, mathematical art</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page concludes the formal dedication to Wilibald Pirckheimer. Dürer continues his introductory remarks by emphasizing the necessity of a mathematical foundation for artists and justifying his focus on external proportions rather than historical theory.</meta>

...instruction is given and becomes a habit, it becomes subsequently easier to create every figure even without such measurement. So that this instruction of mine might be better understood, I have previously published a book on measurement <note>Dürer refers to his *Underweysung der Messung* (Instruction in Measurement), published in 1525, which provided the geometric foundation for his work on human proportions.</note>, specifically concerning lines, planes, and solids <note>original: "corporē," a Latin abbreviation for *corpore* or three-dimensional bodies.</note>, without which this present teaching cannot be fundamentally understood. Therefore, it is necessary for anyone who intends to undertake this art to first be well-instructed in measurement and gain an understanding of how all things should be laid out in a ground plan and raised up in elevation <note>Dürer uses terms familiar to the building trades: *in grund gelegt* (ground plan) and *auffgezogen* (elevation).</note>, just as skillful <term>stonemasons</term> <note>Craftsmen who cut and shaped stone; in Dürer’s time, they were the keepers of practical geometry.</note> do in their daily practice; for without this, he will not be able to fully comprehend my instruction. 

No one should allow themselves to be discouraged if they do not understand everything immediately; for what is entirely easy cannot be very artistic. But that which is artistic requires diligence, effort, and work until it may be mastered and learned. It is truly a wasted effort when much toil and diligence are spent on a false thing; however, if it possesses its correct proportions, it can be criticized by no one, even if it is made quite simply. In this instruction of mine, I intend to write only about the outer lines of forms and figures, and how these should be drawn from point to point, but not at all about internal matters <note>Dürer is explaining that his book focuses on "surface anatomy" and geometric proportion rather than internal organs or medical anatomy.</note>. 

Now, regarding how old this art is, who first invented it, and in what esteem and dignity it was held among the Greeks and Romans—as well as how a good painter or craftsman should be skilled—it is unnecessary to write of that here. However, whoever desires to have such knowledge should read *Pliny* and *Vitruvius* <note>Dürer points the reader toward classical authorities: Pliny the Elder, who wrote on the history of art, and Vitruvius, the Roman architect whose work defined the "perfect" proportions of man.</note>, and they will receive sufficient instruction regarding those matters. 

However, so that these books of mine might find a protector against slander, and so that I might also demonstrate my favorable will toward your Lordship—out of the manifold dear friendship and goodness you have shown me over a long time and in many ways—I have dedicated them to your Lordship <note>original: "ewr herrligkeit," a formal honorific meaning "Your Lordship" or "Your Glory."</note>. This I do with a spirit that I cannot otherwise demonstrate through my work, with the request that you will interpret this undertaking of mine in the best possible light and, as you have always done, remain my favorable Lord and patron. In this I shall place my full trust, and I will be diligent to deserve it as best I can.

<image-desc>A large, elaborate calligraphic flourish with multiple loops and overlapping lines, serving as a decorative terminal ornament for the text block.</image-desc>

<summary>Dürer concludes his dedication by stressing that artists must master geometry and measurement before attempting human proportion, while citing Pliny and Vitruvius as historical authorities. He formally presents the work to Pirckheimer, seeking his protection and patronage against potential critics.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, measurement, stonemasons, Pliny the Elder, Vitruvius, artistic theory, patronage, Wilibald Pirckheimer</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general philosophical introduction of the previous page to the practical application of Dürer’s geometric system. Dürer begins his first book by defining the "Divider" (Teiler), the fundamental tool used to ensure proportional accuracy across figures of different sizes.</meta>

## Beginning of the First Book.

<image-desc>A decorative calligraphic flourish.</image-desc>

First, when I set out and intend to create a human <term>likeness</term> <note>original: "pildnus," referring here to a figure or representation</note>, I make use of this method: I take a ruler <note>original: "richtscheyt"</note> longer than the figure is to be; on it, I draw a straight line as long as the figure should be, so that one end touches the highest point of the crown <note>original: "scheytel"</note> and the other the sole of the foot <note>original: "soln"</note>. And whether a figure be tall or short, I draw for each figure its own individual line and divide it specifically. Therefore, as often as I hereafter name a part by its number, I always take it from the length of the whole figure between the crown and the sole.

Thus I carefully divide this length—which in its number is now one—into from two up to fifty or a hundred parts, as many as I may need. I mark them with points <note>original: "punctire"</note> on the ruler next to the long line, and set them all at the top equal to the height of the crown, and mark them all with their numerals, such as 2, 3, 4, etc. Through this method, the smallest numbers result in the longest parts, and the largest numbers result in the shortest parts. <note>Dürer is describing a fractional scale where the number represents the divisor: "2" indicates 1/2 of the body's total height, while "10" indicates 1/10th. Thus, a larger number denotes a smaller fraction of the body.</note> Thus, of the whole length, the half part is 2, the third part is 3, the fourth part is 4, and so on as mentioned above.

And when I need to, I also divide any previously determined part—be it long or short—into as many parts as I wish, even or odd. For whoever wishes to measure very precisely must make small subdivisions when he cannot reach everything with a single predetermined part. Therefore, I hereafter put together two or three types of numbers, large or small, even or odd, however I may come closest to the measurement. Such numbers, as I set them down hereafter, may also be reversed and used or applied according to each person's pleasure. But for better understanding, I will draw the <term>divider</term> <note>original: "teyler," a scale or proportional gauge</note> now described; for this divider must first be made correctly and certainly. Otherwise, much time and effort would be spent if I always had to search for every length of a named part within the total length between the crown and the sole.

->How I draw the figures, however, follows hereafter.<-

<image-desc>A decorative calligraphic flourish.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A technical diagram on the right side of the page. It features a long vertical line representing the total height of a human figure. At the top, the line is labeled "Scheytel" (Crown) and "Teiler" (Divider). To the right of this main line, nine shorter vertical lines of varying lengths are drawn, all starting from the same top point. These are labeled with numerals from 2 to 10, visually demonstrating how the figure's height is divided into halves, thirds, fourths, and so on. At the bottom of the main vertical line is the label "Soln" (Sole).</image-desc>

<summary>Dürer introduces his "Divider" system, a proportional scale where a figure's total height is divided into fractions (1/2, 1/3, etc.) to allow for precise measurement regardless of the figure's actual size. He explains that larger numbers represent smaller body parts, creating a flexible mathematical framework for artists.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, measurement, ruler, crown, sole, fractional scale, Divider, geometry in art</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general explanation of the "Divider" scale to its practical application. Dürer now demonstrates how to use these fractional measurements to construct a specific figure: a stockily-built "peasant man."</meta>

First, I take as my subject a **thick peasant man** who should be seven of his own heads in height. For this purpose, I draw a straight horizontal line <note>original: "zwerchlini"</note>, and upon it, I place three upright, straight lines at the proper distance from one another, each as long as the figure is to be. I use the first for the **side view** <note>original: "seytlichen"</note> of the man, the second for the **front view** <note>original: "fürsichtigen"</note>, and the third for the **back view** <note>original: "hinderwertigen"</note>. While these lines stand parallel, I first measure the length of the man's limbs between the crown <note>original: "scheitel"</note> and the sole <note>original: "soln"</note>. I mark these same lengths across the three upright lines using horizontal lines at right angles. These lengths of the limbs, as they are described by their numbers and figures, I place outside the drawing with straight upright lines, so that they may be recognized immediately and each specific length of the limbs may be found quickly. 

The principal points of the horizontal lines will be these (though sometimes there may be more or fewer): The first height is the **crown**. The next line below it I call the **forehead line**. Then follows the **eyebrow**, then the **nose**, the **chin**, and further the height of the **shoulder muscles**, the height of the **hollow of the throat**, the height of the **chest**, the **front markers**, the **rear markers** <note>original: "vren," likely referring to the anatomical boundaries of the torso or flanks</note>, the **nipples**, the area **under the breasts**, the **waist**, the **navel**, the **hip joint**, the **end of the hip**, the **end of the belly**, the **pubic area**, the **end of the pubic area**, the **end of the buttocks**, the **indentation** in the middle of the upper leg, then the **outer point above the knee**, the **inner point above the knee**, the **middle of the knee**, the **outer point below the knee**, the **inner point below the knee**, the **end of the outer calf**, the **end of the inner calf**, the height of the **instep** of the foot, the end of the **outer ankle bone** on the shinbone, and finally, at the very bottom, the **end of the sole**.

I will place such words at the ends of the upright lines that indicate the length of the limbs, so that all of this may be easily understood. I intend to use this method for all figures throughout this entire book.

Now, I first measure the length of the limbs as follows:

From the crown to:
- the height of the hollow of the throat: a 10th part and an 11th part. <note>Dürer defines lengths as fractions of the total body height; here, 1/10 + 1/11.</note>
- the height of the shoulder: two 11th parts.
- the end of the chin: a 7th part.

The height of the **vortex** <note>original: "wirbels," the crown or whorl of hair</note> at the back of the head is halfway between the crown and the forehead.

From the chin upward to where the hair begins above the forehead is a 10th part. I divide that into three equal fields: the uppermost for the **forehead**, the second for the **eyes and nose**, and the third for the **mouth and chin**.

From the hollow of the throat downward to:
- the height of the chest: a 30th part.
- under the armpits: a 13th part.
- the nipples: a 10th part.
- under the breasts: an 8th part.
- the waist: two 11th parts.
- the navel: a 40th part.
- the hip joint: a 30th part.

From the height of the waist downward to:
- the end of the hip: a 10th part.
- the pubic area: an 8th part.
- the end of the same: a 6th part.
- the end of the buttocks: a 10th part and an 11th part.

From the end of the buttocks to the indentation of the leg: an 18th part.

From the sole upward to the bottom of the ankle bone of the shin: a 28th part.

But from the sole upward to the highest point of the instep: a 20th part.

Now that I have measured the body of the figure by its length down to the end of the hip, I will subsequently place the knee joint in its position. This will result in the figure having three unequal lengths: namely, the body from the height of the hollow of the throat to the end of the hip is the first and longest; the second, from the end of the hip to the middle of the knee, is shorter; the third, from the middle of the knee to the end of the shinbone, is the shortest of all; for the rear limbs shall...

<summary>Dürer outlines the specific proportional measurements for a "thick peasant man," providing a detailed vertical scale from the crown to the sole. He establishes a system of fractional measurements to define anatomical landmarks for use in front, side, and back views.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, peasant man, anatomy, artistic measurement, vertical scale, Renaissance art theory, geometric drawing</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for proportioning a human figure, specifically focusing on the relationship between the torso, the upper leg, and the lower leg. It follows the previous page's list of anatomical landmarks by introducing a geometric tool Dürer calls the "proportional divider."</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>A iiii</sig>

...be longer and stronger than the front [upper] ones, as can be observed in humans. I take the torso <note>original: "leib," here referring to the trunk</note> to be a long part, although assembled from many pieces, yet undivided, and powerful enough to move the other limbs. These three lengths should hold themselves proportionately to one another: just as the length of the torso relates to the upper leg, so should the length of the upper leg relate to the length of the shinbone. However, I do not use this in all figures; I shall do it thus:

I draw a triangle $a.b.c$. Let $a.b$ be the horizontal side, and $b.c$ the vertical side, so that $b$ remains a right angle. Then I divide the vertical side $b.c$ with two points, $d$ and $e$, into three equal fields, and draw two straight lines from angle $a$ to the two points $d$ and $e$. From this, I seek the proportion as follows:

I take a straightedge <note>original: "richtscheit"</note> and mark upon it with two points the length of the torso, between the hollow of the throat and the end of the hip. Likewise, below that, the end of the outer bony bump <note>original: "knorren," referring to the lateral malleolus or ankle bone</note> at the bottom of the shinbone. I label the top point of the hollow of the throat with an $f$, the end of the hip with a $g$, and the end of the shinbone with an $h$. Then I take this straightedge and place point $g$ on line $a.d$ of the aforementioned triangle. I do not let this point move from that line, yet I slide the straightedge back and forth on the line until the top point $f$ touches the triangle’s line $a.c$ and the bottom point $h$ touches the horizontal line $a.b$. Once I have done this, the line $a.e$ intersects the straightedge between $g$ and $h$ and, together with the length of the torso, creates three proportionate parts. I mark this point with an $i$. Thus it follows: as the length $f.g$ relates to the length $g.i$, so also does the length $g.i$ relate to the length $i.h$.

Thus, I mark at point $f$ the height of the hollow of the throat; at point $g$ the end of the hip; at point $i$ the middle of the knee; and at point $h$ the end of the shinbone. It is also to be noted that when I place my straightedge in the triangle, I incline the top at point $f$ toward the vertical side $c.b$. This triangle may well be called a <term>proportional divider</term> <note>original: "vergleicher," a tool or method for ensuring consistent ratios</note>. This method can also be used in reverse if one wishes to invert something. This method also draws many other useful things into the work for those who can use it correctly. Now I apply this height $i$ to the figure I am drawing later, and place the knee at that same spot; then I measure further.

From the middle of the knee upwards onto the knee: a 21st part.

->a 40th part; there I end the knee.<-

From the middle of the knee downwards:
{ two 19th parts; there I end the outer calf.
{ an 8th part; there I end the inner calf.

And before I go further, I will draw this triangle—the "proportional divider"—here opposite.

---

<image-desc>A large, calligraphic decorative flourish in ink at the bottom of the page, consisting of interlocking loops and a sweeping horizontal tail.</image-desc>

<summary>Dürer explains a geometric method using a right-angled triangle (the "proportional divider") to calculate the ideal ratios between the torso, upper leg, and lower leg. He provides specific fractional measurements for the placement of the knee and calf muscles.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, proportional divider, anatomy, knee measurements, calf measurements, Albrecht Dürer, artistic theory</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical instructions for drafting the human figure, specifically focusing on the "thickness" (depth in profile) and "width" (in front view) of various body parts based on his proportional system.</meta>

Now, regarding the first vertical line of the man in profile, I will first indicate the thicknesses on all the horizontal lines <note>The "thickness" here refers to the depth of the body from front to back when viewed from the side.</note> that are drawn through the length of the limbs.

First, at the very top of the head, on the closest line under the crown, I make the head <term>thick through the back vertex</term> <note>original: "dick durch den hindern wirbel"</note> one 9th part. 
Across the forehead, one 14th part and one 15th part. 
Across the eyebrows, one 7th part. 
Across the nose, one 8th part. 
Across the chin and neck, also one 8th part. 
And the neck below the chin and jaw, I make it one 12th part thick. 
Across the <term>pit of the throat</term> <note>original: "halsgrüblein"; the suprasternal notch at the base of the neck.</note>, one 9th part. 
At the height of the chest, two 13th parts. 
At the <term>armpits</term> <note>original: "ochssen"</note>, one 6th part. 
Across the <term>nipples</term> <note>original: "tütlen"</note>, one 6th part. 
Below the breasts, one 12th part and one 13th part. 
In the <term>flanks</term> <note>original: "weychen"; the soft area of the waist.</note>, one 12th part and one 13th part. 
At the hip joint, one 6th part. 
At the end of the hip, across the belly and buttocks, one 11th part and one 12th part. 
At the <term>groin</term> <note>original: "scham"</note> of the man, also one 11th part and one 12th part. 
And I make the leg below the buttocks one 7th part thick. 
But below that, in the middle of the upper leg, one 14th part and one 15th part. 
Above the knee, one 10th part. 
In the middle of the knee, one 12th part. 
Below the knee, also one 12th part. 
In the middle of the calf, where it is thickest, one 20th part and one 21st part. 
At the end of the inner calf, one 13th part. 
Below, above the instep of the foot where the leg is thinnest, one 18th part. 
And I make the foot one 6th part long.

Thereafter, I make the thicknesses of the side-view arm: at the <term>shoulder</term> <note>original: "achssel"</note>, two 21st parts. 
But below the armpit, one 13th part. 
At the elbow, one 18th part. 
In front of the elbow, in the strongest part of the arm, also one 18th part. 
And at the hand, one 22nd part. 
And I make the hollow of the hand one 30th part thick.

And in the drawing, I wish to place the arms

<column-break/>

beside the figures, separately, so that such things do not cause confusion.

Next, I will set down the widths of the parts on all the horizontal lines relative to the lengths of the limbs, following the other vertical line of the man in front view.

I make the man’s head, on the line of the back vertex, one 10th part wide. 
Across the forehead, one 8th part. 
Across the eyebrows, one 9th part. 
Across the ears, one 8th part. 
Across the nose, one 10th part. 
And the neck below the chin is one 12th part wide. 
Between the shoulder joints at the height of the pit of the throat, one 5th part. 
Across the chest, three 10th parts. 
Between the armpits, one 5th part. 
Between the nipples, two 15th parts. 
In the flanks, one 5th part. 
At the hip joint, one 9th part and two 19th parts. 
At the end of the hip, one 4th part. 
And on this line, the legs stand one 6th part wide apart from each other at the hips. 
At the groin, also one 4th part. 
But the leg at the end of the buttocks, two 17th parts. 
At the <term>indentation of the leg</term> <note>original: "einpeisung"; the inward curve of the inner thigh.</note>, one 10th part.

This indentation is to be noted on the inner side of the legs in the front-view figure.

Thereafter, I make the leg above the knee one 12th part wide. 
In the middle of the knee, one 14th part. 
Below the knee, two 26th parts. 
In the middle of the calf, one 22nd part and one 24th part. 
At the end of the inner calf, one 14th part.

Below in the <term>shinbone<note>original: "schinbein"</note></term>, a 27th part.
Through the <term>ankles<note>original: "knorren"; referring to the bony protrusions of the ankle (malleoli).</note></term>, a 22nd part.

And make the heels behind a 24th part wide.

Thus, when I have now all lengths, thicknesses, and widths

<column-break/>

orderly described and marked by the three upright lines, I then draw the figure with lines according to my best judgment—or, if I can have one, I place a well-proportioned person before me and draw the lines according to him; that is always better than being made according to one’s own imagination.

It is also especially to be noted, when one draws the lines of the man’s figure, how nature has made the man so masterfully, as if he were made of two pieces: the body set upon the thighs. On both sides from the hip area, a curve goes down around the belly, and likewise backward over the buttocks; this I then follow in the <term>construction sketch<note>original: "auffreissen"; the technical process of drawing or laying out the proportions.</note></term>, as can be seen hereafter before your eyes in the side-view, front-view, and back-view images.

I designate this man with an **A**.

<meta>This page begins a new section detailing the proportions of a "strong peasant woman," following a previous section on male proportions. The author, Albrecht Dürer, transitions here from using full horizontal lines to using a system of points and numerical fractions to avoid cluttering the technical drawings.</meta>

Next, I will describe a strong, peasant woman <note>original: "pewrisch weyb"; Dürer categorized human figures by social type and physical build</note> who is seven heads tall, matching the scale of the man described previously. I will do this following the same order as for the man. However, instead of the three upright lines, I will place points in those same locations to mark the length of the limbs. I will no longer draw horizontal lines through these points—neither for the thickness nor the width of the side-view, front-view, or back-view figures. Instead, I will place the numbers and digits at the same location where the horizontal lines would have been, next to the points. These indicate how thick or wide the side-view or front-view figure should be at that spot. I will use this method for all subsequent figures so that the drawings do not become too cluttered.

->Now I first measure the limbs according to their length.<-

From the crown of the head to the height of the shoulder joints: one 10th part and two 23rd parts.
But from the crown to the pit of the throat <note>the suprasternal notch</note>: two 11th parts.
From the crown to the end of the chin: a 7th part.
From the chin upwards to the height of the rear vertebra <note>the atlas or the nape of the neck</note>: an 8th part.
But from the chin upwards to the end of the forehead: a 10th part.
I divide this tenth part into three equal parts: in the uppermost, I place the forehead; in the second, the nose, eyes, and ears; in the third, the mouth and chin. And beneath the chin, I make the flesh descend further.

<margin>The armpit is always lower in the back than in the front.</margin>
From the height of the pit of the throat:
To the waist: a 5th part.
Under the breasts: a 7th part.
At the nipples <note>original: "tütlein"</note>: a 9th part.
Under the front armpit: a 15th part.
At the height of the breast: a 28th part.

From the waist:
To the navel: a 22nd part.
To the end of the hip: a 9th part.
To the end of the belly: an 8th part.
To the end of the genitals <note>original: "scham"</note>: two 11th parts.
To the end of the buttocks: a 5th part.

From the sole of the foot upwards to the height of the instep: a 20th part.
But from the sole to the outer ankle <note>original: "knorren"</note> of the shinbone: a 28th part.
From the end of the ankle upwards to the middle of the knee: two 9th parts.
Or, I position the knee using the divider <note>original: "vergleicher"; a tool for comparing proportions</note> as I did before, which will make the knee lower than before; do whichever you prefer. From the middle of the knee upwards: a 30th part, where I end the knee; likewise, a 30th part below it, where I end the bottom of the knee. From the middle of the knee downwards: an 8th part, where I end the calf, and I make the foot one 6th part long.
Next, I make the arm from the shoulder joint to the elbow: two 11th parts. From the elbow to the end of the fingers: a 4th part.
From the end of the fingers backward to the wrist joint: a 10th part.

Since I have indicated the length of the limbs, I will next measure the thickness at all the points of the previously established lengths on the side-view figure.

I make the side-view head nine parts thick through the point of the rear vertebra.
Now, always pay attention to the points so you do not lose your way.
But at the point of the forehead: a 14th part and a 15th part.

Over the eyebrows also: a 14th part and a 15th part.
Over the head and nose: an 8th part.
Over the chin and neck: also an 8th part.
And the neck: a 12th part.
But at the pit of the throat: a 10th part.
At the height of the breast: a 7th part.

Next, I want to describe a strong, **peasant woman** <note>original: "pewrisch weyb"</note>, seven heads in length, according to the same scale as the previous man. I will arrange her measurements just as I did for the man. However, instead of using three vertical lines, I will place points in those locations to mark the lengths of the limbs. I will no longer draw cross-lines through these points for the thickness or width of the side-view, front-view, or back-view figures. Instead, I will place the numbers and digits at the same spot as the horizontal line by the points, which will indicate how thick or wide the side or front image should be at that location. I will use this method for all subsequent figures so that the drawing <note>original: "riß"</note> does not become too cluttered.

Now, I first measure the limbs according to their length.
From the crown of the head to the height of the shoulder joints: a 1/10th part and two 1/23rd parts.
From the crown to the hollow of the throat <note>the "halßgrüblein" is the suprasternal notch at the base of the neck</note>: two 1/11th parts.

From the chin upwards to the top of the forehead: a 1/10th part.
I divide this tenth part into three equal sections: In the uppermost, I place the forehead; in the second, the nose, eyes, and ears; in the third, the mouth and chin. And beneath the chin, I bring the flesh further down.

B iij

Next, I will describe a man who shall be eight heads tall. In this, I use the nearest frontal arrangement and first measure the length of the limbs as follows:

From the height of the crown of the head to the hollow of the throat is a 6th part.
But from the crown of the head to the end of the chin is an 8th part.
From the end of the chin upward to the top of the forehead is a 10th part.
Within that, I divide the face by length as previously mentioned.
Next, I measure from the crown of the head to the waist <term>waist</term> <note>original: "weichen"; the soft area of the torso between the ribs and the hips</note> a 3rd part.
From the height of the hollow of the throat to under the armpits <note>original: "ochssen"; an archaic term for the armpit</note> is a 14th part. However, on the back, the armpits are lower.
From the height of the hollow of the throat to the nipples <note>original: "tütlein"; literally 'little teats'</note> is a 10th part.
From the crown of the head to the crotch <note>original: "spaltung des manns"; literally the 'splitting of the man'</note> is a 2nd part.

From the waist to
<margin>
{ the navel is a 29th part.
{ the hip joint is an 18th part.
{ the end of the hip is a 20th part and an 18th part.
{ the pubic area is two 13th parts.
{ the end of the buttocks is a 10th part and an 11th part.
</margin>

From the end of the buttocks to the end of the kidneys <note>referring to the lower back area</note> is a 40th part.
From the end of the buttocks to the crease of the leg is a 15th part.
From the end of the sole upward to the outer ankle bone <term>malleolus</term> <note>original: "knorren"; the bony protrusion of the shinbone at the ankle</note> of the shin is a 27th part.
But from the end of the ankle bone upward to the height of the instep <note>original: "riß"; the arched top of the foot</note> is a 21st part.
From the end of the aforementioned ankle bone upward to the middle of the knee is a 4th part.
Alternatively, I place the knee at its position using the divider <note>original: "vergleicher"; a specialized measuring tool or proportional compass used by Dürer</note>, as shown in the first image.
From the middle of the knee upward a 30th part is where the knee ends.

From the middle of the knee downward
<margin>
{ a 30th part is where the knee ends.
{ a 9th part is where the outer calf ends.
{ a 15th part and a 16th part is where the inner calf ends.
</margin>

Afterward, I determine the length of the arm.
From the height of the hollow of the throat, across the shoulder bones to the elbow, is a 5th part.
From the elbow to the tips of the fingers is a 4th part.
From the tips of the fingers to the wrist <note>original: "gelenck der hand"</note> is a 10th part.

->Afterward, I determine the thickness of the man from the side.<-

At the first point of the back of the neck, the thickness is a 10th part.
Across the eyebrows, an 8th part.
Across the head and nose, a 9th part.
Across the chin and neck, a 10th part.
And the neck under the chin is a 16th part, and further down a 14th part.
At the hollow of the throat, a 12th part.
At the height of the breasts, a 7th part.
Across the nipples, a 7th part.
Under the breasts, a 14th part and a 15th part.
At the waist, a 16th part and a 17th part.
Over the navel, a 17th part and an 18th part.
At the hip joint, an 8th part.
At the end of the hip, a 7th part.
Across the leg and buttocks at the pubic area, two 15th parts.

At the elbow, a 24th part.

Afterward, I will describe a proportionate woman corresponding to the man previously described; she shall also be eight heads long, for which I will use the frontal arrangement.

I first measure the lengths of the limbs:
From the top of the crown to the pit of the throat: one-sixth part. <note>Dürer uses a fractional system where "one-sixth part" means 1/6th of the total body height.</note>
Again, from the top of the crown to the end of the chin: one-eighth part.
From the end of the chin upward to the top of the forehead: one-tenth part.
This tenth part I also divide into three equal parts, as previously mentioned, for the forehead, nose, and chin, to divide them equally.
From the pit of the throat to the waist: one-fifth part.
To under the armpits: one-sixteenth part.
To the nipples: one-eleventh part.
To under the breasts: one-ninth part.
From the waist to the end of the buttocks: one-eleventh part and one-fortieth part.
To the end of the private parts: one-thirteenth part and one-fourteenth part.
To the end of the hip: one-tenth part.
To the navel: one-fortieth part.
From the sole of the foot upward to the bottom of the ankle bone of the shin: one-thirtieth part.
To the height of the instep: one-twenty-first part.
To the middle of the knee: one-eighth part and one-ninth part.
Or I set the knee in its place using the comparer. <note>The "comparer" refers to Dürer’s specialized proportional scale or alignment tool used to ensure consistency across different views of the body.</note>
From the middle of the knee upward: one-twenty-sixth part, where I end the knee.
From the middle of the knee downward to the end of the calf, I set: one-eighth part.
After that, I determine the length of the arm:
From the shoulder joint to the elbow: one-fifth part.
From the elbow to the end of the fingers: one-fourth part.
From the end of the fingers backward to the wrist: one-tenth part.
Next, I determine the thickness of the stout woman:
In the head across the forehead: one-ninth part.

Or I change these two previously prepared figures as follows, and yet allow some parts to remain as they were before, which I will name in the description and describe the figure in its entirety.

First, the man according to his length.

From the crown of the head to:
- The pit of the throat <term>pit of the throat: the suprasternal notch, the small dip at the base of the neck</term>: two 1/11 parts.
- The height of the shoulder flesh <note>the trapezius muscle/neck-shoulder line</note>: a 1/7 part.
- The end of the chin: a 1/8 part.

From the end of the chin upwards to the end of the forehead is a 1/10 part.
From this I make three equal parts <note>Dürer refers to the classical division of the face into three equal lengths: forehead, nose, and the area from nose to chin</note>, as mentioned before.

From the height of the pit of the throat to:
- The flanks: two 1/11 parts.
- Under the breasts: a 1/9 part.
- Upon the nipples: a 1/11 part.

- The navel: a 1/35 part.

From the end of the buttocks downwards: a 1/40 part; there the leg has a distinct indentation in the back.
But from the end of the buttocks downwards: a 1/14 part; there I make the curve <note>original: "wůnen," likely referring to the inward curve or hollow of the inner thigh</note> on the inside of the upper leg.

But to the end of the inner calf: a 1/9 part.
And I make the foot a 1/6 part long.

From the elbow to the wrist <note>original: "gelenck der hand"</note>: a 1/7 part.
From there to the end of the fingers: a 1/10 part.

At the joint of the hand <note>the wrist</note>: one 36th part.
And the open hand: one 20th part.

<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of human proportions, moving from shorter figures to a taller, "nine-head" model. It maintains the same fractional measurement system used in previous sections of the treatise.</meta>

In the following, I will describe a man who is nine of his own heads in height, maintaining the same order as with the previous figures. Regarding the division of his length, it is much like the previous figures, and must be as follows:

From the height of the crown to the end of the chin is 1/9th part.
From the end of the chin upward to the top of the forehead is 1/10th part.
I also place the rear crown <note>The vertex or back of the skull.</note> of the man's head at this same height.
And within this 1/10th, I divide the face exactly as previously mentioned.
However, from the crown to the height of the shoulder muscles, I set 1/15th and 1/16th parts.
Again, from the crown to the height of the <term>hollow of the neck</term><note>The suprasternal notch at the base of the throat.</note> is 1/6th part.
From the height of the hollow of the neck down:
{
to the height of the breast is 1/28th part.
to under the armpits is 1/14th part.
upon the nipples is 1/12th part.
under the breast is 2/19th parts.
to the waist is 1/6th part.
to the navel is 1/26th part.
to the hip joint is 1/22nd part.

From the waist down:
{
to the end of the hip is 1/9th part.
to the end of the belly is 1/8th part.
to the groin is 1/7th part.
to the bottom of the buttocks is 1/6th part.
}
From the bottom of the buttocks downward to the inner <term>insertion of the leg</term><note>The point where the thigh meets the torso.</note> is 1/11th part.
From the sole of the foot upward to the height of the instep is 1/23rd part.
However, from the sole upward to the <term>ankle bone</term><note>original: "knorren des schinpeins"</note> of the shin is 1/35th part.
From the end of the ankle bone to the middle of the knee is 1/4th part.
From the middle of the knee to the outer side of the knee is 1/20th part.
From the middle of the knee to the inner side above the knee is 1/30th part.
But from the middle of the knee:
{

to the inner side under the knee is 1/40th part.
to the end of the outer calf is 1/10th part.
to the end of the inner calf is 1/9th part.

If I wish to make the man's head taller than it was before, I take 1/8th of the length between the crown and the sole, and place this length upward from the chin; I thus raise the head with it and otherwise let all things remain as they were before, except for the crown at the back of the head, which I place as high as the head was before. And on the **profile head**, I draw the forehead further forward above the eyebrows; thus, this man becomes approximately eight of his heads long, as has also been illustrated.

Now I wish to create a long, thin man; he shall be ten heads tall. For this, use the previously described method <note>referring to Dürer’s system of dividing the total body height into fractional parts</note> and first measure the length of the limbs.

From the height of the crown to the end of the chin, I set one 10th part.
From there upwards to the end of the forehead, one 11th part.
In this length, I create the face as often mentioned before.
But from the height of the crown to the height of the <term>shoulder flesh</term> <note>the trapezius muscle area</note>, two 17th parts.
To the <term>hollow of the neck</term> <note>the suprasternal notch at the base of the throat</note>, one 13th part and one 14th part.
To the height of the shoulder, one 6th part.
From the height of the shoulder to the height of the breast, one 25th part.
To under the <term>armpits</term> <note>original: "ochsen," an archaic term for the axilla</note>, one 17th part.
To the nipples, one 13th part.
To under the breasts, two 21st parts.
To the flanks, two 13th parts.
From the flanks to the navel, one 30th part.
To the hip joint, one 21st part.
To the end of the hip, one 8th part.
To the pubic area, one 14th part and one 15th part.
To the end of the same, two 13th parts.
To the end of the buttocks, two 11th parts.
From the end of the buttocks downwards to the indentation inside the leg, one 11th part.
Between the end of the buttocks and the sole of the foot, I place the knee here in the middle.
From the sole upwards, one 35th part; there I end the <term>ankle bone</term> <note>the medial malleolus</note> at the bottom of the shin.
But from the sole upwards, one 26th part; I set the height of the instep on the foot.
From the middle of the knee upwards, one 30th part; there I end the top of the knee.
But from the middle of the knee downwards, one 40th part; there I end the bottom of the knee.
Again, from the middle of the knee downwards, on the outside one 10th part, and on the inside one 9th part; there I end the calves on the leg on both sides.
And I make the foot one 7th part long.
Afterwards, I make the length of the arm:
From the shoulder joint to the elbow joint, two 11th parts.

Next, I will present a female figure of equal proportion to the man described just before, in the order often mentioned. I will first set the points for the length of the body and measure the length of the limbs within them.

From the height of the crown to the end of the chin: 1/10th part.

To the height of the shoulders: 1/6th part.

--- Page 65 ---
These previously described figures I demonstrate in the joints through the spine and in all other limbs, as every person should know beforehand. However, to provide a clear understanding here, I wish to first show the previously mentioned figures from the side view. I place the spine in the rear quarter of the body's thickness and mark it with small rings <note>Dürer uses small circles/rings in his diagrams to indicate the pivot points of joints</note>. Likewise, for the front-facing figures, I indicate some of these same joints with small rings.

And although I have measured the previous figures all to the same length using a divided scale for the sake of less effort, if one wanted to put them into practice and arrange the figures together, the thinner figures should always be made longer than the thicker ones.

And when the man and the woman of the same type are arranged together, the line from which the woman is measured should be taken as an 18th part shorter than the man's line is; otherwise, the woman would appear larger than the man. This is because her naked body should be drawn fleshier and softer <note>original: "molleter"</note> than the man's, whereas the man should be of harder build and less fleshy.

I have established these wide differences in the previously made proportions of the figures—from the strongest to the weakest—so that everyone may find a reason to change and distinguish their design according to their preference, as I will further show in the following booklets.

Since I have shown four kinds of differences in the figures above, I will now describe and draft a man’s head more clearly than before, but using the same order. First, make the head as seen from the side within a perfect square <term>vierung</term> <note>a quadratic frame used as a geometric guide</note>, one-eighth of the man's total height in both height and width.

However, for this head, I will take the measurements for the drafting from a longer line than those in which the aforementioned figures were described, in order to show the small parts more clearly because of the greater space.

When I set up this square, I mark the front upright side of the plane—where the nose is to protrude—with an **a**, and the rear upright side with **b**. But the upper horizontal side is marked with **c** and the lower with **d**. After that, I divide the parts of the face, first according to length with horizontal lines <term>zwerchlinien</term> <note>cross-lines or dividers</note> all parallel to one another, and I assign each its letter; these indicate the high and low parts of the face.

First, I draw a horizontal line **e** above side **d**, at a height of one-tenth, as I mentioned previously regarding the man of eight head-lengths. This line separates the hair from the bareness of the forehead in front; but I place the rear crown of the head one-third higher between **c** and **e**. Then I divide **e** and **d** with two lines, **f** and **g**, into three equal fields. The upper line **f** should touch the eyebrows and the tops of the ears, but line **g** touches the bottom of the nose and the ears, and the head has its indentation on the neck at that same place in the back, as was partially shown before.

Then I divide **e** and **f** with two points into three equal fields, and draw a horizontal line **h** through the lower point. This line touches the masculine bulge of the forehead above the eyebrows; from there the forehead rounds upward to the beginning of the hair. But if I divide **f** and **g** with two points into three equal fields, I cut off the upper third with a horizontal line **i**; thus, the whole eye is contained between **f** and **i**, and the two corners of the eyes end at the back and front in the middle between **f** and **i**.

But if I divide **f** and **g** with three points into four equal fields, I cut off the bottom fourth with a horizontal line **k**. This line touches the top of the nostrils <note>original: "naspellen"</note>, and the lower earlobe is also contained between **k** and **g**. After that, I divide **g** and **d** with a line **l** into two equal fields; this line touches the top of the chin, just as **d** does the bottom. But if I divide **g** and **l** in two with a line **m**, this line goes through the middle of the mouth. I place the upper lip <note>original: "lebzen"</note> in the lower third between **g** and **m**, but the upper

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s detailed geometric construction of the human head, shifting from general proportions to the specific placement of facial features within a profile grid. It establishes the "depth" of the head using a system of vertical and diagonal lines within a square frame.</meta>

I assign two-thirds to the groove <note>the philtrum</note> under the nose. I place the lower lip in the upper half-part between **.l.** and **.m.**, but in the lower half-part, I place the hollow of the throat below the lip and above the chin. Thus, with these horizontal lines <term>zwerchlinien</term>, the most prominent parts of the high and low features of the head are indicated.

Furthermore, I will indicate the depth of the face with vertical parallel lines; by depth, understand what moves backward from the front where the nose stands out.

First, I divide the two vertical sides **.a.** and **.b.** with six vertical lines **.n.o.p.q.r.s.** into seven equal fields. The first line **.n.** touches the front of the eyeball and the corners of the mouth. However, I end the back corners of the eyes in the middle between the vertical lines **.n.** and **.o.**, for these features should be understood as being applied to both sides in practice. After that, I draw a diagonal line <term>ort lini</term> from the corner formed by the horizontal side **.c.** and the vertical **.n.** into the corner of the two lines **.a.** and **.f.**, where I place a **.t.** After that, I draw another diagonal line, but from the aforementioned corner **.c.n.** into the corner formed by the vertical **.a.** and the horizontal **.g.**, where I place a **.v.**

The forehead should extend with its shape over the line **.n.t.**, but I draw the protrusion of the nose over the line **.n.v.** And where the horizontal line **.e.** intersects the diagonal line **.n.t.**, there I mark the beginning of the hair above the bare forehead. Then, in the middle between the two vertical lines **.a.** and **.n.** and between the horizontals **.d.** and **.g.**, I draw a vertical line; this touches the front of the upper lip of the mouth and the front of the chin. Then I divide **.n.r.** between the horizontals **.m.** and **.d.** with two vertical lines into three equal fields; the foremost touches the front of the lower lip, while the hindmost touches the end of the hollow of the throat between the lower lip and the chin. 

I draw the eyebrows almost to the line **.o.** I also raise them in their middle a little above the horizontal **.f.** And between the two vertical lines **.q.** and **.r.** and between the horizontals **.f.** and **.g.**, I place the entire ear; however, I draw the lower earlobe in the front half-part between **.q.** and **.r.** and between the two horizontals **.f.** and **.g.** I place the back whorl <note>the crown or "vortex" of hair</note> of the hair on the head between the vertical lines **.s.** and **.b.** and horizontals **.c.** and **.e.** at the third part toward the vertical **.s.** and also a third part above the horizontal **.e.** On the horizontal **.g.** between the vertical lines **.s.** and **.b.**, at the nearest quarter toward **.s.**, I place the head upon the neck. Then I draw the contour lines of the side-view <note>profile</note> face within and along these intersecting lines, such that the top of the head touches the horizontal side **.c.** between the vertical lines **.p.** and **.q.** Likewise, the back vertical side **.b.** between the horizontals **.h.** and **.f.**, and from there down to the neck, and then following that backward all the way down through the square <term>vierung</term>.

Then I draw the head downward at the front to the forehead, and then the forehead, nose, mouth, and chin; the nose touches the front vertical side **.a.** between the horizontals **.f.** and **.g.** Thus the entire head is enclosed in this square, touching all four sides. But from the chin, I draw the jawbone backward and upward to behind the ear. Then I draw the eyes and brows, likewise the ears in their place. I also draw the bare part of the face where the hair begins, and proceed from the front whorl of the forehead over the horizontal line **.e.** and between the two vertical lines **.p.** and **.q.** back down to in front of the middle of the ear. Then I draw the front of the neck below the horizontal **.d.** downward behind the vertical line **.o.**, a quarter toward the vertical **.p.** If I wish, I make the neck one-14th part thick, or two-27th parts. 

Whoever wishes to use these things should pay close attention to the curved contour lines, how they are drawn in and out against the straight lines; for whoever does not pay attention will quickly encounter deformity <note>original: "ongeſtalt"</note>, yet the cross-lines in the square never allow the proportions to run entirely out of measure.

Now that I have finished the side-view head, I will further create the front-facing head in the other square; it will be of equal height with the first, but narrower.

<summary>This page provides a highly technical guide for constructing a human head in profile using a grid-based system of horizontal, vertical, and diagonal lines. It details the precise placement of features like the ear, jaw, and hairline within a geometric square to ensure proportional accuracy.</summary>
<keywords>proportion, Albrecht Dürer, profile view, facial construction, geometry in art, human anatomy, grid system, vierung</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s instructions on the geometric construction of the human head. Having established the profile view on the previous page, he now explains how to project those proportions to create the frontal (face-on) view and the back of the head, ensuring all three views are perfectly synchronized through a grid system.</meta>

First, I draw all straight horizontal parallel lines <term>zwerchlinien</term> from the first square of the profile view <note>the "side-facing view"</note> outward as far as I need them, in the same order as the letters stand from **.c.** to **.d.** These lines divide all heights and lows in the frontal view <note>the "forward-facing view"</note> exactly the same as in the profile. After that, I draw vertical lines through these horizontals; these will indicate all the widths of the parts in the frontal face.

First, I sketch two vertical lines **.a.b.** on both sides through the previously drawn horizontals, and I place them **.10.** parts apart. Then, with four vertical parallel lines **.c.d.e.f.**, I divide the two sides **.a.b.** into five equal fields. In the middle field **.d.e.**, I place the crown of the head in the middle between the horizontals **.c.e.** Then I place the nose in the middle, in such a way that its nostrils touch **.d.e.** between the horizontals **.f.g.** I also let the two corners of the mouth touch the verticals **.d.e.** on the horizontal **.m.**, and the chin remains below, also between the verticals **.d.e.** Then I place the two eyes so that their four corners touch the four vertical lines **.c.d.e.f.** between the horizontals **.f. j.**

After that, I divide the verticals **.a.c.** and **.f.b.** with two vertical lines **.g.** and **.h.** in the middle between them. These two lines **.g.h.** touch, on each side on the horizontal **.k.**, the height of the cheeks; and the ends of the eyebrows on both sides fall in the middle between the verticals **.g.c.** and **.f.h.** under the horizontal **.f.** Further, I divide **.a.g.** and **.h.b.** in the middle between them with two verticals **.i.k.** These two verticals separate the bareness of the forehead from the hair on both sides between the horizontals **.e.f.**, but the head exceeds these two lines on both sides so that half of the ears are hidden from view between the horizontals **.f.g.** I also draw the two ears on both sides between **.a.i.** and **.k.b.** between the horizontals **.f.g.** But at the top, near horizontal **.f.**, they exceed the two vertical sides **.a.b.** and approach at the bottom with their lobes <note>original: "zipffellen"</note> toward the two verticals **.i.k.**

Now that the verticals have been drawn through the horizontal lines—which indicate all widths, heights, and lows in the frontal face—I then draw the contour lines of the face into this division. First, the head from the top, where the horizontal **.c.** touches in the middle between the two verticals **.d.e.** From there, I draw a curve on both sides to the verticals **.a.b.** at the horizontal **.h.**; from there, down to the ears at horizontal **.f.** in the middle between **.a.i.** and **.k.b.** Next, I draw the bareness of the forehead and the jawbones through the two verticals **.g.h.** on the horizontal **.m.** all the way down to the chin. From the aforementioned ends, I draw the neck from both sides down to the horizontal line **.d.**; there, I make it **.15.** parts wide, or a little wider. Then I draw in the eyes, nose, mouth, and chin, along with the principal features that the face requires.

Once that is finished, I create the third square <note>the third "vierung" or quadrant</note> for the back of the head, according to the outer lines of the frontal view, and I place the back whorl <note>the crown or vertex</note> in its place, and draw the curvature of the head onto the neck.

If I wish to lay out this above-described head in a ground-plan <note>a geometric projection</note>, I do so by means of a triangle which I will show hereafter, so that I may transform one given thing into another, yet bring each one to a similar form. I designate this triangle with **1.2.3.** It should always have a right angle so that it may be used on all sides and however one needs it; it may have equal or unequal sides, long or short. Within it, I take whichever side I wish and mark it with points. But here I set approximately **.4.5.6.** etc. From these, I draw parallel lines against one side of the triangle to the third side; thus they divide that side just as the first is divided. If I then go back from the newly formed points with parallel lines, as before toward the undivided side, it will also be divided identically to the first and the second.

<summary>Dürer explains how to project the measurements from a profile view to create a frontal face and a rear view using a coordinate grid. He also introduces a method of geometric transformation using triangles to ensure proportional consistency across different perspectives.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, facial proportions, frontal view, geometry in art, grid system, anatomical drawing, triangulation, perspective projection</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s geometric instructions, moving from the basic profile and frontal views to the method of "transferring" these dimensions to create a plan view (a top-down or foreshortened perspective). He introduces a specific geometric construction he calls the "transfer" to achieve this.</meta>

<sig>E ij</sig>

From this figure, many different things can be made, and for my purpose here, this may well be called a **transfer** <term>vbertrag: a method of projecting dimensions from one view to another</term>; for by this way, one can carry and pull each part into another. But before I press the head down into the ground <note>This refers to creating a "plan view" or a top-down projection that appears flattened or foreshortened</note>, I want to draw this **transfer** here below.

->**The transfer.**<-
<note>original: "Der vbertrag."</note>

And so I use the **transfer** for this. I set the side-facing <note>profile</note> and forward-facing <note>frontal</note> head before me, just as they were described in their squares <term>vierungen</term>, and first I draw all the vertical lines **.a.n.o.p.q.r.s.** straight downward from the square of the side-facing head, as far as I need them. After that, I also draw all the vertical parallel lines **.a.i.g.c.d.e.f.h.k.b.** straight downward from the other square of the forward-facing countenance, as mentioned before. Then I draw a straight parallel line crosswise <term>zwerchs</term> beneath the two squares of the head, through all the vertical lines that were pulled down from above. Where this cross-line intersects the two vertical sides **.a.b.** of the forward-facing countenance, I place **.1.** at line **.a.** and **.2.** at line **.b.** After that, I measure downward from the number **.1.** on the line **.a.** the width of the square of the forward-facing countenance; there I place **.3.** and connect **.2.** and **.3.** together with a diagonal line <term>ortlini</term>. Thus, this triangle is ready for the **pressed-down ground** <note>the plan view</note>, which I call the **transfer**. After that, where this diagonal line **.2.3.** intersects the vertical lines that were pulled down from the forward-facing head, I travel straight across from those same points with parallel lines through all the vertical lines that were pulled down from the side-facing head. Where the cross-line **.2.1.** and the cross-line **.3.** then intersect the two vertical sides **.a.b.** of the side-facing countenance, I place **.4.5.** at the front and **.6.7.** at the back of these corners of the square; this creates an oblong square <note>a rectangle</note> in which the head is contained in the **pressed-down ground**.

<summary>Dürer explains the "transfer" (vbertrag), a geometric projection method using a diagonal line to translate proportions from frontal and profile views into a flattened plan view. This allows the artist to maintain mathematical consistency when drawing the head from different angles or in foreshortening.</summary>
<keywords>transfer, projection, plan view, geometry, facial proportions, vbertrag, vierung, perspective</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s explanation of the geometric "transfer" method. Having established the grid on the previous page, he now demonstrates how these intersecting lines allow an artist to plot facial features accurately across different perspectives, including a plan view.</meta>

Now that the square **.4. 5. 6. 7.** has been fully drawn through with the upright lines from both the side and frontal views by means of the **transfer** <term>vbertrag: a method of projecting dimensions from one view to another</term>, the resulting small squares indicate exactly where each feature of the face and its defining lines should be placed. Within each small square, the placement of features such as the eyes, nose, mouth, chin, ears, neck, and all structural lines is contained, just as I have subsequently drawn them with lines. I have drafted everything precisely: first the head in profile, then from the front, then from behind, and finally projected onto the **flattened ground** <note>nidergetruckten grund: a plan view or top-down projection that appears squashed or foreshortened</note>.

It is useful to know that if this head were made of wax and you were to slice through it at any horizontal line, you could determine the exact shape of that **plane** <note>original Latin: "planus"</note> or cross-section using this method. 

If I wish to make the head one-seventh of the body's total height (whereas in the previous figures it was one-eighth, as I have noted in the illustrations), I keep all the other facial features as they were. However, I increase the height and thickness of the head at the side, from the forehead upward and then downward toward the back of the neck, between the horizontal lines **.g.** and **.m.**; additionally, I make the neck two 1/27th parts thick. After that, I broaden the frontal view of the head according to the rule, so that it becomes approximately two 1/17th parts wider across the forehead. I pull these lines back toward the two vertical sides **.a.** and **.b.** above the ears. I also heighten and widen the **temples** <note>original: "pfossen der stirn," literally the 'posts' of the forehead</note> and position the tops of the ears further from the head, as will be shown in the following sketches. It should be noted that a figure divided into seven of its head-lengths is quite well-proportioned <note>literally "not to be despised"</note>.

In the same way that I have placed the head into a **flattened ground plan**, one can also project an entire figure—whether it stands straight or bent—into a flattened ground plan.

<summary>Dürer explains how his grid system (the 'transfer') allows artists to precisely locate facial features and even calculate horizontal cross-sections of the head. He also provides instructions for adjusting these measurements if the artist chooses to use a seven-head-height proportion instead of eight.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, Albrecht Dürer, geometric projection, plan view, facial features, Renaissance drawing techniques</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page transitions from Dürer’s general geometric "transfer" method to its specific application in anatomical proportions, focusing here on the precise measurements of a "strong man's" hand, following the 1/10th scale of total body height.</meta>

E iij

Furthermore, I wish to describe in more detail the outstretched hand of a strong man, as was indicated earlier; and just as I previously made it one-tenth part <note>Dürer typically uses a "tenth-part" of the total body height as the standard measure for the length of a hand</note> of the body's length, I shall let it remain so here. I set this length of one-tenth before me as an upright line, marking the top with an **.a.** and the bottom with a **.b.**, and I enclose it between two parallel **horizontal lines** <term>zwerchlinien: horizontal or transverse lines</term> **.c.** and **.d.** After that, I divide the line **.a.b.** with 18 points into nineteen equal fields. Then I draw another upright line parallel to the first **.a.b.** between the horizontals **.c.** and **.d.**, placed far enough away so that the hand I intend to draw will not be obstructed. This line shall be **.e.** at the top and **.f.** at the bottom; I place this line in the center of the width of the outstretched hand so that it passes through the long finger <note>the middle finger</note>. I draw the hand upright so that the longest finger touches the horizontal line **.c.** at the top, while at the bottom I let the line **.d.** pass through the middle of the wrist joint. Next, from point **.1.**, I draw a horizontal line **.g.** which touches the tip of the **ring finger** <note>original: "golt fingers," literally "gold finger," the finger where a gold ring is worn</note>. Then I place a point on the line **.a.b.** halfway between **.1.** and **.2.**, from which I draw a line **.h.** that touches the tip of the index finger. Then, from point **.4.**, I draw a horizontal line **.j.** that touches the end of the little finger. Finally, from point **.7.**, I draw a horizontal line **.k.** that touches the end of the thumb.

Once I have indicated the ends of the fingers, I mark their backmost **joints** <term>glyd: a segment or joint of a limb</term> where they meet the hand. From point **.11.**, I draw a horizontal line **.l.**; this passes through the two backmost joints of the index and long fingers, as they stand at the same height next to each other at the front of the hand. Thus, the long finger becomes longer than the entire remaining part of the hand. Where the line **.l.** intersects the upright line **.e.f.**, I place a point **.m.** After that, I set one foot of a compass in point **.f.** and the other in point **.m.**; from there, I swing an arc backward out of the hand as far as I need. On this curved line, I place the two backmost joints of the ring finger and the little finger, for they sit deeper in the hand than the two frontmost fingers. Then I draw a horizontal line **.n.** from point **.14.**, which passes through the middle of the thumb’s backmost joint. Now that the length of the hand and all the fingers are indicated, I will further show the finger joints. From point **.10.**, I draw a horizontal line **.o.** that passes through the middle of the thumb's second joint. Next, I divide the index finger lengthwise between **.h.** and **.l.** with a horizontal line in the middle; this passes through the center of its second joint. Then I divide the front half-section of its length with 6 points into 7 equal fields, and from point **.3.**, I draw a horizontal line that passes through the middle of the frontmost joint of the aforementioned finger. Then I draw a horizontal line **.p.** from point **.6.**, which passes through the second joint of the long finger. Then I divide the space between the horizontals **.c.** and **.p.** with 6 points into 7 equal fields, and from that same point **.3.**, I draw a horizontal line passing through the middle of the frontmost joint of the long finger. The horizontal line **.k.** passes through the middle of the second joint of the ring finger. Then I divide that front length with 8 points into nine equal fields, and from that same point **.4.**, I draw a horizontal line passing through the middle of the frontmost joint of the aforementioned finger.

I make the little finger as long as the two backmost segments of the long finger, or let it remain as it was previously established. I divide this length with ten points into eleven equal fields and draw a horizontal line from point **.6.**; this passes through the middle of the second joint of the little finger. Then I newly divide the 6 upper parts with ten points into eleven equal fields and draw a horizontal line from the newly created point **.5.**, which passes through the middle of the frontmost joint of the aforementioned finger. Everyone should pay close attention to the numbers used, whether they are taken from the main line **.a.b.** or from the other smaller subdivisions.

The nails on the fingers I make half as long as the segment of the joint upon which they sit. Next to the long finger on both sides, the split between the fingers goes down halfway into the length of the hand; however, between the ring finger and the little finger, the split goes deeper.

<summary>This page provides technical instructions for drafting the proportions of a human hand using a geometric grid, specifying the exact placement of finger joints and tips based on a fractional scale.</summary>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s detailed geometric breakdown of the human hand, moving from the vertical lengths established on the previous page to the specific widths and thicknesses required to construct a three-dimensional form.</meta>

...into the hand, but the gap between the thumb goes even deeper into the hand.

Now that I have described the length of the parts of the hand, I will indicate the thickness and width of those same parts. And although I previously described their thickness from the side in the illustrations <note>referring to earlier diagrams in the treatise</note>, it does not fit well here; therefore, I will first describe the width of the outstretched hand, and only then its thickness from the side.

I make the flat hand at the wrist, on the **horizontal line** <term>zwerchlini: a transverse or horizontal line</term> **.d.**, seven-nineteenths wide relative to the vertical length **.a.b.** But at the horizontal **.n.**, it is eleven-nineteenths wide, for the hand is widest at the knuckle of the thumb. The other width, measured from the outside of the index finger along the horizontal **.l.** through the back-most joints of the fingers to the end of the arc <note>Dürer is describing the curve formed by the knuckles</note> at the outer edge of the little finger, I make as wide as the index finger is long.

I make the longest finger <note>the middle finger</note> at its base one-fifth as wide as its own length; at its tip, I make it one-fourth narrower than at the base. I also make the index finger at the base just as wide as the long finger, but at the tip, I make it a little narrower than the long finger. For the ring finger <note>original: "gold finger"</note> and the little finger, I make each one-fifth of its own length wide, and make each one-fourth narrower at the tip than it is at the base. I make the thumb, at its middle joint, one-third as wide as its own length.

The fingers are positioned at the back of the hand, each centered within its own width; there, I will draw small circles to mark the points.

It is to be noted that in the hand, no finger has a form like any other. Let everyone observe this in people, and he will find it to be so. I also observe that when the hand is outstretched, it does not stand entirely straight, but rather shifts outward toward the little finger. Next, I set the thickness of the hand from the side: at the wrist joint, it is five-nineteenths of the vertical length **.a.b.** But through the knuckle of the thumb, on the horizontal line **.n.**, it is half as thick as the flat hand is wide; likewise, it is just as thick below at the ball of the hand. However, the hand makes itself hollow, as the two sides of the thumb and the little finger curve inward; therefore, the hand is not as thick as it appears, or as the outer lines would suggest.

The thumb, at its foremost joint, is exactly as thick as it is long—that is the same as the foremost part of the little finger—but at the very tip, it is one-fourth thinner; yet toward the knuckle, it is quite thick. The fingers are approximately as thick as they are wide, according to their proportions, except that in the joints, they may lack a little <note>meaning the joints may be slightly narrower than the fleshier parts of the finger segments</note>.

Now that I have recorded all the lengths, widths, and thicknesses of the hand, I then draw the shape of the hand with lines, just as I have drafted it below. Whoever replicates this, let him take care that he is not misled, for these lines are difficult to draw correctly.

<summary>Dürer provides specific fractional measurements (using a 1/19th scale) for the width and thickness of the hand and fingers, emphasizing that the hand is asymmetrical and hollowed in the palm.</summary>
<keywords>hand proportions, anatomy, Albrecht Dürer, geometry, finger width, palm thickness, thumb knuckle</keywords>
