<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s instructional treatise on human proportions. Having previously discussed the hand, he now transitions to a precise geometric construction of the foot of a "strong man" based on his system of an eight-head-tall figure.</meta>

E iiij

Furthermore, I wish to describe the foot of a strong man more clearly than I have before. As I mentioned previously regarding the man who is made of eight lengths of his own head, I describe his foot as being one-sixth of his total height in length and one-seventeenth in width.

From these lengths and widths, I draw an **oblong rectangle** <term>oberlengte vierung: literally an "over-lengthened squaring," referring to a rectangle longer than it is wide</term> and place it horizontally before me on a flat surface. Into this, I draw the foot so that it touches both the front and back sides, as will follow. When I draw the foot with its outline, I designate the far long side with an **.a.** and the side closer to me with a **.b.** The two short sides I designate as follows: the front, where I will draw the toes, with a **.c.**, and the back with a **.d.**

Inside this oblong rectangle, I make the foot so that it lies completely flat upon a pressed-down ground. Next, I divide this rectangle along its length between **.c.** and **.d.** with two lines, **.e.** and **.f.**, into three equal fields. The line **.e.** stands at the front. To avoid error in the divisions, take note of the letters I name, even though they may seem scattered, they should always be placed as mentioned above. In the front field, between **.c.** and **.e.**, I will place the toes. In the middle field, between **.e.** and **.f.**, I will make the mid-foot. In the back third field, I will place the heel and the leg <note>the ankle and lower shin area</note> above the foot.

Afterward, I divide the line **.d.** with six points—**.g. h. i. k. l. m.**—into seven equal fields. From point **.g.**, I draw a **parallel line** <term>parallell lini: a line that maintains an equal distance from another at all points</term> to the frontmost line **.c.** Where it touches that line, I place an **.n.**; where it intersects the vertical line **.f.**, I place an **.o.** Then, I divide the space between **.l.** and **.m.** with a point **.p.** in the center. From this point **.p.**, I draw a horizontal parallel line to the line **.f.**, and at that point, I place a **.q.** Then I draw a **slanted line** <term>schleme lini: an oblique or diagonal line</term> between **.k.** and **.q.**

Next, I place a point **.r.** on line **.c.** midway between **.a.** and **.b.** Then I divide **.r. b.** with two points, **.s.** and **.t.**, into three equal fields. I also divide **.b. e.** with two points, **.v.** and **.r.** <note>Dürer often reuses letters or refers to previous points in his coordinate system</note>, also into three equal fields. Then I again divide **.e. f.** with two lines, **.y.** and **.z.**, into three equal fields. I then divide **.f. d.** with two points, **.st.** and **.ß.**, into three equal fields. After this, where line **.g.** intersects line **.e.**, I place a **.k.** Then I draw a horizontal line from point **.h.** to line **.f.**; where it touches, I place **.a. i.** 

Next, I divide **.r. s.** with two points, **1.** and **2.**, into three equal fields. I also divide **.r. e.** with two points, **1.** and **2.**, into three equal fields. Then I divide **.ß. d.** with four points, **1. 2. 3. 4.**, into five equal fields. From point **.z.**, I draw a line to the horizontal **.h. a. i.**, and there I place **.b. i.** It is also to be noted that I have placed the letters of the short lines all upon the horizontal **.a.** From there, I draw several slanted lines: one from **.e.** to **.n.**, the other from **.r.** to **.k.** Then another from point **.2.** (between **.r.** and **.s.**) to point **.y.** Next, one from **.t.** to **.z.**, then **.b.** to **.o.**, and then **.v.** to **.a. i.**

Afterward, I draw a straight line from point **.st.** to the slanted line **.k. q.**, and there I place **.e. i.** Then I set one foot of the **compass** <term>zirckel: a drawing tool used for circles and measuring distances</term> in point **.h.** and scribe with the other foot from point **.n.** out of the foot to the slanted line **.r. k.** Following this, I keep one foot of the compass in the same place and, without moving it, I scribe a circular arc between the slanted lines **.t. z.** and **.b. o.** Then, keeping one foot of the compass still in point **.h.**, I pull it tighter and set the other foot in point **.2.** on the line **.b.** between **.r.** and **.e.** From there, I scribe forward to the slanted line **.b. o.** Keeping the compass foot in point **.h.**, I set the other foot in point **.r.** and scribe from there to the slanted line **.t. z.**

Now that I have indicated with slanted and circular lines where I will place the toes and where they are to be cut off at the front, I will furthermore place their backmost joints. I place a point on line **.e.** midway between the slanted lines **.b. o.** and **.v. a. i.** In these points, I will set the backmost joint of one of the smallest toes. I then set one foot of the compass in point **.z.** and the other in the point just made, and from there, I scribe a curve to the horizontal line **.g. n.** where...

Here I place the hindmost joint of the big toe; afterward, I place two points on this circular mark in the middle between the **oblique lines** <term>schlemen: Dürer’s term for diagonal or slanting construction lines</term> **.v. .y.** and **.t. .z.** and **.b. .o.** There I place the two hindmost joints of the middle toes. I wish to designate these in the drawing according to their sizes with oblong small rings. The hindmost joint of the little toe I place with a point on line **.e.** midway between the **transverse line** <term>zwerchen: a horizontal or cross-line</term> **.b.** and the oblique lines **.v.** and **.a i.** The big toe has two joints, and I make the rear part a little longer than the front part.

The three middle toes I divide, each through its three joints, so that the hindmost part is as long as its front two parts combined. However, each foremost part of the three middle toes is one-fourth longer than the part behind it—not because of the internal bone, but because of the flesh that they have in front of them. But for the smallest toe, I make a strong "rear-ball" at its hindmost joint and let it end at the front on line **.r.** I make its hindmost part longer than the front ones. I make each nail on all toes almost as long as the segment upon which it stands. Afterward, I place one foot of a compass on point **.y.** and the other foot a little above the point of the hindmost joint of the little toe on line **.e.** From there, I draw through the oblique lines, and where the curves intersect the oblique lines, I draw short lines from those same points as a mark. This is how far I "split" the toes into the flesh, although on the foot I draw the furrows and hollows much further back. Line **.f.** touches the front of the instep, from where the bone pulls upward; but beneath it, the cut <gloss>base</gloss> <note>original: "fūnt," likely referring to the foundation or bottom of the foot's structure</note> steps two-thirds past line **.f.** toward **.z.**

Furthermore, I divide **.f. .d.** with four points into three equal fields, and from point **.z.** I draw a straight line that touches the **tendons** <term>spann adern: literally "tension-veins," referring here to the tendons above the heel, such as the Achilles tendon</term> behind the heel.

Once I have drawn all the straight, oblique, and curved lines through which the foot is entirely **modeled** <term>gepossiert: from the trade of sculpting or "bossing," meaning to give three-dimensional form or structure</term>, I then draw the outline lines into it, showing how it should lie on the pressed-down ground, and I begin at the front.

First, I draw the big toe with its ball coming forward out of point **.e.**, outside of the oblique line **.e. .n.** until almost the middle of the rear part of the toe; then I proceed in a curve under the oblique line **.e. .n.** and draw the outline line in front of the foremost joint back to the oblique line **.e. .n.** From there, I draw the toe fully forward to the circular mark midway between **.n. .r.** From there, I draw it backward as far as it is split, up to the line **.r. .k.** After that, I draw the three middle toes between their oblique lines so that they touch the circular marks at the front. Regarding this, it should be noted that the big toe should not stand completely straight out, as is found among many **coarse people** <note>Dürer suggests that a perfectly straight big toe is a sign of an unrefined or "common" physical form, preferring a slight natural curve for a more "noble" or artistic proportion</note>. Therefore, I have drawn the oblique lines so that the toes are placed one more than the other outward toward the little toe, for the sake of strength and beauty. For this reason, I leave a space between the largest and the next toe, for the middle three toes should stand further outward than the largest; but the smallest toe I draw inward toward the other toes of the oblique line **.v. .a i.** I end it on the outside at line **.e.**, two-thirds from its hindmost joint between line **.b.** I also draw the foremost toe the largest, but those following always one smaller than the other, and I indicate therein how the knuckles and other parts should have their shape, each one different from the other. Afterward, I draw the outside of the foot from the ball of the little toe into the angle **.b. .y.**

Then I draw the inner line of the foot starting from point **.e.**, specifically drawn to line **.y.** midway between the long side **.a.** and the line **.n. .g.**, and I draw further backward, curving inward until line **.f.** midway between **.f. .o.** From there, [I go] entirely backward and round the heel to line **.d.** at point **.h.** From there, I draw the heel...

...how it has a foreign curved line up to the **diagonal lines** <term>schlemen: Dürer’s term for slanted or oblique construction lines</term> **.k. .q.** However, along line **.f. .t.**, I draw a curve into the foot; but from point **.q.**, I draw completely to the ball behind the little toe at points **.y. .b.** I mark the soles inside with special small dots on line **.f. .t.** Through the cut in the instep across both ankles of the shinbone, I set the width at a 26th part <note>Dürer uses a fractional system where "a 26th part" refers to a fraction of the total body height</note>; but below the ankles, a 31st part; and I draw the section forward from line **.f.** two-thirds toward line **.3.** I also draw the inner ankle flatter and the outer one more pointed; likewise, the inner one further forward and the outer one further back, so that the hollow beside the **tendons** <note>original: "spanadern" - likely referring to the extensor tendons on the top of the foot</note> behind the inner ankle becomes longer and flatter than the outer one. Afterward, I draw the ball behind the little toe, through line **.y.** up to the halfway point toward line **.3.** In such drawing of the contour lines, one must take great care to maintain a beautiful form, so that nothing is spoiled. Afterward, I draw the two cross-sections through the foot **.e. .f.** separately, showing how they have a form in the plane, so that all this may be more clearly understood; for as I indicate these two sections, one can seek the sections in all bodies, whether they be straight or **slanted** <note>original: "schlems" - referring again to oblique or diagonal cuts</note>.

Next, I will **draw the foot in elevation** <note>original: "aus dem grund auffzihen" - an architectural drafting term for projecting a vertical view from a ground-plan</note> from the ground-plan, one 24th part above the flattened view, and I trace through the transfer from the flattened ground-plan the lines **.e. .e. .y. .3. .f. .d.** as they stood before, and whatever else is necessary, all upward. Thus, the first elevated lines indicate where all parts of the foot belong according to the length, such as the foremost sections of the toes, then their joints and divisions, also where the nails should stand, where the foot with all its character and balls is drawn, as well as the bone at the heel in its section. But through the transverse lines, I indicate the heights of the foot and its parts; I place the soles flat upon line **.b.** Furthermore, I divide the vertical line **.c.** between the transverse lines **.f. .b.** with two points **.1. .2.** into three equal fields; but I divide the vertical line **.e.** between the transverse lines **.e. .f.** with three points **.1. .2. .3.** into four equal fields; then draw a transverse line midway between the transverse lines **.a. .e.** between the verticals **.3. .d.** Then I draw the contour lines of the foot into these markers. I begin in the corner **.a. .f.** and draw the instep down to the vertical line **.e.** at point **.3.** From there, I draw the ball behind the big toe down to the transverse line **.f.** Afterward, I continue below that forward to point **.1.**, where the big toe ends at the front and top; but the smallest toe touches at the top with its hindmost joint and ball the fourth part between **.f. .b.** Then I draw the three middle toes in order; I make the longest the highest, and each subsequent one lower than the other, and I make the smallest toe quite low at the front. <meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical instructions on the geometric construction of the human foot. Having established the base points and the joints of the toes on the previous page, Dürer now describes how to draw the outer contours (the "skin") over the geometric framework and how to project a side elevation (an "up-drawing") from the ground plan.</meta>

...how it has a peculiar curved line up to the **oblique lines** <term>schlemen: Dürer’s term for diagonal or slanted construction lines</term> **.k. .q.** However, I draw a curve into the foot near line **.s. .t.**, but from point **.q.** I draw completely to the ball behind the little toe at point **.y. .b.** I mark the soles within with specific small points on line **.s. .t.** Through the cut in the **instep** <note>original: "rist"</note> across both **ankles** <term>knorren: specifically the malleoli, the bony protrusions on either side of the ankle</term> of the shinbone, I set the width at one 26th part; but below the ankles, one 31st part. I draw the section forward from line **.f.** two-thirds toward line **.z.** I also draw the inner ankle flatter and the outer one more pointed; likewise, the inner one further forward and the outer one further back. Thus, the hollow next to the **tendons** <note>original: "spanadern," referring to the visible sinews or the Achilles tendon area</note> behind the inner ankle becomes longer and flatter than the outer one. Afterward, I draw a particular ball on the outer side closest to the bone, forward from line **.f.** to line **.z.**; this gives the foot a **graceful appearance** <note>original: "wolstand," meaning a balanced, aesthetically pleasing, and anatomically correct posture</note>. Then I draw the ball behind the little toe through line **.y.** up to the halfway point toward line **.z.** In such drawing of the contour lines, one must take great care to guide the form well, so that nothing is spoiled. Afterward, I draw the two **sections** <note>cross-sections or "cuts" showing the internal thickness</note> through the foot **.e. .f.** separately, as they have a shape in the plane, so that all this may be understood more clearly; for just as I indicate these two sections, one may seek out the sections in all bodies, whether they be straight or oblique.

Next, I will **elevate** <note>original: "auffziehen," meaning to create a vertical profile or side view based on the horizontal ground plan</note> the foot from the ground plan, 1/24th part above the flattened plan, and I project from the flattened ground plan the lines **.c. .e. .y. .z. .f. .d.** as they stood before, and whatever else is necessary, all upwards. Through these elevated lines, I draw two **cross-lines** <note>original: "zwerch linien," horizontal lines</note> at a height of one 24th part; this also becomes an **elongated rectangle** <note>original: "vberlengte vierung," literally an over-long square</note> in which I place the elevated foot. Then I divide the horizontal side **.a. .b.** with two other horizontal lines **.e. .f.** into three equal fields. Thus, the first elevated lines indicate where all parts of the foot belong according to their length, such as the foremost sections of the toes; then their joints and divisions, also where the nails should stand, where the foot with all its character and balls is located, and likewise how the bone above the heel is elevated in its section. But through the horizontal lines, I indicate the heights of the foot and its parts, placing the soles flat upon line **.b.** Furthermore, I divide the vertical line **.c.** between the horizontals **.f. .b.** with two points **.1. .2.** into three equal fields; but I divide the vertical line **.e.** between the horizontals **.e. .f.** with three points **.1. .2. .3.** into four equal fields; then draw a horizontal line midway between the horizontals **.a. .e.** between the verticals **.z. .d.** Then I draw the contour lines of the foot into these markings; I begin this in the corner **.a. .f.** and draw the instep down to the vertical line **.e.** at point **.3.** From there, I draw the ball behind the big toe down to the horizontal **.f.** Then I continue beneath it forward to point **.1.**; there the big toe ends at the front and top; but the smallest toe touches at the top with its hindmost joint and ball the fourth part between **.f. .b.** Afterward, I draw the three middle toes in order, making the longest one the highest, and each one further forward lower than the other, and I make the smallest toe quite low at the front. Afterward, I draw its ball behind the vertical **.y.** But I draw the side-ball of the foot so that it touches the horizontal **.e.** at the top, and ends at the bottom in the upper third between the horizontals **.f. .b.** and touches the corner of the horizontal **.f.** and vertical **.z.** and ends behind the vertical **.s. .t.** between the horizontals **.e. .f.** Afterward, I draw the heel so that it touches the vertical **.d.** on the horizontal **.f.**, and draw it at the top into the corner enclosed by the horizontal **.e.** and the vertical **.z.**, which I have drawn from its point on the horizontal **.a.** between **.f. .d.** From there, I draw the bone upwards at the back; then I draw the outer ankle of the shinbone from point **.3.** backwards and down to the middle horizontal line between the horizontals **.a. .e.** And above the instep, over the ankles of the shinbone, I make the bone one 24th part thick at the side, and draw the shin in front...

<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical instructions on the geometric construction of the human foot, moving from the lateral profile to the posterior (back) and anterior (front) views using a fractional proportion system based on total body height.</meta>

...the leg from the points of the **vertical line** <term>auffrechten: Dürer's term for a vertical construction line</term> **.f.** upwards and backwards; and the curve of the **heel** <note>original: "ferschen"</note> I draw downwards and forwards from the **horizontal line** <term>zwerchen: Dürer's term for a horizontal construction line</term> **.e.**

Next, I will also briefly show the foot from behind. For this, I create a **square grid** <term>vierung: literally a "four-ing," a square or rectangular frame used for determining proportions</term> as high and wide as the foot is, and project the points and lines into it from the previous transfer. These divide and indicate all parts of the foot, such as how the **inner ankle bone** <note>the medial malleolus</note> should stand higher and the **outer ankle bone** <note>the lateral malleolus</note> lower. And the width across the **instep** <note>original: "rist"</note> through the ankle bones I make one 26th part <note>Dürer measures specific body parts as fractions of the total height of the figure</note>, and on the centerline between the horizontals **.a.** and **.e.**, one 31st part. However, I make the leg above both ankles one 34th part wide.

The foot is also to be made from the front in the same way the back view was made. I have drafted these ideas very precisely below, and shown more through the **drawing** <term>reissen: a technical term for drafting, sketching, or engraving</term> than in writing. From this, it will be very easily understood how this third view is to be constructed from the first two established views.

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the construction of the human foot to the detailed geometric proportions of a woman's head. Dürer applies the same fractional grid system used for the male head to the female subject, maintaining consistency in his mathematical approach to anatomy.</meta>

Now that the foot is finished, I will further describe a woman's head more precisely than before, using the same method by which the previous male head was described. And just as I previously took one 8th part <note>Dürer defines proportions as fractions of the total body height</note> of the figure’s length for the height of the woman's head in the other type of figures, I will also here set the height as one 8th part—making it as large in scale as the man's head.

First, I place three **rectangles** <term>vierung: a four-sided grid or frame used for proportional drafting</term> side-by-side. Through the **transfer** <note>the process of projecting measurements from one view to another</note>, I make each one an 8th part high. The first I make one 8th part wide; in this, I shall draft the head from the **side** <note>a profile view</note>. But the other two rectangles—one for the **front-facing** <note>original: "fürsichtigen"</note> and the other for the **back-facing** <note>original: "hinderwertigen"</note> head—I make each one 10th part wide.

At the beginning, I mark the two vertical sides of the rectangle in which I draft the head from the side: the front side, where I will place the nose, with an **.a.**, and the back with a **.b.** Then, with other vertical lines between **.a.** and **.b.**, I divide all the depths found in the face, marking each with a specific letter; the numbers I use to describe them are taken from the total length of the figure.

Now, I place the first line **.c.** one 80th part behind **.a.** This line **.c.** touches the **crown** <note>original: "wirbel"</note> above the forehead where the hair begins, and the **eyebrows** <note>original: "augprawn"</note> end at the front of it, for they should not touch each other above the nose. It also touches the **upper lip** <note>original: "obern leffzen"</note> at the front.

Next, I place another line **.d.** one 64th part backwards—this is always understood as being from the front line **.a.** This line touches the front of the upper eyelids, the **lower lip** <note>original: "vndern leffken"</note> of the mouth, and the front of the chin.

Then I draw a line **.e.** one 56th part backwards; this touches the hollows above the eyelids, the front of the eyeball, the back of the **nostril** <note>original: "naspellein"</note>, the corner of the mouth, and the **hollow** <note>original: "holkelen"</note> under the lower lip.

Next, I place a line **.f.** backwards one 40th part; this touches the back corners of the eyes.

Furthermore, I place a line **.g.** backwards one 32nd part; this touches the back ends of the eyebrows and, at the bottom, the **fleshy parts** <note>original: "wünen," likely referring to the soft tissue or jowls</note> under and behind the chin.

After that, I place a line **.h.** backwards one 22nd part; this touches the front of the neck.

Further, I place a line **.i.** one 14th part backwards; this touches the inner part of the **ear** <note>original: "or"</note>.

Then I draw a line **.k.** backwards one 11th part; at this line, the back of the <unclear>ear</unclear> ends, but the lower lobe remains halfway toward line **.i.**

Next, I place a line **.l.** one 9th part backwards; this touches the back **whorl** <note>original: "wirbel"</note> of the hair on the head and the **nape** <note>original: "genick"</note> at the bottom of the head; from there, I draw the neck downwards and backwards.

Finally, I draw the last vertical parallel line **.m.** two 17th parts <note>2/17ths of the figure's total height</note> backwards; this touches the middle of the back of the neck.

When I have drawn all the vertical lines and indicated what they should touch, I then divide the heights and depths of the parts of the face with parallel **horizontal lines** <term>zwerch linien: cross-lines or horizontals</term>.

First, let the topmost horizontal line of the aforementioned rectangle be an **.n.**; this touches the highest part of the head, and let the bottom-most be an **.r.y.**

Then I draw a horizontal line **.o.** under line **.n.** by one 80th part; this touches the back crown of the head.

Next, I draw a line **.p.** down from **.n.** by one 30th part; this touches the front **hairline** <note>original: "harwachs"</note> above the forehead.

Then I draw a line **.q.** under **.n.** by one 16th part; this touches the top of the eyelid and the top of the ears; the eyebrows end on this line at the back and front, but in the middle, I draw them above it.

Then I draw a line **.t.** midway between **.q.** and **.y.**; this touches the bottom of the nose, and there the nape of the neck meets the neck.

After that, I draw a line **.r.** and mark off the top third between **.q.** and **.t.**; this touches the bottom of the eyelid. However, I place the two corners of the eyes in the middle, as well as the eyeball, between **.q.** and **.r.**

Then I mark off the bottom fourth between **.q.** and **.t.** with a line **.s.**; this touches the top of the back of the nostril and the bottom of the ears.

Next, I divide **.t.** and **.y.** from each other in the middle with a line **.r.** <note>Dürer reuses 'r' here as a new marker</note>; this touches the top of the chin.

Then I divide **.t.** and **.r.** in the middle with a line **.v.**; this goes through the middle of the mouth.

However, I divide **.t.** and **.v.** into three equal fields; in the bottom one, I place the **upper lip** <note>original: "obern leffken"</note>, and in the top two, I place the **philtrum** <note>original: "holkeln," the vertical groove above the lip</note> at the front under the nose above the mouth.

Between **.v.** and **.r.**, I make two parts; in the upper one, I place the **lower lip** <note>original: "vordern leffken"</note>, and in the lower part, I place the hollow between the lower lip and the chin.

<summary>Albrecht Dürer details the geometric construction of a female head using a grid of vertical and horizontal lines based on fractional body proportions. He provides precise coordinates for facial features including the eyes, nose, lips, and ears within a profile view.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, facial anatomy, geometric drafting, female head, Early New High German, vierung, fractional measurements</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for drafting a woman’s head using Dürer’s geometric grid system. It moves from the initial division of the side-profile rectangle to the construction of the frontal and rear views, emphasizing the "gentler" proportions of the female form compared to the male.</meta>

Then I draw a slender line <note>original: "schleme lini," referring to a soft, guide line or oblique stroke</note> out from the point where the vertical **.c.** and the horizontal **.p.** intersect into the corner **.a. y.** Above this line, I draw the forehead between **.p.** and **.q.**; but I draw the nose below it between **.q.** and **.r.**

Next, I divide the second **rectangle** <term>vierung: a square or rectangular grid used for proportional drafting</term> of the front-facing head. First, I use horizontal lines by transferring measurements from the first rectangle; then, I also divide it with vertical lines. The two vertical sides of this rectangle I label **.a.** and **.b.** Then I divide the space between **.a.** and **.b.** with nine lines—**.d. e. f. g. h. i. k. l. m.**—into ten equal fields. Further, I divide the sections **.a. d.** and **.m. b.** with two lines, **.c.** and **.n.**, each in the middle of their respective fields. Once the grids in both rectangles are drawn across each other, encompassing the parts of the face, I then draw the contour lines within them. I use smaller and gentler strokes <note>original: "geprechen," here meaning the character of the lines or features</note> and a finer style than for the man’s face. First, I draw the head from the side, so that the top of the head touches the horizontal **.n.** at the vertical **.j.** Likewise, the back touches the vertical **.b.** between the horizontals **.p.** and **.q.** Then I draw the front of the head—forehead, nose, mouth, chin, the cheeks, ears—then the eyes, and then the open space of the forehead, into all the previously indicated marks. And I make the neck one 14th part <note>1/14th of the total figure's height</note> thick.

Next, I draw the contour lines between the intersecting lines of the front-facing rectangle. First, the rounding of the head, so that it touches the horizontal **.n.** at the top center, and both sides touch the verticals **.a.** and **.b.** between **.p.** and **.q.** Then, from the two verticals under the horizontal **.r.**, I draw the jawlines down to the end of the chin. I draw the ears on both sides between the verticals **.a. c.** and **.n. b.**, and between the horizontals **.q. s.** But the inner lobes I draw inside the verticals **.c. n.** I mention this again so that it may be better noted. Then I draw the neck down from under the ears to the horizontal **.y.**; there I make it one 15th part wide. Then I draw the open space of the forehead on both sides across the middle between the verticals **.a. c.** and **.n. b.** and between the horizontals **.p. q.** And from the line **.g.** on the horizontal **.p.**, I draw the hair above the forehead upwards on both sides of the clearing, until the middle between the horizontal lines **.o. p.** Then I draw both eyes, so that their corners touch the vertical lines **.e. g.** and **.i. l.**; and I draw the eyebrows at the verticals **.f. k.** over the horizontal **.q.**, letting them end on both sides under this line, in the middle between the verticals **.d. e.** and **.l. m.** Then I draw the nose, but at the bottom I do not let its wings <note>the nostrils or base of the nose</note> touch the two verticals **.g. i.**; however, I draw the mouth so that both its corners touch them.

Afterward, I draw the back of the head according to the outer lines of the front-facing view and add its proper features.

Then I draw the head by transferring it into the **foreshortened ground** <note>original: "nydergedruckten grund," referring to a perspective view seen from above or below</note>, just as was previously described for the man’s head. I have sketched this out so precisely in the following pages that it will be clear to anyone of even modest understanding, even if there were no writing accompanying it.

And if I wish to increase the height or width of this woman’s head, I do as I indicated for the man’s head. However, it is not quite the same, for it is fitting that the woman’s head remains smaller than the man’s.

<meta>This page transitions from the technical drafting of adult figures to the specific proportions of a young child. Next, I will describe and draft <term>auffreissen: to draw a technical or architectural plan; to sketch proportions</term> a young child <note>original: "jung kindlein"</note>. For this, I will use the same method <note>original: "ordnung"</note> as with the previously described figures. First, depending on how slender I want to make the child, I set three vertical lines or points next to each other that represent that height. With the first, I will draw the child from the side; with the second, from the front; and with the third, the child from behind. These three vertical lines I bound at the top and bottom with two horizontal lines <term>zwerch linien: crosswise or horizontal lines</term>, letting the top be **.a.** and the bottom **.z.**

Further, I divide the length of the parts in this child with horizontal lines. And as **.a.** and **.z.** must touch the crown of the head <term>scheitel: the top or vertex of the head</term> and the soles of the feet, I measure down one fourth part from the crown. There, I place a horizontal line **.b.**, which touches the base of the little neck on the body at the back and at the front where the hollow of the throat <term>halßgrüblein: the suprasternal notch or "neck-dimple"</term> is located.

From this height, I create a square <term>vierung: a four-sided grid or rectangular frame used for drafting</term> for the head standing next to it. Within this, I enclose the head and mark the front vertical side where the nose protrudes with an **.a.** and the rear side with a **.b.** And before I proceed further with the child, I will first fully describe the head.

To start, I divide it according to its length—the heights and depths—with horizontal lines, as often mentioned before. Down from the horizontal **.a.**, I measure one 24th part and place a horizontal line; this touches the top of the forehead at the front. But down from **.a.** one 16th part, I place a horizontal **.c.**, which goes through...

Next, I draw a slanted line <note>original: "schleme lini"</note> from the point where the vertical **.e.** and the horizontal **.p.** meet, extending into the corner **.a. n.** Above this line, between **.p.** and **.q.**, I draw the forehead; however, I draw the nose below it, between **.q.** and **.r.**

Then I divide the second square <term>vierung: a four-sided grid frame used for drafting</term> of the front-facing <term>fürfichtig: a frontal or "forward-looking" view</term> head. First, I do this by transferring the horizontal lines from the first square. Then I also divide it with vertical lines <term>auffrecht: upright or vertical</term>. The two vertical side-lines of this square I mark with **.a.** and **.b.** Next, I divide the space between **.a.** and **.b.** with nine lines—**.d. e. f. g. h. i. k. l. m.**—into ten equal fields. Further, I divide the sections **.a. d.** and **.m. b.** with two lines, **.c.** and **.n.**, each in the middle. These should be drawn with smaller and gentler contours <note>original: "geprechen"; here referring to the character of the line-work</note> and a more delicate touch than the man's face. First, I draw the head from the side, such that the top of the head touches the horizontal **.n.** at the vertical **.J.** Similarly, the back touches the vertical **.b.** between the horizontals **.p.** and **.q.** After that, I draw the front of the head: the forehead, nose, mouth, chin, the cheeks <term>kinpacken: literally "chin-cheeks," referring to the jawline or lower cheeks</term>, and the ears; then the eyes, and finally the bare part of the forehead <note>the hairline</note>, according to all the previously indicated marks. And I make the neck one-fourteenth part thick.

Next, I draw the contour lines between the cross-lines of the front-facing square. First, the rounding of the head, so that it touches the horizontal **.n.** in the middle at the top, and on both sides it touches the verticals **.a.** and **.b.** between **.p.** and **.q.** Then, from the two verticals below the horizontal **.r.**, I draw the jawlines down to the end of the chin. Then I draw the neck down from under the ears to the horizontal **.v.**, where I make it one-fifteenth part wide. After that, I draw the bareness of the forehead on both sides over the middle between the verticals **.a. c.** and **.n. b.** and between the horizontals **.p.** and **.q.** From the line **.g.** on the horizontal **.p.**, I draw the hair above the forehead on both sides, following the bareness upward until the middle between the horizontals **.o.** and **.p.** Then I draw both eyes so that their corners touch the vertical lines **.e. g.** and **.i. l.** I draw the eyebrows <term>augprawen: eyebrows</term> at the verticals **.f.** and **.k.** above the horizontal **.q.**, letting them end on both sides below this line, exactly between the verticals **.d. e.** and **.l. m.** Then I draw the nose, but I do not let its base <note>the nostrils/wings of the nose</note> touch the two verticals **.g.** and **.i.** The mouth, however, I draw so that it touches both of those lines with its corners.

Then I draw the head by transferring it into the ground-plan <note>original: "nydergedruckten grund"; likely referring to a foreshortened top-down view or base projection</note>, just as was described before for the man’s head. I have sketched this so accurately in the following pages that it will be clear to anyone of even small understanding, even if there were no writing accompanying it.

Next, I wish to describe and draft <note>original: "auffreissen"</note> a young child <note>original: "kindlein"</note>. For this, I will use the same order as with the previously described figures. First, according to how tall I intend to make the child, I place three vertical lines or points side by side, which represent the same height. <meta>This page continues the technical geometric breakdown of a child's head, specifically focusing on the grid of horizontal and vertical lines used to determine the exact placement of facial features in a profile view.</meta>

the rear crown <term>wirbel: the "whorl" or cowlick at the top-back of the head</term> on the head; from this line downward, I set another, **.d.**, at a distance of one-sixth.
<margin>chin</margin> This line touches the chin <note>The original text says "knie" (knee), but contextually and according to the marginalia, this refers to the "kinn" (chin).</note> below, and the head rests within it at the back upon the neck. It also touches the top of the shoulder at the height of the body where the shoulder flesh is pulled upward; for one should note that the neck, in its length, is drawn with double serpentine lines.

Afterward, I divide **.d. b.** with two points into three equal fields and cut off the bottom third with a line **.e.** Upon this, the "tröllein" <note>original: "tröllein" — likely referring to the rounded flesh or larynx area</note> on the neck below the chin touches, and thus the infant gains a very short little neck. However, I divide **.a. b.** with a line **.f.** in the middle; this touches the eyebrows at the front and back, but in their center, I raise the line above them. It also touches the top of the ears, and the vertical line **.b.** touches the head there. Further, I divide **.f. d.** with a line **.g.** in the middle; this touches the bottom of the nose and the bottom of the ears. Then, I divide **.f. g.** with two lines, **.h. i.**, into three equal fields; the upper, **.h.**, goes through the middle of the eyeball and both corners of the eyes. I enclose the entire eye, with all its parts, between the horizontal lines **.f. i.**, and the line **.i.** touches the top of the nostrils, and I place the lower tip of the earlobe between **.i. g.** Afterward, I divide **.g. d.** with four horizontal lines, **.k. l. m. n.**, into five equal fields. Line **.k.** touches the top of the upper lip, but I draw line **.l.** through the middle of the mouth. Then I divide **.l. m.** in the middle; in the upper part, I place the lower lip, but in the lower part, I place the hollow <term>holkele: the small cleft or indentation</term> below the lower lip above the chin on line **.m.** I also draw a special line near line **.l.** for the depth of the head above the nape of the neck, where this will be shown hereafter. Line **.n.** touches the front dimple in the chin and the rounding of the cheeks below, and on the side, it touches the top of the little neck.

Now that the horizontal lines are drawn, I then draw the vertical lines into them, which indicate all the depths of the parts of the head, and I also mark them with their letters. I divide the vertical lines **.a. b.** with a vertical **.c.** in the middle; this touches the front of the ear and stands in the middle of the body. But I set a line **.d.** midway between **.a. c.**; this touches the back of the eyebrows, and at the front, the little neck and the clear expanse of the forehead.

Afterward, I divide **.a. d.** with four lines, **.e. f. g. h.**, into five equal fields. Line **.e.** touches the curve above the nose in front of the eye and the upper lip. But line **.f.** touches the ends of the eyebrows at the front and the wing of the nose at the back; at the front, it touches the cheeks, the corner of the mouth, and the hollow under the lower lip above the chin. Line **.g.** touches the hair on the forehead above and the depth of the eyelid above, as well as the eyeball in front and the indentation under the chin. Line **.h.** touches the back corners of the eyes, but the front corners project past line **.g.** by half the distance toward **.f.** However, I divide **.a. e.** with a line **.i.** in the middle; this touches the forehead in front, though the little nose projects slightly forward. Afterward, I divide **.e. f.** with a vertical **.l. d.** <note>Note: the author uses "l. d." to denote a specific vertical alignment</note> in the middle; this touches the lower lip in front and the chin in front. Then, from **.a.**, I place a line **.k.** three-twentieths of the way backward; this touches the back of the ear, but I place the lower earlobe halfway toward line **.c.** Furthermore, I set a line **.l.** a fifth part back from **.a.**; this touches the aforementioned indentation at the bottom of the head above the nape. The neck ends at the back midway between **.k. l.** Afterward, I divide **.l. b.** with three points into four equal fields, and at the third point, I place the crown at the back of the head.

Now that I have drawn the square <term>fierung: a drafting grid</term> of the profile head <term>nebensichtig: "side-sighted" or profile view</term> with the vertical and horizontal lines, resulting in many small squares, I will draw the specific placement of the facial features therein. But before I do that, I will first indicate the length of the parts through the body.

...the middle from each other; it goes right through the crown, nose, mouth, and chin. Afterward, I divide **.d.e.** with two lines **.f.** and **.g.** into three equal fields; these touch the inner corners of the eyes, on the outside the nostrils <note>original: "naſleplein" — literally "little nose-wings"</note>, the corners of the little mouth, and at the front, the width of the chin.

Afterward, I divide **.a.d.** with **.h.i.** and **.e.b.** with **.k.l.** into six equal fields; these two, **.h.** and **.l.**, touch on both sides the width of the face at the horizontal line <term>zwerch: a horizontal or transverse line</term> **.h.** Also, I draw the lower earlobes inside these vertical lines **.h.** and **.l.**, and I draw the ears with their upper part against the square of the vertical lines **.a.b.** The two vertical lines **.i.** and **.k.** should touch the outside curvature of the cheeks.

I bend this little child through the spine and in its limbs, as was mentioned before regarding the other figures.
I make such a child one-third of its mother's length <note>Dürer often uses the adult female figure as a reference scale for infants to maintain consistent proportions in a composition</note>.

Since I have now indicated the length, thickness, and width of the child from the side and from the front, I then carefully draw the contour lines into this record—first to see the child from the side, then from the front. And I draw the rear view of the child according to the outer lines of the front view, and I make the back between the armpits <note>original: "vchsen" — an archaic spelling for armpits</note> a little wider than a fifth part, and I split the buttocks from the bottom up by a ninth part, and I make the heels a 22nd part wide, just as I have properly drafted it hereafter <term>auffgerissen: a technical term for an architectural or anatomical scale drawing</term>.

And no one should take offense or be misled if perhaps the form becomes distorted, shrinks during the gluing of the wood <note>referring to the construction of the woodblocks used for printing</note>, or is somewhat misaligned during carving; but the written text remains more reliable, by which everyone may guide themselves, even though in the process of correcting, some things might occasionally be overlooked.

[A decorative calligraphic flourish consisting of two large interlocking loops with thinner swirling lines, resembling an ornamental infinity symbol.]

He includes a notable disclaimer advising the reader to trust his written proportions over the printed woodcut illustrations, which were prone to warping or carving errors.</summary>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page begins "The Second Book" of Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion" (1528). It transitions from the more rigid geometric methods of the first book to a more flexible system using a proportional measuring rod.</meta>

## The Second Book.

In this second little book, I will teach a new method and a different approach for measuring the human figure—namely, using a measuring rod. I can make this rod long or short, depending on whether I want to measure large or small figures. I always make this measuring rod one-sixth the total length of the figure that I intend to measure.

I also create a special symbol for it; wherever I place this mark, it should be understood that I mean one whole measuring rod.
And its symbol looks like this. ———————————————————————————————— ⚑

Next, I divide this measuring rod into ten equal fields. I call one of these fields a "number" <term>German: *zall*</term>, and the symbol I give it, by which the "number" shall be recognized when I write it, looks like this. ———————————————————————————————— §

Next, I divide one "number" into ten equal fields. I call one of these fields a "part" <term>German: *teil*</term>, and the symbol I give it, by which the "part" is recognized, looks like this ———————————————————— |

Next, I divide one of these small parts into three equal fields. I call one of these fields a "particle" <term>German: *trümlein*</term> <note>Literally "a little crumb" or "fragment."</note>, and I give it a symbol by which it is recognized, which looks like this. —————————————————————————————————————— x

When I wish to measure with this rod and its contents, I draw a vertical table next to the text with five vertical lines divided into four equal fields. I label each of the fields at the top with its name and its character or symbol underneath. In the first field, I place the measuring rod ⚑. In the second, the number §. In the third, the part |. And in the fourth, I place the particle x. Thus, each has its own special field. When I then begin to describe the length, thickness, and width of the external human form and its limbs, I will always extend a line from the text into the aforementioned table. There, I will use digits to set down the parts and numbers of each limb in whichever field they fall—whether in the measuring rod, number, part, or particle—recording how much the measure of length, thickness, or width gives, be it much or little. However, where there is nothing to record, I will place a zero <term>Latin: *nulla*</term> or .o. there.

Now I prepare myself for this measurement just as I maintained the order in the first book. I set three vertical lines for the figure's length before me, and I close them at the top and bottom with two cross-lines called "crown" <term>German: *scheitel*</term> and "sole" <term>German: *soln*</term>. By the first line, I draw the figure from the side; by the second, the front view <term>German: *fürschtichtig*</term>; and by the third, the back view <term>German: *hinderwertig*</term>. Next, I must measure the length of the limbs, starting with the side view <term>German: *nebensichtig*</term>, and I mark these lengths of the limbs and joints with cross-lines. From the side view, I draw as many cross-lines as I will need into the other two vertical lines of the front and back views. I will record all measurements of the limb lengths with vertical lines behind the side view. Whatever the measurement of each line contains and yields according to the measuring rod, number, part, or particle, I will mark that line with the symbol of the rod, number, part, or particle, and add the digit for how much of each it contains. In the same way, I will also mark, as described above, every cross-line of the side and front views, recording how much thickness and width is in each...

<summary>Albrecht Dürer introduces a new proportional measurement system for the human body based on a "measuring rod" that is 1/6th of a figure's total height. He explains how this rod is subdivided into smaller units (numbers, parts, and particles) and how these measurements are recorded in a standardized table alongside front, side, and back view drawings.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, measuring rod, zall, teil, trümlein, anatomy, artistic theory, measurement units, front view, side view, back view</keywords>

| Above the knee — | 0 | 4 | 6 | 0 |
| In the middle of the knee — | 0 | 4 | 4 | 0 |
| Under the knee — | 0 | 4 | 0 | 0 |
| In the middle of the calf — | 0 | 5 | 0 | 0 |
| At the end of the outer calf | 0 | 4 | 6 | 2 |
| At the end of the inner calf | 0 | 3 | 9 | 0 |
| Down at the shinbone — | 0 | 2 | 2 | 0 |
| In the instep through the ankles — | 0 | 2 | 7 | 0 |
| Under the ankles in the foot | 0 | 2 | 4 | 0 |
| And the foot is wide — | 0 | 4 | 2 | 0 |

Afterwards, I determine the widths of the arm as seen from the front as follows.

| | † Measuring rod | 3 Number | L Part | ∷ Particle |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| The arm is wide under the shoulder — | 0 | 3 | 5 | 0 |
| Behind the elbow — | 0 | 3 | 4 | 0 |
| In front of the elbow in width | 0 | 4 | 0 | 0 |
| At the wrist joint — | 0 | 2 | 4 | 0 |
| And the hand is wide — | 0 | 4 | 3 | 0 |

Thus, now that all the lengths, thicknesses, and widths of the limbs have been described...

<summary>This page provides exhaustive data tables for the thickness and width of various body parts for a front-facing figure. These measurements allow an artist to construct a three-dimensional "blocked-out" human form based on Dürer's precise mathematical scale.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the specific instructions for drafting the male figure based on the numerical proportions provided in the preceding tables. It bridges the gap between the raw measurements and the practical application of drawing the figure in front, side, and rear views.</meta>

... are; then I draw within them, using lines, the figure of the man in profile <note>original: "nebenſichtig"</note> and from the front <note>original: "fürſichtig"</note>.

Also outline the man seen from behind using the outer contours of the man seen from the front.

And make him broad at the back between the ears. —
And the ears at the back are lower than those at the front; therefore, the distance is measured downwards from the height of the shoulder joint <note>original: "axelglig"</note> to the back ears. —
And the rear is divided upwards from below <note>This refers to the cleft of the buttocks and the separation of the legs.</note>. —
And the heel is broad. —

And just as described above, I have hereafter sketched <note>original: "aufgeriſſen," referring to a technical or architectural-style draft</note> this man, so that the written word and the visual work may together help provide a clearer understanding.

<summary>This page provides exhaustive data tables for the "proportional woman," specifying the depth and width of every body part from the head to the feet. Dürer concludes by explaining how these measurements are used to construct front, side, and rear-view anatomical drawings.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, anatomical measurements, woman's proportions, Renaissance art theory, measurement units, technical drawing</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of human proportions, likely from Albrecht Dürer’s "Four Books on Human Proportion" (1528). Having established the first figure, the author now applies his systematic measurement process—using his unique fractional scale—to a second male figure, detailing both vertical lengths and side-view thicknesses.</meta>

Following this, I will measure another man in the same order as the first.

And first, I take for myself and measure the length of the limbs and necessary parts along the first vertical line as follows.

| | Scale | Number | Part | Minute |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| From the crown to the back whorl <note>original: "hindern wirbel"; refers to the occiput or the cowlick at the back of the head</note> of the head is | 0 | 1 | 0 | 0 |
| To the forehead | 0 | 2 | 2 | 0 |
| To the end of the chin | 0 | 8 | 6 | 0 |
| To the height of the shoulder muscle <note>The trapezius muscle where it meets the neck</note> | 0 | 9 | 2 | 0 |
| Down into the hollow of the neck <note>term: <term>hals grüblein</term> — the suprasternal notch at the base of the throat</note> | 1 | 1 | 4 | 0 |

Between the forehead and the end of the chin, I divide with two horizontal lines <note>original: "zwerch linien"</note> into three equal fields: in the uppermost, I place the forehead; in the second, the nose, eyes, and ears; in the third, the mouth and chin. After that, I divide further as follows.

| | Scale | Number | Part | Minute |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **From the height of the hollow of the neck to** | | | | |
| To the height of the shoulder joint is | 0 | 0 | 6 | 0 |
| To the breast | 0 | 2 | 2 | 0 |
| To the front of the armpit | 0 | 4 | 0 | 0 |
| To the back of the armpit | 0 | 5 | 0 | 0 |
| Upon the nipples | 0 | 5 | 8 | 0 |
| Under the breast | 0 | 7 | 0 | 0 |
| Into the waist <note>original: "weichen"; the soft part of the flank</note> | 1 | 0 | 7 | 0 |
| **From the waist to** | | | | |
| To the navel is | 0 | 2 | 2 | 0 |
| To the end of the hip | 0 | 6 | 0 | 0 |
| Upon the genitals | 0 | 7 | 7 | 0 |
| To the end of the buttocks | 0 | 9 | 8 | 0 |
| **From the end of the buttocks to** | | | | |
| To the fold of the leg <note>original: "einpeissung des peins"; the gluteal fold where the thigh meets the buttock</note> | 0 | 5 | 0 | 0 |
| Upon the knee | 0 | 9 | 7 | 0 |
| Into the middle of the knee | 1 | 2 | 1 | 0 |
| **From the middle of the knee to** | | | | |
| Under the knee is | 0 | 1 | 9 | 0 |
| To the end of the outer calf | 0 | 5 | 9 | 0 |

<column-break/>

| | Scale | Number | Part | Minute |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| To the end of the inner calf | 0 | 6 | 6 | 0 |
| To the height of the instep | 1 | 3 | 3 | 0 |
| To the end of the ankle bone in the shinbone | 1 | 4 | 3 | 0 |
| To the end of the sole | 1 | 6 | 0 | 0 |
| And the foot is long | 1 | 0 | 0 | 0 |

Afterward, I determine the length of the arm as follows:

| | Scale | Number | Part | Minute |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| From the shoulder joint into the elbow is | 1 | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| From the elbow into the joint of the hand | 0 | 8 | 6 | 0 |
| From the joint of the hand to the ends of the fingers | 0 | 6 | 4 | 0 |

Afterward, I determine at the first vertical line, along all horizontal lines, the thickness of the man from the side <note>original: <term>seytlichen mans</term> — a profile view</note> as follows:

| | Scale | Number | Part | Minute |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| The man seen from the side <note>term: <term>nebensichtig</term> — seen from the side or in profile</note> is thick in the head at the horizontal line of the back whorl | 0 | 5 | 3 | 0 |
| On the line of the forehead | 0 | 7 | 1 | 0 |
| Across the eyebrows | 0 | 7 | 8 | 0 |
| Across the nose | 0 | 7 | 0 | 0 |
| Across the lips | 0 | 6 | 4 | 0 |
| Across the neck and chin | 0 | 6 | 4 | 0 |
| And on this line the neck is thick | 0 | 4 | 2 | 0 |
| But on the line of the height of the shoulder muscle | 0 | 4 | 5 | 0 |
| And across the hollow of the neck | 0 | 5 | 5 | 0 |
| At the shoulder joints | 0 | 6 | 6 | 0 |
| Across the breast | 0 | 8 | 0 | 0 |
| At the front of the armpit | 0 | 8 | 7 | 1 |
| Across the nipples | 0 | 8 | 8 | 0 |
| Under the breasts | 0 | 8 | 1 | 0 |
| In the waist | 0 | 7 | 4 | 0 |
| In the navel | 0 | 7 | 5 | 0 |
| At the end of the hip | 0 | 8 | 6 | 0 |
| At the genitals | 0 | 7 | 7 | 0 |
| And the leg is thick at the end of the buttocks | 0 | 6 | 6 | 0 |
| At the fold of the leg | 0 | 6 | 0 | 0 |
| Above the knee | 0 | 5 | 5 | 0 |

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of human proportions from Albrecht Dürer’s "Four Books on Human Proportion" (1528). The previous page established the vertical measurements for a second male figure; here, the author details the thicknesses (depth) from a side-view and the widths from a front-view, concluding with instructions on how to outline the figure from the back.</meta>

G iiiij <note>This is a signature mark, used by early printers to keep the pages of a book in the correct order during binding.</note>

| | scale | number | part | minute |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| In the middle of the knee | 0 | 4 | 0 | 0 |
| Below the knee | 0 | 4 | 0 | 0 |
| In the middle of the calf | 0 | 4 | 4 | 0 |
| At the end of the outer calf | 0 | 4 | 1 | 0 |
| At the end of the inner calf | 0 | 3 | 9 | 0 |
| At the bottom, at the thinnest part of the shinbone | 0 | 2 | 5 | 0 |
| In the instep | 0 | 2 | 8 | 0 |
| At the end of the outer ankle bone <note>original: "knorrē"; referring to the lateral malleolus</note> in the foot | 0 | 4 | 4 | 0 |

And thereafter I make the thicknesses <note>Referring to the depth of the body when viewed from the side</note> of the side-view arm as follows:

| | | | | |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| The arm is thick in the armpit <note>original: "arsel"; an archaic term for the axilla</note> | 0 | 4 | 8 | 0 |
| In the muscle <note>term: <term>maus</term> — literally "mouse," a common Renaissance term for a prominent muscle, likely the biceps here</note> | 0 | 4 | 0 | 0 |
| In the elbow | 0 | 2 | 6 | 0 |
| Before the elbow | 0 | 2 | 7 | 0 |
| In the joint of the hand <note>The wrist</note> | 0 | 1 | 7 | 0 |
| And the hand is thick | 0 | 2 | 0 | 0 |

Thereafter I make the widths of the front-facing man along the other vertical lines on all horizontal lines <note>original: "zwerchlinien"</note> thus:

| | | | | |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| The man is wide in the head on the line of the back whorl <note>The occiput or crown of the head</note> | 0 | 5 | 5 | 0 |
| On the line of the forehead | 0 | 6 | 8 | 1 |
| Across the eyebrows | 0 | 6 | 3 | 0 |
| Across the ears | 0 | 6 | 9 | 0 |
| Across the nose and jaws | 0 | 5 | 0 | 0 |
| And the neck is wide at the chin | 0 | 4 | 1 | 0 |
| At the height of the shoulder muscle <note>The trapezius</note> | 0 | 5 | 0 | 0 |
| And the width at the hollow of the neck <note>The suprasternal notch</note> is | 1 | 1 | 2 | 0 |
| And below that, the armpit joints stand far from each other | 1 | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| But the width across the breast is | 1 | 4 | 7 | 0 |
| And between the armpits is | 1 | 3 | 0 | 0 |
| But between the nipples <note>original: "tütlen"</note> is | 0 | 7 | 2 | 0 |
| And the body is wide under the breasts | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 |
| In the flanks <note>original: "weychen"; the soft part of the waist</note> | 0 | 9 | 9 | 0 |

<column-break/>

| | scale | number | part | minute |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| Across the navel | 1 | 3 | 0 | 0 |
| Across the end of the hip | 1 | 1 | 9 | 0 |
| And on this line the hips stand far from each other | 0 | 8 | 8 | 0 |
| But the width at the pubic area <note>original: "scham"</note> is | 1 | 1 | 8 | 0 |
| And at the end of the buttocks <note>original: "hinderen"</note> the leg is wide | 0 | 5 | 6 | 0 |
| At the indentation of the leg | 0 | 4 | 4 | 0 |
| Above the knee | 0 | 3 | 5 | 0 |
| In the middle of the knee | 0 | 3 | 0 | 0 |
| Below the knee | 0 | 3 | 3 | 0 |
| In the middle of the calf | 0 | 4 | 0 | 0 |
| At the end of the outer calf | 0 | 3 | 6 | 0 |
| At the end of the inner calf | 0 | 3 | 1 | 0 |
| At the bottom in the shinbone, at the thinnest | 0 | 1 | 6 | 0 |
| Through the instep over the ankle bones in the instep | 0 | 2 | 2 | 0 |
| Through the foot below the ankle bone | 0 | 2 | 0 | 0 |
| And the foot is wide | 0 | 3 | 5 | 0 |

Following this, I make the widths of the front-facing arm thus:

| | | | | |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| The arm is wide in the muscle down from the armpit | 0 | 2 | 6 | 0 |
| Before the elbow in width | 0 | 3 | 0 | 0 |
| At the joint of the hand | 0 | 2 | 0 | 0 |
| And the hand is wide | 0 | 3 | 2 | 0 |

Now that all the lengths, thicknesses, and widths of the man have been described, I then draw in with lines the figure of the man in side-view and front-view.

Thereafter, I outline the rear-facing man along the third vertical line with the outer lines of the front-facing man. And I make him wide at the back between the armpits.

| | | | | |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| And I make him wide at the back between the armpits | 1 | 1 | 5 | 0 |
| And the buttocks are cleft upward from the end | 0 | 6 | 3 | 0 |
| And the heel is wide | 0 | 2 | 0 | 0 |

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical study of human proportions. Following the numerical tables from the previous page, Dürer now transitions to the visual application of these measurements, defining the "square" proportions of the body and providing a detailed list of anatomical landmarks for the figure shown in the accompanying woodcut.</meta>

When this man stretches out his hands crosswise, he forms a square; he reaches as wide as he is tall.
And just as I have described this man above, I have sketched him accordingly in what follows, so that I might understand it the better.

<image-desc>
An anatomical woodcut illustrating the proportions of a male figure in profile, facing right. The figure is overlaid with a technical grid of horizontal lines and vertical measurement boxes containing numbers. To the right of the figure is a vertical column of text labels identifying anatomical landmarks aligned with the horizontal lines. To the far right, a separate diagram displays a human arm, also marked with measurement lines and numerical proportions.
</image-desc>

Now I will, following the description of the man, describe a **measured woman** <note>original: "gemes weyb"; this refers to a woman proportioned according to a mathematical canon</note> / and begin in the same order as previously shown / and first measure the length along the first upright line.

From the crown of the head <note>original: "schentel"</note> high down to the back whorl on the head is —

| measuring rod | number | part | drum |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| | ⟓ | 🕀 | Ⅼ | % |
| | 0 | 1 | 0 | 0 |

But from the crown down
| | | | | |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| To the line of the forehead — | 0 | 2 | 4 | 0 |
| To the end of the chin — | 0 | 8 | 6 | 0 |
| To the height of the shoulder — | 1 | 1 | 0 | 0 |
| Into the hollow of the neck — | 1 | 2 | 0 | 0 |

Between the forehead and the end of the chin, I divide with two lines into three equal fields / in the topmost I place the forehead / in the second the nose / eyes / and in the third the mouth and chin / After that, I divide further.

From the hollow of the neck down
| | | | | |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| To the height of the breast is — | 0 | 1 | 5 | 0 |
| To the front groin fold <note>original: "vren"; refers to the inguinal fold or waist crease</note> — | 0 | 3 | 4 | 0 |
| To the back waist — | 0 | 4 | 0 | 0 |
| Onto the nipples <note>original: "tütlein"</note> — | 0 | 6 | 0 | 0 |
| Under the breast — | 0 | 7 | 4 | 0 |
| Into the flanks <note>original: "weychen"; the soft part of the waist</note> — | 1 | 1 | 6 | 0 |

| | | | | |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |

From the end of the buttocks down

| To the middle of the knee — | 1 | 0 | 1 | 0 |

Now I will follow [the description of] this man by describing a well-proportioned woman, and I begin in the same order as previously shown, measuring first the length along the first upright line.

| | measuring rod | number | parts | small-parts |
|:---|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|

| To the end of the chin — | 0 | 8 | 6 | 0 |
| To the shoulder height — | 1 | 1 | 0 | 0 |
| Into the hollow of the throat — | 1 | 2 | 0 | 0 |

Between the forehead and the end of the chin, I divide with two lines into three equal fields <note>Dürer is applying the classical "Rule of Thirds" for the face: forehead, nose, and lower face.</note>. <summary>This page provides a detailed technical breakdown of the anatomical measurements for a "well-proportioned" woman, covering both vertical lengths and profile thicknesses. It utilizes Dürer's specific measurement system of rods, parts, and "small-parts" to define every feature from the crown of the head to the sole of the foot.</summary>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical breakdown of the female body's proportions, shifting from vertical lengths to the "thickness" (depth) and "width" (breadth) of various body parts. It provides the data necessary to construct a three-dimensional understanding of the figure from the front, side, and back views.</meta>

| | measuring rod | number | parts | small-parts |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| In the instep | 0 | 2 | 9 | 0 |
| At the end of the outer ankle bone through the foot | 0 | 4 | 4 | 0 |

Afterward, I determine the thicknesses of the side-view arm as follows:

| | measuring rod | number | parts | small-parts |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| The arm is thick at the armpit <note>original: "arsel"</note> | 0 | 4 | 4 | 0 |
| Below the shoulder in the muscle <note>original: "maus"; the term "muscle" traditionally comes from the Latin "musculus" or "little mouse," which Dürer uses here to refer to the bicep.</note> | 0 | 3 | 8 | 0 |
| In the elbow | 0 | 2 | 7 | 0 |
| Before the elbow <note>referring to the forearm</note> | 0 | 2 | 6 | 0 |

Once all these lengths, thicknesses, and widths have been described and sketched, I then draw the figure of the woman with her lines skillfully arranged; the first vertical line serves for the outer profile, while the second serves for the front-facing view.

Afterward, I outline the rear-facing woman along the third vertical line, using the outer lines of the front-facing woman.

| | measuring rod | number | parts | small bits |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| But the rear-facing woman is wide at the back between the armpits | 1 | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| Also, the rear armpits stand lower than the front ones; therefore, from the height of the hollow of the neck down to the rear armpits is | 0 | 4 | 0 | 0 |
| And the buttocks are divided from below | 0 | 6 | 5 | 0 |
| Also, the heel is wide at the back | 0 | 1 | 7 | 0 |

And just as described above, I have sketched this woman accordingly.

Now I draw the contour lines of the man and the woman into the previously described lengths, thicknesses, and widths of their measurements in such a way that, somewhere between the horizontal lines <note>original: "zwerch linien," the transverse lines of the proportion grid.</note>, the contour line curves in or out, just as I have then diligently drafted <note>original: "auffgerissen," a technical term for architectural or geometric drawing.</note> the same in the next two

<column-break/>

figures of the man and woman.

However, if you wish to enlarge the head on these figures, do it like this: increase the vertical line of the figure’s length by this amount:

| | † measuring rod | z number | l part | % small parts |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| | 0 | 0 | 8 | 0 |

And then draw the head from the side, starting from the forehead, rounding upwards to the predetermined height, and from there backwards and out, and down to the nape of the neck <note>original: "gnick."</note> and into the neck.

But for the frontal head, draw from the added height round outwards and downwards on both sides, and at the horizontal line of the eyebrows <note>original: "augpraen."</note>, draw it back in towards the ears, just as I have also indicated in the drawing.

Now that all these lengths, thicknesses, and widths are described and plotted, I then draw the figure of the man into them with traced lines <note>original: "ertrignen linien," likely meaning lines transferred or drafted from the data</note>; at the first upright line according to the side view; but at the second upright line I draw his figure facing forward.

Afterward, I outline the rear-facing figure of the man at the third upright line using the outer lines of the front-facing man, and split his buttocks from below upward.

| | measuring rod | number | part | fraction |
|:--- |:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| | 0 | 6 | 0 | 0 |
| And make his heels wide | 0 | 2 | 2 | 0 |

Also, the armpits are lower in the back than in the front; therefore, from the height of the hollow of the throat to below the rear armpits is:

| | measuring rod | number | part | fraction |
|:--- |:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| | 0 | 4 | 5 | 0 |

When you stretch out this man’s arms and raise them as high as he is long, and then place one foot of the compass on his navel, you can sweep around him with the other foot in the manner of a circle <note>This is a reference to the Vitruvian Man concept, where a man's proportions relate to the geometry of a circle and square</note>.
And as described above, I have clearly drawn these men following this, and marked them with numbers and their specific signs.

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructional series on human proportions by Albrecht Dürer. Having previously established the measurements for a male figure, the author now transitions to the proportions of a female figure, utilizing a standardized system of measurement across vertical and horizontal lines.</meta>

Following the man just previously described, I wish to describe a woman in the same order, and first, I must measure the length of the limbs and the necessary parts of the body along the first vertical line as follows:

| | Measuring Rod | Number | Part | Bit |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **From the height of the crown-line to:** | <note>Dürer uses a relative scale: 1 Rod is the total height. Numbers, Parts, and Bits are progressively smaller subdivisions.</note> | | | |

If you stretch out this woman's arms and raise them as high as she is long, you can encompass her with a circle if you place one foot of the compass on the navel and swing around with the other. <note>This describes a variation of the Vitruvian Man, applying the geometry of the circle and square to the female form.</note>

And as is described above, I have hereafter plotted and marked this woman with all the numbers and characters as they appear before your eyes, the better to guide oneself accordingly.

### Sig. M iij

I will now assign another woman to the man previously described. From the previous arrangement, I first measure the lengths of the limbs and the necessary features as follows:

| | <term>measuring rod<note>Original: "meßſtab". Marked with a cross ✝, this represents the total height of the figure.</note></term> | <term>number<note>Original: "zal". Marked with a 't', this is the largest subdivision of the rod, typically 1/6th of the height.</note></term> | <term>part<note>Original: "teyl". Marked with an 'l', this is a subdivision of the 'number'.</note></term> | <term>fraction<note>Original: "trümlein". Marked with a '%', this is the smallest unit of measurement.</note></term> |

| And from the forehead to the eyebrows is | 0 | 2 | 2 | 0 |

Just as described above, I have drawn this woman below so that you may better guide yourself by it.

And just as I have presented this man described above, I have also plotted him in the following pages so that you may better prepare yourself accordingly. One should also know that every body is wider across its front surface than it is thick from the side; however, the legs, when viewed from the side, are thicker than they are wide from the front. The figures measured in the first book with parts and numbers may also be measured approximately with the measuring rod. It makes a great difference if a figure’s head is removed and another is set in its place.

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer's technical instructions on the geometric construction and proportions of the human head, following the systematic approach established in his earlier books on proportion.</meta>

<insert>
measuring rod
number
part
small segment
§ ∟ % . <note>These symbols represent Dürer’s specific units of measurement: § (large parts), ∟ (minutes/numbers), % (parts), and . (points).</note>
</insert>

In what follows, I wish to describe two types of men's heads more precisely, as I have previously indicated in the illustrations of this booklet; and I shall partly employ the order described in the first book.

First, I create a perfect <term>square|Vierung</term> with a height of one-seventh of the figure’s total length. However, whoever wishes may make the square one-eighth of the figure’s length, provided that in the eighth-part everything is divided equally as it is in the seventh-part, in both small and large scales. Now, I take for my purpose the square as it stands above, as wide as it is high. Let the two vertical sides be **a** for the front and **b** for the back. But let the two horizontal sides be **c** for the top and **d** for the bottom. Thereafter, I divide the height of the seventh-part into eight large parts (§), and each such part into ten numbers (∟). From these, I measure as follows, in the same manner as previously shown. First, I divide the vertical lines: the first line, **e**, I place behind line **a**—

<margin>
| § | ∟ | % | . |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 0 | 9 | 0 |
</margin>
This line touches the eyeball at the front and the <term>nostrils|naßpellen</term> at the back. 
Next, I place a line, **f**, behind line **a**—

<margin>
| § | ∟ | % | . |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 1 | 8 | 0 |
</margin>
This line touches the back of the <term>eyebrows|augpraen</term>, but the eye itself remains with its back corner in the middle between **e** and **f**.
Now I place the third line, **g**, behind **a**—

<margin>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the third book of Dürer's treatise. Having established the "ideal" or standard proportions in Books One and Two, he now introduces a method for variation and distortion, allowing artists to create a diverse range of human figures by manipulating specific physical characteristics.</meta>

## The Third Book.

In this third book, I will demonstrate how one may alter and transform the previously described measurements. According to each person's will, one may increase or decrease all things, so that a figure becomes unrecognizable and nothing remains of its former shape and proportion. I will apply this in the following pages to the figures of men, women, and children, as well as to everything else.

To understand this properly, however, one must first know which things allow such transformations to happen. This can be achieved through the combination of opposites <note>Dürer uses the term "widerwertige zusammen fügung," referring to the Aristotelian concept of contraries to explain how variation is created.</note> in all proper proportions or <term>accidents|zufellen</term> <note>In historical philosophy, "accidents" are qualities that can change (like being fat or thin) without changing the essential nature of being human.</note>. For in identical things, there is no difference other than that one is not the other; therefore, identical things cannot be distinguished from one another. But those things that may be called opposites useful for measurement are the following:

<image-desc>Two lists of four antonyms grouped by braces with the word "or" centered between them.</image-desc>

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| Large | | Small |
| Long | or | Short |
| Broad | | Narrow |
| Thick | | Thin |

Following these are several attached or accidental things that also transform the shape, such as:

<image-desc>Two lists of four antonyms grouped by braces with the word "or" centered between them.</image-desc>

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| Young | | Old |
| Stout | or | Lean |
| Pleasant | | Ugly |
| Hard | | Soft |

Furthermore, one uses other things that serve to change such forms, such as:

<image-desc>Two lists of five terms describing form, grouped by braces with "or" centered between them.</image-desc>

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| Crooked | | Straight <note>original: "schlecht." In the 16th century, "schlecht" meant straight, simple, or plain, rather than "bad."</note> |
| Even | | Uneven |
| High | or | Low |
| Round | | Angular |
| Pointed | | Blunt |

These things also transform <image-desc>A right-facing brace grouping the following three items.</image-desc>
->Upright<-
->Transverse<-
->Or diagonally<-

These also transform significantly <image-desc>Two right-facing braces grouping the following four items in pairs.</image-desc>
->Adding<- <note>original: "Geben," literally 'to give'. In this context, it refers to adding to a measurement.</note>
->Subtracting<- <note>original: "Nemen," literally 'to take'.</note>
->Much<-
->Little<-

Furthermore, one sometimes uses <image-desc>A right-facing brace grouping the following six directional terms.</image-desc>
->Right<-
->Left<-
->Front<-
->Back<-
->Top<-
->Bottom<-

By using these aforementioned things, one can distinguish a new form from the one first created.

<summary>This page introduces Book Three, outlining Dürer's "theory of opposites" which provides a systematic way to vary and distort the human figure. He lists various physical and spatial qualities—such as thick/thin, young/old, and crooked/straight—that an artist can manipulate to create unique characters.</summary>
