--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Third Book, where Dürer begins explaining the practical application of his theory of "accidents" or variations in human proportion. He is transitioning from theoretical definitions to the actual methods of distorting a figure's dimensions.</meta>

...divide differently and make them different than before. We want to put this into practice a little and first take up the two words: **Large** or **Small**. These are used in all things that one wishes to make larger or smaller. In itself, this brings no other change to the shape than that one says "this is a large thing" or "that is a small thing," yet both may be of one kind and one proportion. Therefore, the written proportions can be made large or small, and each contains its own parts of measurement and number. For this, let us take the example of a model of a small, scaled-down house, after which a large palace is built and erected; or how a small sphere compares to a large one. Thus, the words "large" or "small" remain, and the shape does not change, as was mentioned above.

->But if the words "large" or "small" are to bring about a distinct change in the shape, then this must happen in certain parts and not to the whole all at once. If, however, one makes some parts larger and others smaller, then the appearance of that thing becomes different. For example, if you make a figure large above the waist and smaller from the waist down—and likewise, as mentioned above, apply this throughout all the <term>limbs|glidmaß</term> in every single part, from the smallest to the largest—the possible variations are infinite. Therefore, it should be noted that no thing is recognized as large or small next to another unless the opposite is placed against the large or small thing. Anyone may use this as far as they need.<-

->Furthermore, we want to take up the words **Long** or **Short**. From these, we want to make the first described "thick man" of the first book long and short.<- 

Now note: if this man is lengthened, he becomes thinner and narrower in his proportions; but if he is made shorter, he becomes thicker and wider according to his kind and measure. In such lengthening or shortening, one leaves the thickness and width unchanged in all things, just as they were shown in the first described man. But if the two men—the lengthened one and the shortened one—are stretched or compressed back into the length of the first described man while keeping their own [newly adjusted] scale, then their thickness and width will all be different from the first described man from which they were derived. 

And all parts of the length become equal again to the first described man, yet they look different from one another, because one has become thinner and narrower, while the other has become thicker and wider. By the path determined above, the length of the limbs is lengthened or shortened proportionally between all the <term>transverse lines|zwerch linien</term> <note>These are horizontal lines used in Dürer's grid system to mark the height of anatomical features like the knees, hips, or shoulders.</note> which then indicate the lengths of the things in the body. If you stretch them far apart, the figure lengthens; if you push them close together, it makes the figure shorter. 

Therefore, as spoken above, such a thing neither adds to nor takes away from the thickness or width in all the limbs; nevertheless, the words "long" or "short" create a difference in the figures, and if one then measures them anew with the <term>compass|zirckel</term>, their description will be different from that of the first man. Now, it would be tedious if one always had to take the trouble to search for every single part with the compass when lengthening or shortening a figure; therefore, I will hereafter set down a rule for how one should find this lengthening and shortening easily and uniformly.

->First, I must have a dotted line, after which I want to dot two others—one long and one short—proportionally. This I do as follows.<- 

I take a line for the side **a b**, as long as the first described man of the first book is. This line is dotted with the transverse lines with which the figure is described through all lengths of the limbs. This line **a b**...

<summary>Dürer explains the difference between scaling a figure uniformly (which keeps proportions the same) and scaling specific parts or dimensions (which creates new body types). He introduces his method for lengthening or shortening a figure by manipulating the horizontal reference lines of his measurement system.</summary>
<keywords>proportion, scaling, large and small, long and short, transverse lines, compass, geometry in art, anatomical distortion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the "transformer" (verkerer), a geometric tool Dürer designed to distort or scale human proportions. Having introduced the concepts of "Large" and "Small" on the previous page, he now provides the geometric construction for lengthening or shortening a figure.</meta>

<sig>D ij</sig>

I place a line upright before me / and set a point **.c.** far off to the side. From this point **.c.**, I draw straight lines through all the points of the upright line **.a. b.** and continue them further outward. Now that **.a. b. c.** has become a <term>triangle|triangel</term> / and the radiating lines look almost like a brush <note>Dürer refers to the way the lines fan out from a single point, resembling the bristles of a brush or a comb</note> / I then place two other parallel upright lines next to the upright line **.a. b.**, one on each side. The shorter one closer to point **.c.** I label at the top as **.d.** and **.e.** The longer one, however, I place further out; let it be labeled **.f.** and **.g.** at the top. By as much as I want the man to be longer / so much further out I place the line / and by as much as the man should be shorter / so much I move the line closer toward the point **.c.** Thus, what we desire is now divided by the radiating lines drawn from point **.c.** through the three upright lines **.d. e.**, **.a. b.**, and **.f. g.** / and each line is divided in exact proportion to the others.

The rule as it stands above / I have actually drawn out below / to be used not only for this / but for <term>internal|inwen-term</term> <note>original: "inwen-term," likely a contraction for "inwendig," meaning internal measurements or proportions within the body</note> use. And I give this figure its own name / and call it a <term>transformer|verkerer</term> <note>literally a "shifter" or "converter." This is Dürer's specific term for a scaling grid</note> / because a thing is transformed through it. Alongside this transformer, the two words **Much** or **Little** may be used.

Through this transformer, any thing that has <term>infinite parts|vnnentlich teyl</term> can be uniformly drawn into a larger or smaller size / for many wonderful things can be made from it.

Through this transformer, one can make a figure that was previously seven heads tall / eight, nine, or even ten heads tall / because between the head and the <term>instep|ristz</term> of the foot, the figure is always lengthened by the length of the head one, two, or three times. However, if the figure is lengthened by that much / one must then also lengthen the head a little as the situation requires / so that the head does not look too <term>stunted|muncket</term> <note>original: "muncket," referring to a head that appears too small or pinched in proportion to a stretched body</note> / but the figure will then no longer be as many "heads tall" as it was before.

Particular care must be taken when one wishes to make the specifically described hands and feet from the first <term>booklet|büchleins</term> <note>referring to the first book of this four-volume treatise</note> longer or shorter through the transformer / so that one proceeds with great diligence.

-><image-desc>A large, elaborate calligraphic flourish or ornament consisting of interlocking loops and decorative swirls, centered horizontally below the main text block.</image-desc><-

<summary>Dürer explains the geometric construction of his "transformer," a radiating grid used to lengthen or shorten human figures while maintaining proportional consistency. He specifically notes how this tool can be used to stretch a figure from seven heads tall to ten, provided the artist also adjusts the head size to avoid a stunted appearance.</summary>

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical instructions on his "transformer" (Verkerer), a geometric method for altering the proportions of the human figure. It follows the theoretical explanation of how lines can be shortened or elongated to create different body types.</meta>

<sig>D iij</sig>

Now, if these two newly made dotted lines—the long line **f. g.** and the short line **d. e.**—are each drawn through with cross-lines at all the points they have received, then I draw the figure of the man, both long and short, from the side and the front as indicated in the first small book, in all his thickness and breadth. Just as this rule indicates the length of the elongated and the shortened man, I have drawn them both as they should be. However, I could not conveniently place the elongated man here because of the smallness of the paper; therefore, I have returned them to their original length but redrawn them using their newly acquired measurements. 

By this method, which follows below—and which can be used at any time for other things as well—the measurement on line **f. g.** becomes the thinner and narrower man, and on line **d. e.** a thicker, broader man. You may also use this <term>transformer<note>Original: "verkerer." A geometric device or diagram Dürer designed to distort or "shift" proportions—such as making a figure taller or wider—while maintaining consistent ratios.</note></term> in the same way for the terms thick and thin, or broad and narrow, as you do for long and short. It is also good and proper that all figures throughout the books be drawn at a single length, so that they may be better compared against one another. 

Note also that by using the aforementioned transformer, one may change every specific part of a previously made figure and leave others as they are; yet, whatever is taken from one part is added to another—this refers to the length of the <term>limbs<note>Original: "glidmas." Referring to the anatomical segments or proportions of the body parts.</note></term>. But one must do the same for all the cross-lines of the figures by which the length is divided, and also in between, to make them thicker, thinner, broader, or narrower. For as often as you have a previously described line and place it in the transformer, setting two other lines—one before and the other after or behind—it shows you what it yields. In this transformer, you can see what you need, and it changes it for you proportionally.

Now I will show how one should proceed so that one may work from it; I will make an instrument out of the transformer so that you may use it to find how to make every previously described part longer or shorter, thicker or thinner, broader or narrower, perfectly proportional to one another through all parts of the entire divided figure's length, thickness, or breadth.

First, draw a cross-line starting at **a.** and ending at **b.** Make this line fixed and steady. But from point **a.**, extend another line ending at **c.** The line **a. c.** must be movable, yet the two lines **a. b.** and **a. c.** must never move away from each other at point **a.**, but rather remain fixed together.

And note: when I speak here of points or lines, I do not mean only those points and lines provided by <term>Mathematics<note>Original: "Mathematica." Referring here to abstract Euclidean geometry which deals with theoretical points that have no volume and lines that have no width.</note></term>—though those same invisible, indivisible points and lines are contained or intended here—but I speak here of such points and lines that are dotted and drawn with a needle or sharp-pointed pens against a <term>straightedge<note>Original: "richtscheit." A tool used for drawing straight lines, essential for the precision Dürer required.</note></term>, made understandable to the eye.

And when you wish to use this instrument, prepare the tool as follows: Draw the aforementioned line **a. b.** across a suitable flat board and drive a sharp needle firmly and securely into point **a.** Tie a clean, twisted silk thread to it, stretch it out to the end of **c.**, and use it in place of the movable line **a. c.**, for the thread is easily moved back and forth.

Once this is prepared, draw a straight, upright line **d. e.** from the middle of the cross-line **a. b.** at right angles upward.

Upon this line **d. e.**, the chosen part—be it length, thickness, or breadth—which you wish to change must be placed so that one end touches the cross-line **a. b.** below. Thereafter...

<summary>Dürer explains how to construct a physical proportional instrument using a board, a needle, and a silk thread to adjust the dimensions of a human figure. He distinguishes between theoretical mathematical lines and the practical lines drawn by an artist.</summary>
<keywords>proportional divider, Verkerer, geometry, drafting tools, human proportion, Albrecht Dürer, measurement, artistic theory</keywords>

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical explanation of how to mathematically distort the human figure—stretching or compressing it—using a geometric device he calls a "transformer." It follows the previous discussion on establishing baseline proportions.</meta>

<sig>D iij</sig>

Now, if these two newly made marked lines—the long one, **f. g.**, and the short one, **d. e.**—are each crossed with transverse lines <note>Horizontal lines that intersect the vertical axis.</note> through all the points they have received, then I draw the figure of the man, long and short, according to the side and front views as shown in the first-described booklet, in all its thickness and breadth. And just as this rule indicates the length of the elongated and the shortened man, so have I drawn them both as they should be. However, I could not conveniently place the elongated man here in the print because of the smallness of the paper. Therefore, I have placed them back into their first-described length and redrawn them accordingly with their newly gained measurements. This is done through the method that follows, which can also be used at any time for other things. Thus, the measurement on line **f. g.** results in the thinner and narrower man, and on line **d. e.**, a thicker, broader man. You may also use this transformer <note>original: "verkerer." Dürer’s term for a geometric proportion tool used to convert dimensions.</note> in the same way for the terms thick and thin, broad and narrow, as well as for long and short. It is also good and quite fitting that all figures throughout the booklets be drawn in a single length, so that one may better compare them against each other. It is also important to know that through the aforementioned transformer, one may change every single part in the previously made figure and leave others as they are; however, whatever is taken from one part must be added to another part—this refers now to the length of the limbs. But the same must also be done on all the transverse lines of the figures by which the length is divided, and also in between, to make them thicker, thinner, broader, or narrower. For as often as you have a previously described line and you set it up in the transformer, with two other lines—one this way, the other following or behind it—it shows you what it gives you. In this transformer, you may set whatever you need, and it will change it for you proportionally.

Now I will show how one should proceed so that one may work from it, and I will make an instrument out of the transformer. With it, you can find how to make every previously described part longer or shorter, thicker or thinner, broader or narrower, in total proportion to one another through all parts of the entire divided figure's length, thickness, or breadth.

First, draw a transverse line; let the beginning be **a.** and the end be **b.** Make this line fixed and steady. But from point **a.**, draw another line whose end is **c.** The line **a. c.** must be movable, yet the two lines **a. b.** and **a. c.** must never move away from each other at point **a.**, but rather remain fixed together.

And note, when I speak here of points or lines, I do not mean only those points and lines that Mathematics <note>original: "Mathematica." Dürer distinguishes between theoretical geometry and the physical act of drawing.</note> provides—although those same invisible, indivisible points and lines are contained or intended herein. Rather, I speak here of such points and lines that are dotted and drawn with a needle or a sharp pen against a straightedge, made understandable to the sight.

And if you now wish to use this instrument, prepare the tool thus: Draw upon a suitable flat board the aforementioned transverse line **a. b.**, and drive a sharp needle into point **a.** firmly and securely. Tie to it a clean, twisted silk thread; stretch it out past the end of **c.** and use it instead of the movable line **a. c.**, for the thread is easily moved back and forth.

When this is thus prepared, then draw from the middle of the transverse line **a. b.** a straight, upright line **d. e.** at right angles upward.

Upon this line **d. e.**, the chosen part—be it length, thickness, or breadth—which you then wish to change, must be placed so that one end touches the transverse line **a. b.** at the bottom. Thereafter...

<summary>Dürer describes the construction of a physical geometric "transformer" using a board, a needle, and silk thread to proportionally scale human dimensions. He emphasizes the difference between theoretical mathematical points and the practical lines drawn by an artist.</summary>
<keywords>proportion instrument, transformer, geometric scaling, Albrecht Dürer, technical drawing, artistic theory, measurement, draftsmanship</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how to use a proportional "shifter" to modify the dimensions of the human figure, transitioning from theory to the construction of a physical tool.</meta>

<sig>D iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: Thereafter</meta>

Now, when these two newly made dotted lines—the long one $f.g.$ and the short one $d.e.$—are each crossed with transverse lines through all the points they have received, I then draw the figure of the man (long and short, from the side and the front, as indicated in the first little book) in all its thickness and breadth within them. Just as this rule indicates the length of the elongated and the shortened man, I have sketched them both as they should be. However, I could not conveniently place the elongated man here in print <note>original: "in trug," likely meaning in the printed layout</note> because of the small size of the paper; therefore, I have set them back into their first-described length and redrawn them according to their newly gained measurements. This is done through the method that follows, which you can also use for other things at any time. Thus, the measurement on line $f.g.$ becomes the thinner and narrower man, and on line $d.e.$ a thicker, broader man. You may also use this <term>verkerer: a proportional shifter or transformer</term> in the same way for the qualities of "thick and thin" or "broad and narrow," just as for "long and short." It is also good and fitting that all figures throughout the little books be drawn in one length, so that they may be better compared against one another.

It is also important to know that through the aforementioned shifter, one may change every specific part of a previously made figure while leaving others as they are; however, whatever is taken from one part is added to another—this refers to the length of the limbs. But the same is also to be done on all transverse lines of the figures by which the length is divided, and also to make them thicker, thinner, broader, or narrower in between. For whenever you have a previously described line and set it up in the shifter, and place two other lines (one before and one after or behind it), the tool shows what it gives you; and into this shifter, you may set whatever you need, and it will change it for you proportionally.

Now I will show how one should proceed so that one may work from this, and I will make an instrument out of the shifter. With this tool, you can find how to make every previously described part longer or shorter, thicker or thinner, broader or narrower, in perfect proportion to one another through all parts of the whole divided figure’s length, thickness, or breadth.

First, draw a transverse line where the beginning is $a$ and the end is $b$. Make this line fixed and steady; but from point $a$ draw another line where the end is $c$. The line $a.c.$ must be movable; yet the two lines $a.b.$ and $a.c.$ must never deviate from each other at point $a$ but remain fixed together.

And note, when I speak here of points or lines, I do not mean only the points and lines that Mathematics <note>original: "die Mathematica," referring to theoretical geometry</note> provides—though those same invisible, indivisible points and lines are intended here—but I speak here of such points and lines that are dotted and drawn with a needle or sharp pen against a <term>richtscheit: a straightedge or ruler</term>, made understandable to the sight.

And if you now wish to use this instrument, prepare this tool thus: Draw on a suitable flat board the aforementioned transverse line $a.b.$ and drive a sharp needle firmly into point $a$. Tie to it a clean, twisted silk thread; stretch it out toward the end of $c$ and use it instead of the movable line $a.c.$, for the thread is easy to move back and forth.

When that is thus prepared, then draw from the middle of the transverse line $a.b.$ a straight upright line $d.e.$ at right angles upward.

On this line $d.e.$, the chosen part—be it length, thickness, or breadth—which you then wish to change, must be placed so that its bottom touches the transverse line $a.b.$ at one end. Thereafter...

<summary>Dürer explains how to use a "shifter" to proportionally scale human figures and describes how to construct a physical instrument using a board, a needle, and a silk thread to perform these geometric transformations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how to use a proportional instrument, which the author calls a "shifter" or "selector," to adjust the dimensions of a figure. It follows the description of how lengths and widths are transferred between lines.</meta>

Take the thread that stands in place of line $a.c.$ and stretch it out so that it touches the top of the part you have placed on line $d.e.$ Now, extend your intended part as much as you wish, and set one end of this extended line on the transverse line $a.b.$ at right angles, parallel to line $d.e.$ toward the end $b$. Move it upright until it touches the stretched thread $a.c.$ at the top; let this upright line be $f.g.$ If you wish to have the part shorter than the one you placed on line $d.e.$, then set your intended shorter line upright at right angles on the transverse line $a.b.$ behind the upright line $d.e.$ toward point $a$. Move it also upright on the line $a.b.$ until it touches the stretched thread $a.c.$ at the top; let this upright line be $h.i.$ 

Thus, in this <term>werckzeug: instrument or tool</term> there are three upright parallel lines next to each other. The first described length is placed on line $d.e.$; however, the lengthened part is taken from line $f.g.$, and the shortened part from line $h.i.$ Therefore, as a part is placed on line $d.e.$, the stretched thread $a.c.$—when it touches the top of that length on line $d.e.$—measures <term>vergeychliche: proportional or comparable</term> longer and shorter things on the two upright lines $f.g.$ and $h.i.$ and changes almost everything over which one has power. Much can be made from this instrument, for whether you move the thread $a.c.$ high or low, it creates proportional parts against one another, whether in long, short, or medium-sized things. This is true even if the parts are inexpressible <note>original: "innenbarlich," meaning irrational or not easily named as a simple fraction</note> in the numbers one wishes to measure—as when one cannot say "this is a 2nd part, a 3rd part, or a 5th, a 6th part," etc., as previously mentioned.

I have sketched <note>original: "auffgerissenn," meaning drafted or drawn</note> my aforementioned plan below so that everyone may properly follow it and see it before their eyes. For this reason, I also call this instrument the <term>weler: the selector or chooser</term>, so that it may be known; it is so named because one chooses from it whether one wants much or little. From such a "selector," this thinner and that thicker man were later made and sketched, as you can see from the figure of the selector. 

In this transformation, it should be noted that everything made this way (as said above) moves further apart in measure as it enters the longer, thicker, wider, and larger parts. Conversely, the smaller the parts are taken, the closer the things run together. Therefore, the thinner image transformed by the selector becomes much gentler <note>original: "linder," suggesting a more delicate or refined appearance</note> than the enlarged image. This described method can also be done with a <term>quadrant: a mathematical instrument shaped like a quarter-circle</term>, just as the compass arcs <term>zirkelriß: arcs drawn with a compass</term> in the "selector" indicate.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for modifying a human figure's proportions using Dürer's geometric transfer method. It specifically addresses how to correct the distortions—such as an overly long head or a foot that has become too short—that occur when a figure is mechanically lengthened or shortened using the described instrument.</meta>

<sig>D iiij</sig>

To this lengthened man, one-fourth of his own length is to be added. And if one wishes to use him, one must make his head a little shorter, for the lengthening gives him a deformity <note>original: "vngestalt," meaning a misshapen or disproportionate appearance</note>. Therefore, push the chin upward and divide the face again; or take from the top of the head and divide the face again using the <term>weier: a proportional transfer-instrument or 'shifter'</term>. I proceed here so that it touches at its highest point, and I make the length from the highest point of the crown to the end of the chin two-fifteenths [of the total height], and from the end of the chin upward to the end of the forehead one-tenth. After that, I divide the face lengthways again through the transfer-instrument, but it retains the thickness and narrowness as it was shaped by the instrument, just as I have drawn such faces according to my judgment next to the images. Also, I make the foot two-thirteenths long, for it has become too short through the instrument; also I make it flatter <note>original: "niedertristiger," referring to the height of the arch or the instep</note> as is appropriate. And especially, for the sake of elegance, the thighs should be drawn a little thinner and narrower; in which parts this should happen, I have indicated in the subsequently <term>auffgerissenē: drawn or plotted</term> images where I mean it to be. And this man is one-fifth thinner than the first man described in the first book from which he was altered. And how the head and all that I mean should be drawn, I have plotted in what follows, so that you may presently see the difference I present between the first-described man and the other man derived from him.

It is, however, further to be noted how this short man was made; understand it thus: In the <term>verkerer: shifter or inverter</term> where I have placed and taken the shorter line $d.e.$, I have drawn two straight lines from both ends of the lengthened line $f.g.$ crosswise through the middle of the upright line $a.b$; where their ends then intersect the two lines $c.f.$ and $e.g.$, between those points the straight line $d.e.$ is drawn, of which I spoke before. This shortened man, if he is drawn back to his original length, becomes very thick, just as the instrument produces. Yet, if one wishes, the thickness and breadth of the head should be reduced so that it remains human, if one intends to use such a figure; and make the foot again one-sixth [of the body] long. Such things are drawn hereafter; all this remains at your choice to make longer or shorter as you wish.

<summary>Dürer explains how to manually correct the aesthetic distortions—such as an elongated head or shortened feet—that result from using his geometric proportional instrument. He provides specific fractional measurements for the face and feet to ensure the modified figures remain "human" and elegant.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, geometry, anatomy, head measurements, facial proportions, aesthetic correction, drafting tools</keywords>

--- Page 197 ---
From the words "long" and "short," I will again show how to take the man’s head—which was described in the first book within its own specific squared grid <note>Dürer refers to his "würffleten vierung," a system of dividing the head into a cubic grid for proportional study.</note> divided and described by crossed lines—and how, when that is understood and taken as a **cube** <note>original: "cubo"</note> or block, one may stretch that same cube; that is, how one may heighten it or press it down. By this, one may know how much thinner and narrower it becomes when stretched, or how much thicker and wider it becomes when pressed down, for the sake of maintaining the same volume <note>original: "eins gleychen inhalts wegen," referring to the preservation of the object's mass or capacity despite the change in shape.</note>. To achieve this measure and weight, one must proceed as follows.

I take for you a proper, equal-sided cube that is as high as the aforementioned man’s head, for the head must be contained within this cube. Now, I must first deal with the cube alone before anything is drawn or marked within it. Let it be at each person's discretion how much they wish to heighten, stretch, or press down the cube; and so, begin.

Take one side of the proper cube; I mark the bottom **a** and the top **b**. I lengthen this line **a. b.** with an extension **c**, according to how much higher I want the cube to be than it was before. After that, I take the side **a. b.** and the lengthened line **a. c.** and make a right angle from them, so that **a. c.** is horizontal <note>original: "zwerch," meaning transverse or across.</note> and **a. b.** is upright, with **a** being the corner. Then, the horizontal line **a. c.** is extended out beyond the corner **a**; let the end be **d**. Thus, **d. a. c.** becomes a straight horizontal line. Then, a diagonal line **b. c.** is drawn.

Then I take a right-angled **square** <note>A "winckelhacken" or set square, a fundamental tool for draftsmen and masons.</note>—whether it be made of brass or wood—and place one of its sides against the diagonal line **b. c.** so that the corner of the square touches point **b**. Then, draw a straight line at a right angle to the tool, down from point **b**. Where this line (drawn square) intersects the horizontal line **d. a. c.**, place a point **e**. Once this is done, a comparable line has been found between the two lines **a. b.** and **a. c.**; that is the line **a. e.**

Now you must find another middle line between **a. b.** and **a. e.** To do this: Take the two lines **a. b.** and **a. e.** and join them together to make one straight horizontal line **e. a. b.** Then draw a straight vertical line upwards from point **a** at right angles. Then take a **pair of compasses** and place one foot in the center of this joined line **e. a. b.**, and place the other foot on point **e**. From there, draw an arc upward and around to point **b**. Where this arc intersects the vertical line drawn up from point **a**, mark it **f**. This is the width of one of the sides of the heightened cube; thus, **a. c.** is its height and **a. f.** is its width. <note>Dürer is using a geometric construction to find "mean proportionals." Mathematically, this ensures that the volume of the new, tall block remains exactly the same as the original cube.</note>

If, however, you want the cube to be lower than its side **a. b.** is, then it must become wider. This is done through the same path as before. But it is done in reverse, in the same way that the side of the cube **a. b.** was previously heightened with point **c**; it must now be shortened with that same point **c**. To find this expansion, do as follows: Take the side of the first cube **a. b.** and stand it upright before you. As said before, join the shorter line **a. c.** to it at a right angle or horizontally. Then extend the horizontal line **a. c.** out beyond point **a**; let the end be **d**. Draw the diagonal line **b. c.** and take a square again. Place one side of it against the diagonal line **b. c.** so that the corner of the square touches point **b**. Draw a line with the square from point **b** at a right angle through the horizontal line **d. a. c.** Where this line intersects, place a point **e**. Thus, the first middle line is found between the two lines **a. b.** and **a. c.**; that is the line **a. e.**

<summary>Albrecht Dürer explains a geometric method for altering the proportions of a cube while maintaining a constant volume. This technique allows an artist to "stretch" or "compress" a human head—represented as a cube—to create various physical types while keeping the mathematical scale consistent.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, geometry, proportion, cube, mean proportional, drafting tools, human form, mathematical art</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the geometric instructions for modifying a head's proportions by distorting its bounding cube. The previous page established points a, b, c, and d to define the new height of a "stretched" cube. This page explains how to determine the corresponding width (point f) to maintain proportional volume and how to apply these changes to the frontal view of the face.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>P ij</sig>

Now you must find the other middle line between **a. b.** and **a. e.**; do it as follows. Take the two lines **a. b.** and **a. e.** and join them together according to their length horizontally <note>original: "vber zwerch," meaning across or transversely.</note> against each other. This line is **e. a. b.** After that, draw an upright line from point **a** at equal angles straight above it. Then take a compass <note>original: "zirckel"</note> and set it with one foot in the center upon this horizontal line **e. a. b.** and with the other foot draw a circular arc from point **e** over the top to point **b**. Wherever this circular arc intersects the upright line that was drawn upwards from point **a**, mark that point **f**. This line **a. f.** is the length of one side of the widened, pressed-down **cube** <note>original: "cubus"</note>. Thus its height is **a. c.**, just as it is properly drawn <note>original: "auffgerissen," referring to a technical draft or diagram.</note> in the figures that follow.

It is also notable that in every cube—whether heightened or pressed down—the form of the face, when drawn and divided by the crosswise lines, becomes either long or short.

It should also be noted that if one wishes to draw the **front-facing view** <term>front-facing view <note>original: "fürsichtig angesicht," the face seen directly from the front.</note></term> into its cube, it should be made narrower by a fifth part of its width, as the measure dictates. Or proceed as follows: Take the width of the lengthened and pressed-down cube, and place eight of those same widths (of either sort) next to each other. Divide these eight parts into ten parts. From one of those ten parts, make the width of the cube for the front-facing view once the **narrowing** <note>original: "schmelerung"</note> has occurred in the long and short cube. Then first divide the lines through the grid intersections <note>original: "weier," likely referring to the intersection points of the proportional grid.</note> again, and draw the form of the face within it. This process described above for the head should be used throughout the entire figure.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Albrecht Dürer’s geometric methods for modifying human proportions, specifically focusing on how shifting the "cube" or grid system alters the shape of the head.</meta>

It is possible to alter the <term>cube (the geometric grid used to measure the body)</term> even further (thereby changing the shape of the head) in the following manner, as follows / so that the cube is expanded equally on all sides on the top <term>plane (flat surface or level)</term>, and as much as it is flared out at the top, it is narrowed by that same amount at the bottom plane.

Afterward, one does the opposite / Now that the cube has been distorted in these two ways / the first being wide at the top and narrow at the bottom / and conversely narrow at the top and wide at the bottom / I then redraw the straight lines of the previously described head through the changed grid, along with the contour lines of the face / In this way, two types of faces are produced / the first head becomes large at the top and small at the bottom / but the other head becomes small at the top and large at the bottom / This method can be applied to the entire body.

And just as the distortion is applied to the top and bottom of the cube / one can also apply it to the front and back; for if the cube is widened at its front plane, it is narrowed by the same amount at its back plane / The same follows if one does the opposite.

<summary>This page explains Albrecht Dürer's method for distorting human facial proportions by mathematically manipulating the underlying grid (the "cube"), allowing an artist to create varied head shapes by widening or narrowing the top, bottom, front, or back of the grid.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, geometry, facial anatomy, grid system, artistic theory, Renaissance, transformation</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of Albrecht Dürer’s geometric transformations. While the previous page discussed widening or narrowing the grid at the top, bottom, front, or back, this section explores how these distortions affect the profile and volume of the head, and introduces skewed, rhombus-like transformations.</meta>

And then, when the straight lines together with the shape of the face are proportionally drawn back in through the <term>divider (a tool or method for transferring proportions)</term>, the face in the first <term>cube (the geometric grid)</term> becomes large in the front and small in the back; and conversely, in the other cube, the face becomes small in the front and the head large in the back, just as I have also drawn below.

One may also, in such a <term>distortion (transformation)</term>, leave the <term>plane (the flat surface)</term> of the cube level at the top or bottom, rather than skewed <note>original: "vber ort," meaning diagonally or across the corners</note>, as is also shown in the drawing below; and such methods can be put to many different uses.

Another distortion may be used with the cube by setting the two side faces into the shape of a <term>rhombus (a diamond shape)</term>; this occurs when two opposite corners are made narrow, while the other two opposite corners are made wide. From this, you can first place one of the two narrow corners at the front by the crown of the head, and the other at the back by the neck; this leaves a wide corner at the back of the head and the other at the front by the chin. One then does the opposite as well, as is also drawn below. The first head acquires a pointed forehead, but the other acquires a high head in the back. These heads do not retain the same <term>volume (internal capacity)</term> as the head first described in the first book.

Every object drawn within a cube is distorted as often as the cube itself is distorted, provided that everything drawn inside it is proportionally redistributed by the one performing the transformation or by using the <term>divider (proportional transfer tool)</term>.

The top-left diagram shows a side profile of a head inside a grid that tapers toward the face. The top-right shows a frontal view of a head in a grid that tapers slightly toward the top. He emphasizes that any drawing within the grid will transform proportionally along with the grid itself.</summary>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer’s technical explanation of how to manipulate the "described head" (his idealized mathematical model of a face) to create a variety of different facial types using a grid system.</meta>

These next distorted faces <term>angesicht</term> are changed together with the entire cube <term>cubo; a three-dimensional block used as a geometric frame</term>; yet everything is comparably redistributed and yet changed through the transformer <note>original: *verkerer*; a geometric tool or scale Dürer designed to shift coordinates from a standard grid to a distorted one</note> or selector from the head first described. Therefore, I will proceed further with the first described face, though leaving its body <note>original: *corpus*</note> as it was before. However, I wish to change the division of the features in the face by shifting the horizontal <term>zwerch linien</term>, vertical <term>auffrechten linien</term>, and diagonal lines. By this, I aim to show that one may use the previously mentioned distorted cubes for faces and demonstrate how many varied shapes of faces may be made. Such ways and means are infinitely many; therefore, different shapes of people may be made always and as often as one desires.

Now we wish to take up again the first described face of the first book and treat it from the side using horizontal lines, through which all things are distinguished according to their length. Chiefly, there are three of these lines that distinguish the largest parts. Therefore, it should be noted that the top line, **k**, first touches the top of the forehead; the second line, **l**, goes through the eyebrows and touches the ears; and likewise, line **m** shows the end of the nose. 

Now, if you move these lines up or down, away from or toward each other, they create high or low fields between them. For whatever the horizontal lines take away from one field, they give to another. Through such shifting, you may make whichever parts of the face you wish shorter or longer. And just as you may shift these three previously determined lines—**k**, **l**, **m**—back and forth, you may also shift the other horizontal lines between them—**s**, **r**, **q**, **p**, **o**—so that they remain straight lines. Alternatively, you may shift them slightly diagonally <note>original: *vber ort*</note>, slanted, or crooked, and draw them in opposition to one another; through this, the fields between them become wide in one part and narrow in another. 

In this way, you can shift everything that can make a feature long, short, wide, narrow, thick, or thin, equal or unequal, according to your will. For every single horizontal line can be shifted in three specific ways: upward, downward, or diagonally. Also, all horizontal lines may be shifted upward or downward at once, or half of them upward and the other half downward, or however one happens to wish them moved. The same is likewise to be done with the vertical lines. 

However, whatever is changed and is to be drawn comparably from the first face into a distorted one must be done through the transformer or selector. Therefore, whoever searches in these matters will find rare and wonderful things. And according to the shape you wish your intended face to have, you must direct the straight lines that are to provide such a change.

For as was said before, if the horizontal lines are moved close together, whatever is made within them will become short; but if they are moved far apart, whatever is made between them will become long. But such things also occur between the vertical lines...

<summary>Albrecht Dürer explains a geometric method for distorting a standard facial model by shifting the lines of a coordinate grid. By moving horizontal and vertical lines closer together or further apart, an artist can mathematically generate an infinite variety of facial proportions and character types.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, human proportion, Albrecht Dürer, grid distortion, facial features, Renaissance art theory, transformer, technical drawing</keywords>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s instructions on altering the proportions of the human head by manipulating a geometric grid. He specifically focuses on how shifting horizontal lines affects the length of the face and the height of the cranium.</meta>

...the lines of the side profile, thick and thin. Therefore, whoever takes these things in hand to use them will well perceive what variety <note>original: *frembdicheyt*; literally "strangeness" or "foreignness," here referring to the diverse range of unique facial types possible</note> it is possible for him to create.

Now I will put part of what was mentioned above into practice. Take the first described man's head from the first book; leave all the vertical lines <term>auffrecht linien</term> as they are and work first with the horizontal lines <term>zwerch linien</term>. Shift the three lines **k**, **l**, **m** between **i** and **n** so that they create four equal fields, within which the largest divisions of the face are enclosed. Afterward, using the divider <term>weiler; a proportional scale or divider tool Dürer designed to redistribute measurements proportionally</term>, one redistributes the other lines **o**, **l**, **p**, **q**, **m**, **r**, **f** accordingly. This seems to me to create a good figure, because the shape of the face is thereby divided equally. Therefore, I have sketched such a shifted face with its lines from the side and front below for you to follow.

*Two woodcut diagrams illustrating facial proportions. The left diagram shows a profile view of a head facing right, overlaid with a grid labeled 'b h g f e d c a' at the top. The right diagram shows a frontal view of a head with a symmetrical grid labeled 'b k h f e d e f h k b'. Between the two heads is a vertical lettered scale: 'J, k, i, l, p, q, m, r, f, n'.*

Furthermore, it should be noted that heads sometimes have long or short faces; this is to be understood as follows: if the face in the head is very long, then the head, as far as it is covered with hair above the forehead, becomes very low. Therefore, I will set such a long face so that line **k** is placed upon line **i**, and afterward, using the divider <term>weiler</term>, the other lines **o**, **l**, **p**, **q**, **m**, **r**, **f** shall be redistributed proportionally between **k** and **n**. In this way, the hair growth <term>harwachsung; the area of the scalp where hair begins</term> at the crown is entirely removed.

If, however, it is intended to make the face short, that same head gains a high, pointed crown. Therefore, next to the previously made head with the long face, I will set and sketch one with a short face in its form, as I shall describe. Take the forehead line **k**, shift it down one-third between **i** and **n**, and afterward redistribute the other horizontal lines **o**, **l**, **p**, **q**, **m**, **r**, **f** proportionally using the divider <term>weiler</term>, just as it is set before your eyes.

*Two woodcut profile diagrams of human heads facing right, showing variations in proportion. The left head has a long face and a low forehead, while the right head has a short face and a tall, pointed cranium. Both are overlaid with grids and labeled 'b h g f e d c a' at the top. A vertical scale 'i, f, o, l, p, q, m, r, f, n' is positioned between the heads.*

<summary>Albrecht Dürer demonstrates how to mathematically adjust the proportions of a face to create "long" or "short" facial types by shifting horizontal grid lines. He explains that a longer face results in a flatter skull, while a shorter face results in a taller, more pointed crown.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, facial proportions, geometric grid, profile, anatomy, Renaissance art theory, woodcut diagrams, weiler</keywords>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s geometric exploration of facial distortion. Having previously discussed shifting horizontal lines within a standard rectangular grid, he now introduces the concept of distorting the grid itself into trapezoidal shapes (the "geviert corpus") to create vertically or horizontally skewed facial proportions.</meta>

Another such variation is to make the **square frame** <term>geviert corpus; the four-sided geometric grid used as a coordinate system for the head</term> in which the head is described narrow in the front and wide in the back; whatever is taken away from the front is added to the back.

One does the same in the opposite way. If one then redraws everything proportionally within this distorted frame <note>original: *verkert geviert corpus*</note> as is proper, two different faces are produced, just as I have sketched both in the two diagrams printed below.

*Two sets of technical diagrams illustrating the proportional distortion of a human head. The top set consists of a trapezoidal frame on the left that is shorter on its left vertical edge than its right. To the right, a profile of a human head is mapped onto a corresponding distorted grid; because the grid expands toward the back, the rear of the head appears vertically elongated while the face is compressed. The bottom set consists of a trapezoidal frame on the left that is taller on its left vertical edge than its right. To the right, a profile of a human head is mapped onto a corresponding distorted grid; because the grid tapers toward the back, the face appears vertically elongated while the back of the head is compressed.*

Now, note further that all the **horizontal lines** <term>zwerch linien</term> of the head are to be shifted using the **proportional divider** <term>fellscher; a specialized scale or tool Dürer designed to redistribute measurements</term> which will be described hereafter. This is done so that the fields between them lengthen upward and become shorter toward the bottom. One can also do the same in reverse, or apply it to just half of the head—either the top or the bottom—or between whichever horizontal lines one chooses.

This produces great variation by shifting the lines in reverse <note>original: *im widerwerdigen*</note>. For instance, you might now shift a line downward that you previously shifted upward. This should also be observed with the **vertical lines** <term>auffrecht linien</term>, shifting them forward or backward. Therefore, I will describe several more faces to show how the methods mentioned above should be put into practice. In doing so, I hope to provide the inspiration <note>original: *vrsach*; literally "cause" or "reason," here meaning the starting point for further creative exploration</note> for much deeper thought on what else can be created beyond what is shown here. I will, however, employ several such shifts in the examples that follow.

<summary>Dürer explains how to transform facial features by distorting the square grid into trapezoids and by using a specialized proportional tool to shift horizontal and vertical lines.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, facial proportions, geometric distortion, grid systems, proportional dividers, Renaissance art theory, physiognomy</keywords>

--- Page 207 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s instructions on facial deformation. Having previously discussed shifting the entire frame of the head, he now focuses on the internal horizontal divisions of the face to alter specific features like the forehead, nose, and chin.</meta>

First, it is our intention that we wish to completely shift the face described earlier using the **horizontal lines** <term>zwerch linien; lines running across the face to mark proportions</term> so that nothing remains in its original place. Whether we want to make a long or short forehead, a long or short nose, or a long or short chin. If it happens that you shift the horizontal line **l** far upward toward line **f**, then a short forehead will be created between them, and the nose will lengthen between **l** and **m**. However, if you shift line **l** downward toward line **m**, you will get a long forehead between **f** and **l** and a short nose between **l** and **m**. But if you leave line **l** standing and shift the horizontal line **m** upward, a short nose will again be created between **l** and **m**, but the mouth and chin will become long. If line **m** is shifted downward, there will be a long nose between **l** and **m** and a short mouth and chin. 

Now, the two lines **k** and **m** can be shifted far away from each other, so that line **k** is near line **i** and line **m** is near line **n**; then the height of the head and the length of the mouth along with the chin remain short, but the forehead and nose become very long. But when you shift the two horizontal lines **k** and **m** close together, the height of the head becomes long, as do the mouth and chin, but the forehead and nose become very short. If you shift **f** and **l** high upward, you will get a short head and a short forehead. Afterward, divide the other horizontal lines between **l** and **n** equally again using the **distorter** <term>verkerer; a proportional scale or divider used to redistribute measurements</term>, and the result will be a long nose, mouth, and chin. You may do the exact opposite of this, and you will get a high head and long forehead, and a snub <note>original: "kuß" (kurz); a short or snub nose</note> nose, mouth, and chin. 

But for another shifting: move line **k** near line **i**, and move line **m** near line **l**, and let line **l** stand; from this results a low head, a long forehead, a short nose, and a long lip and chin. Do the opposite of this: move line **k** near line **l**, and move line **m** near to line **n**; thus results a high head, a short forehead, a long nose, and a short mouth and chin. In such matters, there would be much to point out that is omitted here for the sake of brevity. Also, in this "giving and taking" <note>the trade-off of proportions</note>, it should be noted that one should shift the lines in such a way that one does not force nature too much, so that it remains recognizably human. 

Whoever else wishes to put such things into practice: just as you have dealt with the three lines **f, k, l, m**, so you may do with the other horizontal lines **o, p, q, r, s, f**. Now, since it is divided according to our will to make things long and short through the horizontal lines as we intended, and the things become different from the first intended **square frame** <term>vierung; the basic geometric grid</term> (though still in the manner of straight lines), one may also pull these same points upward at the front and downward at the back, and vice versa; or leave some parts straight and make others curved. One may also shift the previously described **diagonal lines** <term>ort-lini</term> of the first head by the nose however one wishes; one may also "break" the straight lines upward or downward, or in the vertical lines, forward or backward; the same is done with the diagonal lines. In some parts, you may turn the lines wherever you wish; you may also slide the fields between the horizontal lines—in whichever parts they are broken—forward or backward, upward or downward. Everyone will find in practice what I am talking about.

So that you understand what I call a **broken line** <term>gebrochne lini</term>, mark my words: Let a given line be **a–b**. Place a point **c** anywhere you wish; there, break it apart. Shift one half upward or downward. Or, place two points on the aforementioned line, such as **c** and **d**; break that same piece **c–d** out, upward or downward. Likewise, one can break vertical lines forward or backward, and also diagonal lines. Therefore, I will say how one shall use the broken diagonal line in the changing of the face. However, look below at the figure where the broken line is drawn out, of which I spoke above, and afterward, the recently described distorted faces are drawn out.

<summary>Dürer explains how to manipulate the internal horizontal lines of a facial grid to alter individual features like the forehead, nose, and chin while warning the artist to maintain "human" proportions. He introduces the concept of 'broken lines,' where segments of the grid are disconnected and shifted to create complex facial distortions.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, facial proportions, horizontal lines, broken lines, geometric distortion, forehead, nose, chin, Renaissance art theory, verkerer</keywords>

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s geometric manual on facial variation. Having previously discussed how to shift horizontal guide lines up and down to change the height of features, he now introduces more complex distortions: "breaking" lines at angles, curving them, and shifting them diagonally (across the corner) to create a vast array of unique facial structures.</meta>

<sig>C ij</sig>

And as was previously shown in the first described section of the first booklet, where the <term>horizontal and vertical lines<note>German: *zwerch un̄ auffrechten linien*. These form the basic grid for Dürer’s facial proportions.</note></term> are drawn in a straight manner; these lines are almost entirely transformed when one "breaks" them or turns them into diagonal lines. Therefore, the two terms used—**broken** and **across the corner**—must always be considered whenever they are needed. Now, I will first use the term **across the corner** <note>German: *überort*. This refers to skewed or diagonal shifts that distort the square grid into a diamond or trapezoid shape.</note> for all horizontal and vertical lines: I either push each horizontal line upward or downward at the back or the front. Similarly, I push the vertical lines either forward or backward at the top or the bottom. Through this, however, they all remain straight.

Because of this, you must curve each of the aforementioned lines individually into an arc or a "snake-like" fashion, either upward or downward, forward or backward, and bend each line wherever it is necessary for your use. We shall begin with the **forehead line**, drawing it upward or downward at the back or the front. Likewise, we "break" the line of the **eyebrows** in their place for as long as the eyebrows are, drawing them either upward or downward at both ends, or broken in the middle above the eyes and drawn upward or downward. For you may curve the eyes upward or downward according to each individual case; the same is to be done with the **corners of the eyes**. If the corner is broken away from the line's length, you may turn the front or back corners upward or downward, just as the <term>indicator needle<note>German: *zünglein*. Literally "little tongue," referring to the pivoting needle of a magnetic compass.</note></term> is turned in a compass. You may also make the inner or outer corners of the eyes deeper or shallower.

Likewise, if the **nose line** is broken, it may be drawn upward or downward at its back "tips" <note>the nostrils</note>. The same is to be done with the **mouth**, and similarly with the **jawbone**, which is drawn upward or downward at the back. It is also very distinct to see from the side how the neck is placed forward on the head near the chin; and conversely, if shifted "across the corner" far behind the chin, the head becomes almost upright, whereas the former bows toward the earth. We still have the **ears** to shift within their square: upward, downward, forward, backward, or diagonally, on whichever side you wish. You may also make the square of the ear long, short, wide, or narrow, large or small. Some ears lie flat against the head, while others protrude far from it. 

From such instructions, all straight things may be drawn "across the corner," curved, or angled. From this, you will find how you can make all heights lower, thicknesses thinner, and flat things slanted. If the shape of the face is then drawn back in between such pre-determined altered lines, the appearance of the previous shape is entirely transformed. In all of this, one must take very precise note of the terms of distinction described at the beginning. For from these, one finds that three types of facial distinctions are to be seen from the side: namely, a **slanted, flat face**; one **curved outward** like the heads of hares; and one **curved inward** <note>concave</note>, for which "taking and giving" <note>Dürer’s method of adding or subtracting from a standard measurement</note> is used here. These three types of faces can be changed in countless ways; one may also make these three kinds of faces sharp and highly prominent or flat. To demonstrate such writings of mine, I have sketched my meaning of these three faces below.

<image-desc>Large decorative calligraphic flourish consisting of multiple interlocking loops and fine line work.</image-desc>

<summary>Dürer explains how to manipulate the geometric grid of the face by "breaking" lines at angles or curving them to create diverse facial features, including convex and concave profiles. He compares the movement of facial features to the pivoting of a compass needle, emphasizing the infinite variety possible through these distortions.</summary>
<keywords>facial proportions, geometry, broken lines, diagonal shift, convex profile, concave profile, artistic theory, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 211 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s explanation of how to manipulate the facial grid. He moves from simple vertical shifts to "breaking" and skewing lines to create more complex distortions of the human profile.</meta>

<sig>C ij</sig>

As was previously shown in the first part of the first book, where the <term>horizontal and vertical lines<note>German: *zwerch un̄ auffrechten linien*. These form the basic grid for Dürer’s facial proportions.</note></term> are drawn perfectly straight; these are now almost entirely transformed when one "breaks" them or sets them **across the corner**. Through this, many different types of faces are created.

Therefore, the two terms used here—**broken** and **across the corner**<note>German: *gebrochen und uberort*. "Broken" refers to bending a line into an angle, while "across the corner" refers to a diagonal or skewed shift of the grid lines.</note>—must always be considered whenever they are needed.

Now, I will first use the term **across the corner** for all horizontal and vertical lines: I either push each horizontal line upward or downward at the back or the front. Similarly, I push the vertical lines either at the top or the bottom, forward or backward. Through this, however, they all remain straight.

Because of this, you must bend each of the previously described lines individually into an arc or a "snake-like" fashion, whether it be upward or downward, forward or backward, and bend each line wherever it is necessary for your use.

We shall begin with the **forehead line**, drawing it upward or downward at the back or the front. Similarly, we "break" the line of the **eyebrows** in their place for as long as the eyebrows are, drawing them either upward or downward at both ends, or broken in the middle above the eyes and drawn upward or downward. For you may curve the eyes upward or downward according to each individual case; the same is to be done with the no... <note>Text cuts off at "na...", likely "nase" (nose).</note>

<summary>Dürer explains how to create diverse facial types by distorting the standard geometric grid, specifically by bending ("breaking") lines or shifting them diagonally ("across the corner").</summary>
<keywords>facial proportions, geometry, grid transformation, artistic anatomy, Renaissance drawing techniques</keywords>

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s instructions on how to systematically distort the human profile by shifting the lines of his geometric grid. He moves from discussing horizontal shifts to explaining how vertical lines and curved paths create different physical characteristics.</meta>

<sig>D iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: follows the</meta>

In these **shifted faces**, all **vertical and horizontal lines**<note>German: *auffrecht und zwerch linien*. These are the foundational grid lines in Dürer's system of proportion.</note> may also be moved, as is permitted in the others. Furthermore, one requires instruction on how to handle the vertical lines to differentiate the shapes of the faces. Since the two sides of the **square**<note>German: *vierung*. This refers to the geometric box enclosing the head in profile.</note> of the side-view face are the front vertical **.a.** and the back vertical **.b.**, and the six other lines **.c.d.e.f.g.h.** in between divide it into seven equal fields: these may be moved according to anyone's will, just as easily as the horizontal lines. Each individual field between the vertical lines may be shifted specifically backward or forward, and likewise, several more or all of them together.

And in the same way as was previously shown with the horizontal lines to make things long and short, so it is with the vertical lines to make things **thick, thin, broad, or narrow**; here there is nothing to do but give and take. And as I do with straight lines, so one also does with **curved, slanted, and diagonal lines**<note>German: *krumen, schlemen vnd ort linien*. These are the non-straight lines used to define the contours of the face.</note>. And if one wishes to use such things, one must pay attention. First, regarding the **forehead**, whatever one takes from its **projection**<note>German: *ausladung*. This refers to how far the forehead sticks out.</note> must be added to the height of the nose. This is also done in reverse. This may happen through lines that are bent outward or inward, or straight broken lines; thus it is to be done in all parts throughout the entire face as seen from the side.

Therefore, opposing things in the transformation must always be placed together. This happens from what was said above: if you push one part forward, you then push the other part above or below it backward; this then makes a part thick or broad, thin or narrow.

Now, if the change in the whole head is made between the horizontal lines by the vertical, diagonal, or broken lines, one must note that in every specifically small part of the face, the words **flat** or **curved outward or inward** are to be used, just as well in the individual parts as in the three different faces. From this, it follows that you may make a plain, outward-curving, or inward-curving forehead; the same is to be done with the **nose, lips, mouth, and chin**, and in all other things.

It should be noted that the line through which the roundness of the head is made, as seen from the side, may be led in many ways; for one person has a **flat head**, another a **pointed** one, the third an **angular** one, and so forth in many varieties, just as nature provides many kinds.

Now we wish to speak further of the **diagonal line**<note>German: *ort lini*. Literally "corner line," likely referring to the slope of the facial profile.</note> that is drawn for the sake of the nose; it may be leaned backward as far as the vertical line **.d.**, or it may be drawn vertically in the middle at **.a.c.**, or it may be leaned forward at the top to the vertical **.a.** But in such things, I consider the **proportionate** things to be the most beautiful; although the other deviant things bring wonder, they are not all lovely. Therefore, an example is to be taken from the aforementioned indications, so that one may say no pointed head, nor any flat one, is **well-formed**, but a **round head** is considered **pretty**, because it is a middle ground between the others. By this, it is not proven that every middle ground between all things is the best; I only take this to be used in certain things. As when one says, "that is a face too long or too short," and likewise in its parts: "that is a forehead too long, short, **bulging**<note>German: *beulette*.</note>, or **pitted**<note>German: *grubete*.</note>." Thus, one must also deal with the nose; for some have large, **hooked**, long, and overhanging noses. On the other hand, some have entirely short or **snub**<note>German: *murret*. Referring to a blunt or upturned nose shape.</note> noses, upturned, or thick.

<summary>Dürer explains how to use a grid of vertical, horizontal, and diagonal lines to manipulate facial features in profile, defining characteristics like the curvature of the forehead and the shape of the nose. He advocates for "middle" proportions (like a round head) as the standard for beauty, while noting that extreme variations exist in nature.</summary>
<keywords>facial proportion, profile drawing, geometry in art, facial distortion, nose shapes, forehead curvature, aesthetic theory, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s geometric exploration of the human face. Having previously discussed how to adjust the height and width of facial features by shifting horizontal and vertical lines, he now introduces "rhomboid" distortions to create faces that appear to lean forward or backward.</meta>

->A level face.<- ->An outward-curved face.<- ->And an inward-curved face.<-

<image-desc>Three profile drawings of human heads, each inscribed within a square grid of 4x4 units. The first shows a standard profile ("A level face"), the second a profile with more convex features ("An outward-curved face"), and the third, partially visible, a profile with more concave features ("And an inward-curved face"). The grids help define the proportions of the nose, eyes, ears, and chin.</image-desc>

There are two more types of faces to be viewed from the side, which are to be shifted in their **square grid**<note>German: *vierung*. Dürer uses this term for the square frame that organizes the proportions of the head.</note> in this manner. The two **horizontal lines**<note>German: *zwerch linien*.</note> of the grid, at the bottom and the top, remain straight horizontal lines in both squares and stay **parallel**<note>German: *parnn*.</note> to one another. However, both squares must be shifted into a **rhombus shape**<note>German: *rautens weyß*. Literally "diamond-wise."</note>; understand it this way: The first square is pulled forward at its front upper corner and backward at its lower back corner. One does the same in reverse for the other. Afterward, one re-divides all the previously described sections using the straight horizontal lines. But when one re-divides the **vertical lines**<note>German: *auffrechtenn [linien]*.</note>, then these, along with both sides, all become **diagonal lines**<note>German: *ort lini*. A term Dürer uses for slanted or diagonal lines that create a tilt.</note>, which in the first version lean forward and in the other lean backward. Finally, one draws the contour lines of the face into this grid; then you will see in both squares what the result is, just as I have **sketched**<note>German: *auffgerissen*. A technical term for architectural or proportional drafting.</note> these faces immediately following the three previously mentioned faces.

->A forward-leaning face.<- ->A backward-leaning face.<-

<image-desc>Two profile drawings of human heads inscribed within skewed, rhomboid grids. The left diagram ("A forward-leaning face") shows the head leaning forward due to the grid's forward tilt. The right diagram ("A backward-leaning face") shows the head leaning backward due to the grid's backward tilt. A decorative calligraphic flourish appears in the bottom right corner.</image-desc>

<summary>Dürer demonstrates how to tilt a facial profile forward or backward by distorting the standard square proportion grid into a rhombus. This method allows an artist to systematically alter the posture of a head while maintaining consistent internal proportions.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, geometry, grid distortion, profile drawing, Albrecht Dürer, Renaissance art theory, rhombus, diagonal lines</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on geometric distortions of the face. After explaining how shifting the horizontal and vertical lines into rhomboid shapes alters the overall tilt of the profile, Dürer now details how individual sections of the grid can be manipulated to change specific features like the forehead, nose, and chin.</meta>

<sig>N iij</sig>

In these shifted views, all vertical and horizontal lines may also be displaced, just as was permitted in the others. Furthermore, one needs instruction on how to work with the **vertical lines**<note>German: *auffrechten linien*.</note> to differentiate the various shapes of the face. Since the two sides of the **square grid**<note>German: *vierung*. This refers to the geometric frame used to measure the profile.</note> of the side view are the front vertical line **.a.** and the back vertical line **.b.**, and the six other lines between them—**.c. d. e. f. g. h.**—divide the area into seven equal fields, these may be shifted according to anyone's will, just as with the horizontal lines. Each individual field between the vertical lines may be pushed specifically backward or forward, or several of them, or even all together.

And in the same way as was shown before regarding how to make things long and short with horizontal lines, so one can use the vertical lines to make features thick, thin, wide, or narrow; here, one may "give and take" from the fields. And just as I do with straight lines, so one also does with curved, slanted, and **diagonal lines**<note>German: *ortlinien*. Dürer uses this term for lines that are neither perfectly vertical nor horizontal, often used to create a tilt or slope.</note>. If one wishes to use these, one must pay attention. First, regarding the forehead: whatever one takes away from its outward projection must be added to the height of the nose. One also does this in reverse. This may be done through outward or inward curves, or straight **broken lines**<note>German: *gebrochen linien*. These are lines made of straight segments meeting at angles, rather than a smooth curve.</note>. This is to be done in all parts throughout the entire face when viewed from the side.

Therefore, contradictory things must always be placed together in these transformations. This happens as stated above: if you push one part forward, you then push the other part above or below it backward; this then makes a part thick or wide, thin or narrow.

Now, if the change in the whole head is made between the horizontal lines by using vertical, top-slanting, or broken lines, one must also note that in every individual small part of the face, the terms "level," "outward-curved," or "inward-curved" are to be used. This applies to the parts just as it does to the three different types of faces. From this, it follows that you may make a simple outward or inward curved forehead; the same is to be done with the nose, **lips**<note>German: *lebsē*. An archaic term for the lips.</note>, mouth, and chin, and in all other things.

It should be noted that the line used to create the roundness of the head, when seen from the side, may be drawn in many ways. For one person has a flat head, another a pointed one, the third an angular one, and so on in many varieties, just as nature provides great variety.

Now we will speak further of the **diagonal line**<note>German: *ort lini*.</note> that is drawn because of the nose. It may be slanted backward as far as the top of vertical line **.d.**; it may also be drawn vertically in the middle between **.a.** and **.c.**; it may also be slanted forward toward the top of vertical line **.a.** But in such things, I consider the **proportional**<note>German: *vergeychlichen*. Meaning balanced or harmonious.</note> things to be the most beautiful. Although the other extreme things bring wonder, they are not all lovely. Therefore, an example should be taken from the aforementioned instructions: one might say that neither a pointed head nor a flat one is well-formed, but a round head is handsome. This demonstrates that a middle path between all things is the best. However, I take this up only to be used in certain instances, such as when one says: "that is a face too long or too short," and similarly for its parts, such as a forehead that is too long, short, **bulging**<note>German: *beulette*.</note>, or **pitted**<note>German: *grubete*.</note>. The same applies to the nose; for some have large, hooked, long, and overhanging noses. Conversely, some have very short or **snub**<note>German: *murret*.</note> noses, or those that are upturned, thick, or **bulbous**<note>German: *kolbet*.</note>...

<summary>Dürer explains how to manipulate the vertical and diagonal lines of a facial grid to create specific physical characteristics, emphasizing that while nature offers many extremes, beauty is found in balanced proportions.</summary>
<keywords>facial proportions, geometry in art, profile drawing, aesthetic theory, nose shapes, forehead shapes, Albrecht Dürer, grid systems</keywords>
