<meta>This page continues Dürer’s detailed exploration of how to manipulate the facial features within his geometric grid system. While the previous page focused on shifting the vertical fields of the grid to change the width of the head, this page discusses specific variations of eyes, lips, and chins, and introduces the concept of "stepped" or staggered profiles. It concludes with Dürer’s famous artistic philosophy: that an artist must understand deformity to truly recognize beauty.</meta>

...where between the eyes they are pressed deep in, or they are high and protruding, level with the
forehead. Furthermore, some have deep, small little eyes, or high, large, bulging eyes; some
open their eyes narrowly like a pig, and sometimes pull their lower lid further up
than the upper lid down. Also, there are some who pull their eyes as wide as a circle, so that
one sees the entire eyeball. Then there are some whose eyebrows are raised high above the
eyes; for others, they lie right upon them or hang over them; and some eyebrows
are thin, others thick.

Furthermore, some have thick, high, club-like mouths, or thin lips that look bitten-in; and for
some, the upper lip extends beyond the lower, and vice versa; and often one lip is thicker than the
other. Some have a long lip from the nose, others a short one. So, too, many a man
has a thick, broad, large chin; another, a small pointed one; for some, they are nearly recessed and
sloped away toward the neck; for others, they project forward from the neck; and some are long,
others short, as was mentioned above, which the **horizontal lines**<note>German: *zwerch linien*.</note> provide.

Further, there are some "stepped" faces; that is to say, they are furthest forward
at the top. Thereafter, the further down they go, the further back they recede. It is also the opposite:
at the bottom they are furthest forward, and the further up they go, the further back they recede.
For this, one should use the **broken lines**<note>German: *gebrochen linien*. These are lines that change direction at an angle rather than being a single straight stroke.</note>; much could be written of these things—how one
should use them in even the smallest parts—yet a diligent seeker will soon find this out for himself.

It is also permitted here to make more or fewer of the previously described **vertical**, **horizontal**, and
**diagonal lines**<note>German: *auffrechten, zwerchen vnnd ort linien*. These form the coordinate system for Dürer’s proportional drawings.</note>, either for a more precise search or for
less effort. It is also permitted to bend the straight lines as needed,
however one wishes. And so that I may be clearly understood, I will here **draft**<note>German: *auffreyssen*. In this context, it means to construct or draw according to a geometric plan.</note> several of the above-described
heads, with their prepared vertical, horizontal, diagonal, and broken lines—
including some curved ones—and then draw the shape of the face back into them,
to instruct those who might not understand the drafting of all the previously described faces,
which have also been altered once. In many ways, one can transfer these from one
into the other and mix them all together—from the last through all of them back into
the first, as one wishes—and deal with them as strangely as one can imagine. For all things
are constantly to be transformed. And this should not be forgotten: whatever is taken from the
thickness of a face in profile must be added by the prudent artist to the width,
and this is also to be used in reverse. Furthermore, note this instruction: all vertical and
horizontal lines in the **square grid**<note>German: *vierung*. The bounding box of the profile view.</note> of the side-view face, I will shift quite arbitrarily
and willfully across the diagonals, and then draw the contour lines of the face back
into it. Whatever results from this—as seen here at the end in its figure—anyone else
may do differently as he pleases.

But such a described method serves more for the sake of **variety**<note>German: *vnderscheid*. Dürer is emphasizing how to create different types of people rather than a single ideal.</note> than for the form of
**beauty**<note>German: *hübsche*.</note>; yet one must know such things and the like, so that one may learn many things from
much experience. For no one will well know what constitutes a good form unless he
first knows what constitutes a **deformity**<note>German: *vngestalt*. Literally "un-form" or "ill-shape." Dürer believes beauty is found by understanding the extremes of deviation.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of facial proportions, moving from static side profiles to the geometric manipulation of frontal views through coordinate grids.</meta>

Furthermore, I will demonstrate how a forward-facing head may be altered and <term>distorted<note>original: "verkern", meaning to transform, reverse, or distort from a standard proportion.</note></term>. First, one may transfer the modified horizontal lines from all distorted side profiles into the <term>grid square<note>original: "vierung", referring to the square coordinate system or quadrate grid used for mapping proportions.</note></term> of the frontal head. For this reason, I will not say much more about the horizontal lines in the frontal head, except to note that one may draw them diagonally—some or all of them as one pleases—because such methods result in crooked faces, just as if one side were set lower than the other. Therefore, I intend to deal solely with the <term>vertical lines<note>original: "auffrechten linien".</note></term>.

Now observe that between the two sides of the facial grid, **a** and **b**, there are other vertical lines—such as **i**, **g**, **c**, **d**, **e**, **f**, **h**, and **k**—which encompass and distinguish the primary features of the face according to their <term>width divisions<note>original: "breiten teylen".</note></term>; these include the forehead, eyes, nose, ears, mouth, chin, and other parts.

It should be noted that each of these vertical lines in the aforementioned <term>grid<note>original: "nez" (net), a network of lines.</note></term> can be shifted individually or all together. Wherever you move two lines further apart, the features between them become wider; but at the ends where they run close together, they become...

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how to manipulate the vertical lines of a facial grid to create various types of physiognomy and facial expressions.</meta>

the parts between narrow, whether at the top or the bottom. These straight lines may also be drawn in a bowed fashion. By following this aforementioned method, no line remains vertical; instead, they slant outward or inward. From such a shifting of the aforementioned lines—within which the form of the face is then drawn anew—you will find that there result wide or narrow foreheads; likewise, eyes that are long or short horizontally, set close together or far apart. One may make one feature larger than the other, with larger or smaller <term>eyelids<note>original: "aug glidern", literally "eye-limbs" or the components of the eye area.</note></term>, and set them unevenly, one higher than the other; the same applies to the eyebrows.

There will also be wide or narrow noses—whether crooked or straight at the top or bottom, bulbous or pointed, angular or round—wide at the back by the nostrils and narrow at the front, and conversely, large and wide or small and narrow nostrils. Likewise, the mouth, chin, and cheeks may be wide or narrow; thus some parts become wide at the top and narrow at the bottom, and vice versa.

Through such unequal divisions, <term>bowed<note>original: "büglette", referring to a curved, arched, or humped shape.</note></term> and crooked faces are created, along with other oddities—such as narrow or wide mouths; crooked, thick, large, or small lips; or jawbones of unequal width or narrowness, pointed or blunt, whole or <term>cleft<note>original: "gespalten", here likely referring to a dimpled or "split" chin.</note></term>. A diligent worker may thoroughly investigate these and similar things that belong to such variety; and from the aforementioned method, he may find many wonders that lie buried within this approach. 

And this is a particular truth: whoever understands and recognizes what makes a thing ugly and <term>clumsy<note>original: "vngeschickt", meaning lacking in skill, grace, or proper proportion.</note></term> can thereby realize what he ought to avoid. The more <term>deformity<note>original: "vngestalt", meaning a lack of proper form or beauty.</note></term> is stripped away, the more loveliness and beauty remain. And whoever learns to understand these described things will see in every instance how the distortion is arranged; this is of great use to anyone who must create a recognizable likeness of a person, whether in a painting or in <term>sculpture<note>original: "erhabnen bilden", literally "raised images" or three-dimensional figures.</note></term>. However, for one who practices such work but does not understand these things, it is certainly impossible for him to make his work correctly, unless he should happen upon it by <term>accident<note>original: "vngefert", meaning by chance or unintentionally.</note></term>.

Furthermore, a head on a long, thin neck appears different than on a thick, short neck. Note also that some people have very large <term>features<note>original: "gebrech", here used to describe the prominent components of the face rather than "defects" in the modern sense.</note></term> in their faces—that is, the eyes, nose, mouth, and chin fill the space well—while others have small features among these aforementioned things.

Furthermore, every rendered head may be altered and made unrecognizable by the hair—whether it be shorn, curly or straight, thick or thin, long or short, locked or combed, dry or wet. The same applies to the beard or lack thereof, using all the same variations as with the hair.

Based on these aforementioned methods, I will sketch several faces. Every woman’s head can be <term>distorted<note>original: "zuuerkern", to transform or deviate from the standard proportion.</note></term> just like a man’s, provided that the female character remains. With such distortion as mentioned above, one may proceed through the entire body and investigate all the limbs.

And yet, when I speak of what is female or male in nature, it must be understood as follows: one should not alter a man or a woman so much that the male nature is no longer recognizable in the man, nor the female nature in the woman. Beyond this, great differences may be made. To this end, one should take the example that all humans or other animals are similar to one another within their species; nonetheless, one can usually distinguish the female from the male. Thus, in all things, woman and man must remain human within their differences. Among animals, one sees that a lion is never so deformed that one would mistake it for a donkey, nor is a fox mistaken for a wolf. Therefore, no kind of creature should be turned away from its natural order.

<summary>Albrecht Dürer explores how artists can systematically vary facial features and hair to create diverse characters, while cautioning that an artist must understand deformity to achieve beauty. He also emphasizes the importance of preserving the essential "nature" or species-identity of a subject despite these geometric distortions.</summary>
<keywords>facial proportions, physiognomy, Albrecht Dürer, artistic theory, deformity vs beauty, gender in art, anatomical distortion, natural order</keywords>

--- Page 219 ---
<meta>This page transitions from technical grid manipulation to the philosophical limits of artistic transformation. Dürer addresses physiognomy—the practice of comparing human faces to animals—and warns that while an artist can alter a face's proportions, they must preserve the fundamental nature of the subject.</meta>

But when it is said from time to time that a person looks <term>lion-like<note>original: "lebisch", derived from "Löwe" (lion).</note></term>, or like a bear, wolf, fox, or dog—even though he does not have four feet like such an animal—it does not follow that the animal's limbs are present. Rather, it is the person's outward appearance that, to our perception, indicates the <term>temperament<note>original: "gmüt", referring to the inner spirit, disposition, or character.</note></term> of such an animal in a human being. This comparison that we form in our minds does not affect the actual limbs; therefore, one species is not mixed into the other.

For this reason, the comparison of the differences I spoke of may partly be attributed to the <term>canine race<note>original: "hūntischen geschlecht", referring to the species or category of dogs.</note></term>. For the variation among them is exceptionally great: some are quite large, others very small; they differ more than a thousandfold in their <term>proportions<note>original: "proporzionen"</note></term> across every limb, having entirely distinct shapes. They are smooth-haired or completely shaggy and of many different colors. Indeed, they are more distinct from one another in their form than a wolf is from a fox; yet one always sees that they are neither wolves nor foxes.

From this it is obvious that the <term>transformation<note>original: "verkerūg", here referring to Dürer's method of "distorting" or "shifting" the coordinate grid to change facial features.</note></term> of form does not cause nature to turn one thing into another, for one can easily tell a dog and a wild beast apart. This itself is a specific distinction of form about which much could be said, but it is omitted here for the sake of brevity. Therefore, one thing should not become another in its <term>kind<note>original: "geschlecht", meaning its basic category or species.</note></term> if it is to remain distinct. This is said so that you may <term>alter<note>original: "verkern", to transform or vary through the grid method.</note></term> the man, yet in such a way that he does not become a woman or something else; and conversely with the woman as well.

--- Page 220 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for modifying human proportions. While the previous page discussed the philosophical implications of transformation—comparing human features to animal temperaments—this page moves into the practical geometric application of these changes using Dürer’s "twin-scale" method.</meta>

Following this, I will demonstrate another way by which one can measure anew—and change from what it was before—any previously described figure through the terms **thick, thin, broad,** or **narrow** using two types of lines. You may make it thicker, thinner, broader, or narrower. Through this, a thing is altered just as was previously described with the words **long** and **short**, but through a different application. 

This must be understood as follows: if the previously made figure is to remain at its first-described length, but become thicker, thinner, broader, or narrower on the horizontal lines and in between, then the line for the length of the figure is already measured. But for the thickness and breadth, you must have another line with which you wish to measure. To enlarge the figure, make this line longer; however, if you want the figure to be more slender, make this second line shorter. Then, use the <term>proportional transferer<note>original: "uerkerer", a geometric method or device Dürer uses to scale dimensions proportionally from one length to another.</note></term> to divide all the necessary parts proportionally back into your new line, and then measure the figure with it. In this way, it will be measured thicker and broader from the longer line, but thinner and narrower from the short line. For this purpose, I will draw for you below a special figure of these two lines, which I will call the <term>twin-scale<note>original: "zwiling", literally "twin." It refers to a right-angled diagram where two axes represent different scales of the same proportion.</note></term>, because two types of lines are used for one thing.

First, I set upright on one side the first divided line of the figure’s length, with **a** at the top and **b** at the bottom, which is already divided into its parts. At the bottom end **b**, I attach a horizontal line, shorter or longer according to my pleasure, the end of which shall be **c**. Thus, a <term>triangle<note>original: "triangel"</note></term> **a-b-c** is formed. Now, if this horizontal line is divided again according to the upright line itself—either by the divider described in the first booklet or by the <term>proportional transferer</term>—then the <term>twin-scale</term> is finished. You may then measure your figure with it and take all your parts from the horizontal line, whatever concerns **thick, thin, broad,** or **narrow**.

This <term>twin-scale</term> may also be used for the terms **long** and **short**. Thus, first, if the upright line **a-b** is lengthened and then divided back into itself by the <term>proportional transferer</term>, and the figure is then measured with it, the figure will become long and smaller in body according to its measure. Therefore, whichever line it may be—the upright or the horizontal—that is changed and used to measure the previously described figure, the figure will always be different. Thus, you understand that if the previously described figure which you wish to change is to remain at its length but become larger or smaller in body than it was before, you must use the transformed horizontal line from the <term>twin-scale</term>.

However, if you wish to let the figure remain at its previously described thickness and breadth, and only make it longer or shorter, then you may use the upright line as stated above. Thus you are informed that each of the two sides of the <term>twin-scale</term> is a separate divider, as was described in the first booklet; here, the terms **much** or **little** are also to be used.

It is particularly to be noted that through these two types of lines—as long and short—wonderful things are to be measured. And it is even more wonderful when one of them is moved or shifted from point to point during measuring, and most wonderful of all when both are shifted or moved. Some <term>builders<note>original: "bauleut", referring to architects, masons, or master craftsmen who used complex geometry in their work.</note></term> will well understand what I am saying. From these things, matters regarded as wonderfully difficult are easily found by whoever searches diligently through these ways. Therefore, I have drawn this figure, the <term>twin-scale</term>, here below opposite for you to follow.

<summary>This page introduces the "twin-scale" (zwiling), a geometric tool consisting of two lines at a right angle that allows an artist to scale a figure's length and width independently. Dürer explains how to construct this diagram to create variations in body type—making figures thicker, thinner, or longer while maintaining proportional harmony.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for modifying human proportions, specifically moving from the male figures discussed previously to the female figure (the "wife of the thick, strong man") introduced in the first book of the treatise.</meta>

Based on the doublet <term>doublet|German: 'Zwiling'; a geometric transformation tool or proportional scale used to stretch or compress a figure</term> described above, I will now take the first woman described—the wife of the first thick, strong man—from the first small book, and use this doublet to change her, making her thinner, narrower, thicker, and wider than she was previously described.

It should be noted that if a human form becomes too distorted <note>original: 'gefelscht'; literally 'falsified' or 'corrupted' in terms of natural appearance</note> through the aforementioned changes, and deviates entirely from its proper nature—such as when certain parts become far too thin and narrow, or too thick and wide—one must then correct those parts using the "give and take" <note>This refers to a method of proportional adjustment described in earlier chapters.</note> instructions described previously.

Therefore, I will adjust several things on the woman who has become thinner or thicker. Specifically, for the thinner woman, the head should be made shorter; from the highest point of the crown to the bottom of the chin shall be two 15th-parts <note>Dürer uses a system of fractional "parts" to define body measurements.</note>. The total height of the figure should not be reduced from the top; instead, the face should be shifted upward, though the internal divisions of its length should remain the same as before. However, the shoulder lines should be drawn down from the chin line on both sides toward the armpits. And since the foot became shorter in the square <term>square|The 'Vierung' or grid system used to plot the body's coordinates</term>, it should be lengthened again so that it measures one 13th-part and one 14th-part. After that, make the legs from the line of the buttocks down to the lower ankle <note>original: 'knorren'; refers to the protruding bone of the ankle</note> a little thinner and narrower as appropriate. 

But for the enlarged <note>or 'stouter'</note> woman, I have left her as I described in the first book, except the jowl <note>original: 'trollen'; refers to the flesh under the chin or the throat area</note> under the chin is larger. You may treat this woman’s head just as you did the man’s head previously. I am making the shoulders higher from the armpits up toward the neck, and I am moving the line of the buttocks further down, otherwise the rear would be too tucked in. I also make the leg, as seen from the side, a little narrower below the knee, and make her foot 1/6th [of the body height] in length. However, I make the front-facing legs a little narrower around the ankles and the feet. I have drafted <note>original: 'auffgerissen'</note> and presented both the smaller and larger woman twice before your eyes.

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the first book of Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion," specifically focusing on the mathematical transformation of a "type" into a thinner, more elongated figure.</meta>

Furthermore, I wish to describe the fourth man in the first book, changing him using the <term>twin</term> <note>Dürer refers to a proportional divider or a specific scale used to calculate widths relative to heights</note> to make him thinner and narrower than he was before. This is how I proceed: I make the horizontal line on the "twin" one-sixth shorter than the vertical line of the man’s total height, and I use the <term>transposer</term> <note>A geometric method or tool Dürer used to shift proportions from one scale to another</note> as a new divider, as mentioned previously. However, the vertical line will remain divided along its length by the horizontal lines as before—just as it was described for the man—except that I shift the <term>oxen-line</term> <note>Dürer's term for the line marking the armpits or shoulders</note> slightly further upward as is appropriate, so that the shoulders do not become too thick. I also omit the lower chest line and place the <gloss>nipples</gloss> on the previous "oxen-line" so that they stand higher than before. Additionally, I push the two horizontal lines above the foot further downward so that the <gloss>instep</gloss> of the foot does not become too high. After this, I derive all the thicknesses and widths for this new man from the shorter horizontal line of the "twin," as well as the measurements in between; in this way, a long, thin, narrow figure is produced.

Likewise, if one wishes to make this figure’s head one-tenth of the body's length, they may do so, provided that the <gloss>forehead line</gloss> is shifted to the highest possible point and the horizontal lines of the face are again divided equally. However, the figure will become long-necked if the head is shifted upward in this way. The foot should be made one-seventh of the body's length.

To better understand all of this, I have drawn this aforementioned modified man below, along with his corresponding woman, modified in the same way as the man, so that you may follow these instructions.

In the same manner that I shifted the horizontal lines for the man and omitted one, I do the same for the woman.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions from Albrecht Dürer’s treatise on human proportion, specifically focusing on how to adjust the geometric measurements of a figure to create a thinner, more elongated appearance.</meta>

Furthermore, I wish to describe the fourth man in the first book by altering him using the <term>twin<note>original: "zwiling". This refers to a specific proportional scale or measuring tool Dürer uses to compare and adjust dimensions.</note></term> to make him thinner and narrower than he was before. I do it in this manner: I make the horizontal line on the "twin" one-sixth shorter than the vertical line of the man’s total height. I then use the <term>transformer<note>original: "verkerer". A geometric device or transfer-ruler used to shift proportions from one scale to another.</note></term> to create a new scale, as previously mentioned. However, the vertical line will remain divided along its horizontal markers as before, as was described for the man; only the horizontal line of the <term>armpits<note>original: "ochsen". This is an archaic anatomical term Dürer uses to denote the axillary region or the line across the armpits.</note></term> I shift further upwards according to my judgment, so that the shoulders do not become too thick. I also omit the lower breast line and place the <term>nipples<note>original: "tütlein".</note></term> on the previous armpit line, so that they stand higher than before. Additionally, I shift the two horizontal lines above the foot further downwards so that the instep of the foot does not become too high. Then, using the shorter horizontal line from the "twin," I apply the thickness and width to all his horizontal lines and the spaces between them; in this way, an elongated, thin, and narrow figure is produced.

Likewise, if one wishes to make the head of this figure one-tenth of the total height, they may do so, provided that the forehead line is shifted to the highest possible point and the horizontal lines of the face are divided equally again. However, the figure will become long-necked if the head is shifted upwards; and make the foot one-seventh of the length.

To ensure all of this is better understood, I have <term>plotted<note>original: "auffgerissen". This refers to technical drafting or a geometric diagram.</note></term> the aforementioned modified man here following this text, along with his corresponding woman, modified just as the man was, to serve as a guide.

In the same way that I shifted the horizontal lines for the man and omitted one, I do the same for the woman.

<summary>Dürer provides technical instructions on using geometric tools like the "twin" and "transformer" to mathematically thin and elongate the human figure. He details specific adjustments to the armpits, nipples, and instep to maintain aesthetic balance while altering proportions.</summary>

--- Page 234 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on geometric transformations of the human figure, likely from Albrecht Dürer's "Four Books on Human Proportion." It introduces a mechanical/geometric method for distorting figures.</meta>

Next, I wish to show another way that one may deliberately distort <note>original: "felschlich mag verendern"—here "felschlich" implies an intentional, artificial deviation from the standard proportion rather than a mistake.</note> a previously drawn figure, yet do so systematically. By this method, if one wishes, all parts may be elongated upwards and shortened downwards. Or, one may do the opposite: shorten the parts of the figure upwards and elongate them downwards. In all of this, the thickness and width of the figure remain the same on all horizontal lines <term>zwerch linien|horizontal lines</term> and in the limbs in between, just as they were before. Or, one might choose to make the parts of the figure that are elongated at the top thinner and narrower as the situation requires; likewise, in the opposite figure where the upper parts are shortened and the lower ones elongated, the horizontal lines and what lies between them may be thickened and broadened. How this is to be done, I shall show thus:

I place a straight line **a. b.** horizontally. Underneath it, I place a point **c.** If you now wish to greatly widen the parts at the top, set the point **c.** closer to the horizontal line **a. b.** But if you do not wish to widen the parts excessively, set the point **c.** quite deep below the horizontal line **a. b.** Now note and keep in mind: let the line **a. b.** be the figure's ground line <note>original: "soln lini"—literally "sole line" or base line.</note>. Above this ground line **a. b.**, I draw another horizontal parallel line <note>original: "barlini"—a contraction of "parallel-linie."</note> **f. g.**, raised as high as the length of the figure I am describing. Between them, I place a vertical dotted line in which the proportions of the limbs are divided according to length; let this be **h.** at the top and **i.** at the bottom.

Afterward, I draw a horizontal line **k. l.** exactly in the middle between **f. g.** and **a. b.** Once that is done, I then draw all horizontal lines through the points of division on the figure's vertical line **h. i.** Then I draw two lines upward from point **c.** as long as I need them; let their ends be **d. e.** These split apart from each other at the top and should be movable <note>likely referring to a physical instrument or a geometric construction acting like a pair of calipers.</note>, for with them one must measure the parts that are to become larger and smaller. But both lines must remain joined at point **c.** below.

If you now wish to make a specific part—whether it be at the top or bottom—larger or smaller, and find how it should rightly be done, do as follows: Let the part that is to be measured stand upon whichever horizontal line it may; always take the same widths or thicknesses as they were described in the first original figure, and place them in the middle of the horizontal line **k. l.**, so that the vertical line **h. i.** passes through the center of the placed part. If you then let the two movable lines **d. e.** touch the edges of this placed part on both sides, they will show you the enlarged width up on the horizontal line where it is meant to stand. Thus, this method enlarges and diminishes all things above and below the line **k. l.**, both upward and downward. All of this can be used by turning the bottom to the top and inverting it; then everything becomes the opposite of the first method.

I have drawn this figure twice hereafter, the second time inverted, and I give it a special name, calling it the **Indicator** <note>original: "Zeyger"—meaning a pointer, indicator, or hand of a clock.</note>.

<summary>This page describes a geometric method for systematically distorting the proportions of a figure, either elongating or shortening parts while maintaining or adjusting widths using a tool or construction called the "Indicator." It provides step-by-step instructions for setting up the horizontal lines and the central point of perspective needed for these proportional shifts.</summary>

--- Page 236 ---
<meta>Continuity note: The catchword "cken" likely completes the word "rücken" (to move or shift) or "strecken" (to stretch) from the preceding page.</meta>

But to return to the matter of **distortion** <note>original: "verfelschung"; here referring to the intentional geometric transformation of a figure</note> (for which the previously described indicator is specifically intended and will be better understood), I will explain how it should be made. To do this: Take the fifth man and his corresponding woman described in the second book. Just as their vertical lines of height are divided by the transverse lines, let one of those vertical dotted lines be **a** at the top and **b** at the bottom. Stand this upright before you. From point **b**, draw a transverse line at right angles; let its end be **c**. Then draw a diagonal line from **a** to **c**. Next, from the dotted line of the figure, draw straight lines from all the points that indicate the divisions of length into point **c**. In this way, this tool is ready. From it, you must take the distortion of the figure that is lengthened in its upper parts and shortened below, as well as the opposite. Therefore, this specific hanging line should be measured as it falls "falsely" <note>meaning distorted from the natural proportion</note>.

Now you must first learn its use, as follows: Take another line of the figure’s length, let it be **d** at the top and **e** at the bottom. Place this line with its bottom end **e** on the line **b-c**, and push it back from the angle **b** toward point **c** by approximately one-eighth of the figure's length. Lean its top end **d** against the diagonal line **a-c**. By doing this, this line **d-e** will be intersected and proportionately divided by the lines drawn from the points of the figure’s division to point **c**, yet it will be distorted. For all things will lengthen upward and become shorter downward. If you now place this newly created dotted line before you (the one that is **d** at the top and **e** at the bottom) and draw horizontal lines through all the points, and then mark upon and between them the previously described thicknesses and widths of the figure, and then draw the shape of the figure anew within it, you will see its transformation. For this reason, I have drawn this **distorter** <note>original: "felscher"; a geometric construction used to alter proportions</note> twice below, and on the second one, I have turned the bottom to the top to show how to adjust it. And this is why: if you wish to reverse the figure opposite to what was mentioned before, turn your dotted line upside down and use it according to the same method described above. I have drawn a pair of such figures—a man and a woman—for each of these two methods, which have been distorted by the aforementioned means. However, I have neither enlarged nor diminished them at the top or bottom, as was said above regarding the indicator. But whoever wishes to do so may. And whoever considers this distorter well and uses it correctly in various ways will achieve wonderful things and create much of use. For the previous terms of distinction are all to be considered here, so that hidden things necessary for such work may be found. However, do not give or take as much from things as is done here <note>an advice for moderation in artistic distortion</note>. Anyone who wishes may, using the scale, describe anew all these previously made and altered figures as they have become.

But if someone wishes to describe them, let them avoid the deformity that arises from too much distortion. For I do not present these things as if they must be so and not otherwise; rather, I have set them so far out of their right nature for the purpose of teaching, so that from such coarse distinctions, one may clearly recognize that all these described things should be used out of necessity and not for ruin. Therefore, do not push the leaning line in the distorter too far back on the horizontal line, whether it be upright or inverted, and guard yourself so that your transformations do not become **changelings** <note>original: "wechselbelg"; a folkloric term for a deformed or monstrous substitute child, used here to mean a grotesque or unnatural figure</note>. For as useful as these things are when used out of necessity, they are just as misleading if used without need or out of mere curiosity in excess, when one does too much without reason. This distorter may be used in specific parts just as well as in the whole. From such various transformations of the human figure, one finds which are stronger or weaker, more agile or slower, free or heavy. Therefore, it is necessary to know all this, for such things extend much further than is shown here.

<summary>This page describes the construction and use of a geometric "distorter" tool used to systematically alter the proportions of the human figure for artistic effect. Dürer emphasizes that while these transformations help create different physical types, they must be used with moderation to avoid creating grotesque or "unnatural" figures.</summary>
<keywords>geometric distortion, human proportion, artistic tools, foreshortening, transformation, figure drawing, moderation in art</keywords>

<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the instructions for using the geometric distortion tool described on the previous page, specifically focusing on how to adjust the resulting figures for aesthetic effect.</meta>

Furthermore, anyone who wishes to use the pair of figures—the woman and the man—that have been altered by the **falsifier** <note>original: "felſcher"; Dürer’s term for a geometric tool used to create distorted proportions</note> for the sake of oddity <note>original: "ſeltzamkeyt"; referring to a curious or unusual aesthetic appearance</note> should do the following: For the two figures that were lengthened at the top, he should make the head above the forehead quite low and make the feet higher in the instep <note>original: "hochriſtiger"</note>.

But for the other pair of figures that were lengthened at the bottom, one should make the shins along with the instep <term>instep<gloss>the arched upper part of the human foot between the toes and the ankle</gloss></term> of the foot much shorter, and then re-divide everything equally through the **inverter** <note>original: "verkerer"; another geometric device used to redistribute or reverse proportions</note>. However, in the following figures, I have left everything exactly as it turned out.

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the first "felscher" (transformer/distorter) tool. This page continues the instruction on geometric transformations for artistic perspective. Catchword: "Diſe" (These).</meta>

Now I wish to draft another <term>transformer<note>German: *felscher*. In this context, it refers to a geometric device or grid used to distort or "transform" an image's proportions.</note></term> and demonstrate it, so that one may also transform a previously described human figure according to its length. However, its thickness and width are transformed in an orderly fashion through the <term>transfer-scale<note>German: *weier*. A tool or method for transferring measurements.</note></term>, and one may use this as often as it is needed. It is measured in two ways: one is measured on the outside on the leg of a <term>compass<note>German: *zirckel*.</note></term>, the other on the inside of the leg of a hollow compass. Any clever person may do with this what he pleases for as long as he likes. Whoever grasps this with the right understanding will well realize what he should use it for. Through this, one can also understand how it happens that a straight, parallel ruler appears curved before a round mirror, so that when it stands upright before it, it gains a protruding belly in the middle of the mirror and becomes wide, but at the top and bottom it is narrow and bent backwards. Likewise, all other things align themselves according to the circle. Therefore, whoever wishes to paint in <term>vaults<note>German: *gwelber*. Architectural arched ceilings.</note></term> must know such things—though this is not yet true <term>perspective<note>Latin: *perspectiua*.</note></term>; however, it follows along if one sees such things in a mirror. But here I speak only of the measurement itself, for this transformer only shows what is measured on a round or hollow object from a straight one. But the reason why it falls this way you will realize and see in the transformation of the images when you draft them from this transformer, for it reveals itself. And although it looks strange in the drafting—where it is of no help in its application and seems useless—it is nonetheless necessary that one knows this and other practices, so that an intelligent person may extract what is useful to him; yet the ignorant may mock this and even greater things.

Now I will show how to prepare this second transformer so that one may transform a previously made image from it. When this is used correctly, it brings improvement to certain things; therefore, I avoid using too many words. But the reason I provide much detail here is so that the distinction may be recognized more clearly in a small scale, and also because I do not have the space here to make it on a small scale due to the <term>smallness of the sheet<note>German: *kleinen blaß halben*.</note></term>; therefore, use this thing moderately in larger works. Make this transformer thus: First, take the length of the image on its line, which is divided with all its points; set it upright before you. Mark its ends **r** at the top and **y** at the bottom. Thereafter, divide **r y** with a point **c** in the middle. From this point, draw a straight line at equal angles across; let the end be **z**. Thereafter, draw two lines **r z** and **y z**; thus a long <term>triangle<note>German: *triangel*.</note></term> is formed, and the **z** is the apex. Thereafter, draw lines from all points of the upright line **r y** to the point **z**, as far as you need them. Thereafter, draw behind the line **r y** toward the point **c** an upright <term>parallel line<note>German: *bar lini*.</note></term> as long as you need it, for it shall go far below and above the front line of the triangle. Thereafter, take a long compass and set one foot in the point **z** and the other foot on the center line of the triangle near behind the upright **r y**, so that it touches the line **r y** at one point. From there, draw a circular arc upwards and downwards until it reaches the hindmost upright parallel line. And where this circular stroke cuts the hindmost upright parallel line, set an **f** at the top and a **t** at the bottom. And where this circular stroke subsequently cuts the lines that are drawn from the points of the line **r y** into the point **z**, from all those same points draw horizontal parallel lines into the hindmost upright line **f t**. Thus the line **f t** will be divided from the line **r y**, but it changes entirely: first, because it becomes shorter; thereafter, because it is drawn from the points of the circle, and thus all things are shortened at the top and bottom, the longer they are, the more so. Thereafter, mark all points in this newly formed line above the middle. Mark the uppermost horizontal line with an **a**, the second **b**, then **c**, and so on: **d, e, f, g**, etc. But mark the lower half with numbers: begin at the bottom with a **1**, then **2, 3, 4, 5**, etc. These...

<summary>Albrecht Dürer explains a geometric method for distorting human figures to fit curved surfaces like architectural vaults, using a triangular grid and circular arcs. He emphasizes that while these transformations may look strange on paper, they are essential for achieving the correct visual effect on non-flat surfaces.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for using the "transformer" (felscher) and "transfer-scale" (weler) to modify the proportions of a human figure. It follows the discussion of how geometric shapes appear distorted in curved mirrors or vaults.</meta>

<sig>S iiij</sig>

line a. i. with its contents and the full length of the limbs, use it as previously described. Place two of these lines of equal height next to one another: the first for the profile view and the second for the frontal view. Draw through all your newly created divisions with <term>horizontal lines<note>German: *zwerch linien*.</note></term>. Once the lengths of the parts are divided, you must then find how to divide the thickness and width. Do this through the transfer-scale with its movable line; however, you must use it on both sides, which will give you equal results at the top and bottom.

First, draw a straight horizontal line where both ends are labeled **a.** and **a.** In the middle, set an upright line at right angles; let this be **g.** at the top and **b.** at the bottom. Now make two of the movable lines **a. c.**, one on each side. You must use them as follows: take from the <term>transformer<note>German: *felscher*. A geometric construction used to calculate distorted proportions.</note></term> you just made, on the middle horizontal line of the triangle drawn from point **3.** through the arc **f. t.**, the greatest width between the straight line and the arc. Place one end of this measurement at the bottom on the horizontal line of the transfer-scale **a. a.**, upright on the line **g. b.** At this height, place the two movable lines **a. c.** so that they cross over one another.

Afterward, take the upper half of the horizontal lines between the arc and its <term>chords<note>German: *sennen*. The straight lines connecting the ends of an arc.</note></term> (or straightened lines) of each respective length, and note which letters they are marked with. Set them upright on one side of the transfer-scale so that one of their ends touches the horizontal line of the scale **a. a.** Slide them parallel along the horizontal line **a. a.** back and forth until their tops all touch the positioned line **a. c.** Thus, the short lines in the transfer-scale near point **a.** appear just as they do in their arc in the transformer near point **f.** However, the distance between them does not remain the same as in the arc because of the arc's curvature—for the straight against the curved creates a tapering. And just as I placed the upper half (marked with letters in the transformer) into one part of the transfer-scale, I do the same with the other half of the arc at the bottom of the transformer (using the parts marked there with numbers); I place these in the other half of the transfer-scale. Now that this is prepared, take any thickness or width of the figure previously described on every horizontal line and in between, and place it in the transfer-scale on the upright line **g. b.** so that it touches the horizontal line **a. a.** at the bottom. Then lay your movable line **a. c.** upon it, and it will cut off all the upright lines that are or stand in the transfer-scale. Therefore, whatever upright line a width or thickness is taken from, its measurement will be correct through this positioning and cutting-off, just as I have very precisely drawn hereafter.

And so, I have hereafter also drawn and transformed two figures, a man and a woman, which I described at the end of the other little book, using this transformer. Only the arms and hands, and likewise the feet, I did not make according to this transformer, but rather according to artistic skill; for when this transformer reaches the extreme heights or depths, it distorts far too much.

Furthermore, if this transformer is inverted and used in reverse, and a figure is transformed from it, that same figure will be lengthened more and more in its parts in <term>steep perspective<note>German: *vberstich vnd vnderstich*. These terms refer to extreme foreshortening from a high or low viewpoint.</note></term>, but toward the middle, it will be shortened more and more, as in the figure prepared before. To make this inverted transformer, do as follows: take again the dotted line **r. y.** of the figure's length. Stand it upright before you and lengthen it with **f. t.** as before. Then set your point **3.** and draw the two lines **r. 3.** and **y. 3.** Then, from all points on the line **r. y.**, draw straight lines to point **3.** After that, set a point **t.** across from point **3.** at the same height. Take a long pair of compasses, set one foot on point **t.** and the other foot on point **f.**, and draw a circle around to point **f.** so that the arc is between **r. y.** and point **3.** Where this circle-trace cuts the straight lines drawn from the points between **r. y.** to point **3.**, draw straight parallel horizontal lines from those intersections. Behind the arc, toward point **3.**, draw an upright line through all these horizontal lines. This is then the newly found line, newly divided by the horizontal lines through the entire length of the figure. Afterward, you must seek the thickness and width of the figure through the transfer-scale as before, but inverted. Whoever wishes may draw a figure this way themselves, for hereafter I have drawn nothing more than this transformer for that purpose.
