--- Page 256 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer's technical instructions on the manipulation of human proportions, moving from the mechanical application of tools to the theoretical and ethical considerations of artistic practice.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>Z</sig>

Whoever wishes to go further may again transform any of the previously described proportions of the figures that have already been altered—be it through the shortener, widener, doubler, pointer, or distorter <note>original: "verkorer, weier, zwiling, zeyger oder felscher." These are Dürer's technical terms for instruments or mathematical methods used to stretch, compress, or mirror human figures in his proportion studies.</note>—either in specific parts or as a whole. You may also mix all the previously made figures together, out of their proportions, in whatever way seems most skillful, until you find something pleasing to yourself and others. However, such mixing produces strange results; and especially take note: when you wish to draw the form of any figure with its lines, both along the straight dividing lines and between them, you must take great care not to create a deformity. 

For between the outer horizontal lines <term>zwerch linien: cross-lines or horizontal coordinates used in a grid to measure the body</term>, the length and character of the limbs are indicated; and upon these lines, the thickness and breadth of that specific part are shown, so as not to fail the written description. But between these horizontal lines, one can easily make mistakes, for sometimes one draws the lines too far in or out; consequently, those parts become too thick, thin, wide, or narrow. For the sake of brevity, these matters are not described here with such absolute precision as one might demonstrate by hand to show how it ought to be done. 

Therefore, I expect that many who are unskilled at drafting <term>auffreyssen: the technical process of laying out or sketching a proportional figure</term> will follow my instructions, spoil the work, and then lay the blame on me, saying I have described it poorly. Therefore, anyone who drafts figures according to this little book and is not well-versed in the matter will find it difficult at first. But let him then place a person before him who roughly corresponds to those same proportions. After that, let him draw the outer lines as best he can and understands; for it is considered good when one follows life so closely in the drawing <note>German: "ab machen," literally "making from" or copying from a model.</note> that it looks like the subject and becomes similar to nature. Especially when the finished work is beautiful, it is held to be artistic <term>künstlich: in this period, the term refers to being "full of art" or "skillfully made" rather than "artificial."</term> and is rightly praised as it deserves. 

But henceforth, it is up to each individual's will whether or how he uses all the previously described variations. For if he wishes, one may learn to work with the art in which truth resides, or he may work without art, whereby through that very "freedom" everything is led astray, and his labor becomes a mockery to those who understand. For well-performed labor is honorable to God, and to man it is useful, good, and lovely. But to produce contemptible work in the arts is reprehensible and harmful, and it is detested in small works as much as in great ones. 

And for this reason, it is necessary that everyone uses discretion <term>bescheydenheyt: meaning "judiciousness," "modesty," or "proper measure" here.</term> in the work he brings to light. From this it follows that whoever wishes to create something righteous should not detract anything from nature, nor impose anything unbearable upon her. Yet some wish to make so little change that it can hardly be noticed; such a thing is worthless if it cannot be perceived. Conversely, too much change is also of no use; a proper middle ground is best. 

However, the reason I have diverged so far into extremes earlier in this booklet was so that the differences might be more easily perceived in small things. But whoever wishes to work on a larger scale should not follow this "harshness" of mine; instead, he should make his work softer, so that it does not appear beastly, but rather looks artistic. For such variations are not good to look upon when they are used incorrectly and without mastery. It is also no wonder that a skillful master considers many different variations of form that he could produce if he had enough time; however, he must leave such things be, for such possibilities <note>original: "zufel," literally "accidents" or "incidences," here referring to the myriad potential variations of the human form.</note> are countless for artists, and their minds are full of images that it would be possible for them to create. 

Therefore, if a man who skillfully practiced such art and was naturally gifted for it were granted many hundreds of years to live, he would—through the power God has given to man—be able to pour out and create many new forms of humans and other creatures <term>creaturen: any created living things.</term> every day, such as had never been seen before nor even imagined by another.

<summary>Dürer warns artists against creating deformities through the misuse of proportional grids and encourages them to balance mathematical theory with the observation of nature. He concludes by reflecting on the infinite creative potential of the artist's mind as a gift from God.</summary>
<keywords>proportions, artistic theory, nature, drafting, creativity, Albrecht Dürer, human form, aesthetics</keywords>

--- Page 257 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer's discussion on the variation of human proportions. Having previously discussed the mechanical tools for distorting figures, he now addresses the philosophical and practical nature of "difference" in art—distinguishing between accidental variations and intentional artistic choices.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>

Therefore, God gives skillful <term>künstreychen: possessing artistic skill, ingenuity, or "art-richness"</term> people much power in these and many other matters. And although much has been said about "difference," one knows well that all things a person can do naturally differ from one another. Thus, no artist lives who is so certain of his hand that he can make two things so exactly alike that they cannot be distinguished from each other. For in all our doing, no one thing is exactly and entirely equal to another. We cannot avoid this; for we see that when we take two prints from one engraved copperplate, or cast two images in one mold, differences are immediately found by which they may be known apart for many reasons. If this is found to be true in the most certain of things, how much more so in other things made by a free hand.

This, however, is not the "difference" of which I speak here; for I am speaking of the difference that a person specifically intends, and which stands within his own will—of which I have spoken further and further. When it occurs to a person that he wishes to make this or that, he chooses something for himself from among various things. But I do not mean the aforementioned difference that we cannot separate from our work <note>Dürer refers here to the "accidental" differences he just described, like those in printmaking</note>, but rather such a difference that makes a thing beautiful or ugly. These are achieved through the "words of difference" described earlier in this booklet <note>Referring to his earlier categories of "large, small, thick, thin," etc.</note>. When one draws such things into the work, it will be judged by each person’s sight in their mind according to their own thinking; these judgments seldom agree with one another. 

And therefore, I wish to leave all my previously described things—including those I have altered—entirely free to everyone, should they wish to leave nothing as it was shown. Concerning this, I will hereafter give instruction on by what means one may do so. Yet let each man beware that he makes nothing impossible that Nature cannot suffer <note>Dürer's core philosophy: art may vary, but it must remain grounded in natural possibility</note>; unless it be the case that one wishes to make "dream-work" <term>traumwerck: fantasy or imaginary art</term>. In such a case, one may mix all kinds of creatures together. 

But to come to the work: let us first take before us a figure as it is divided by horizontal lines <term>zwerch linien: cross-lines or horizontal coordinates used in a grid to measure the body</term> in its proper length. These same lines have their own numbers in all the previously described figures. Whoever wishes may make more or fewer of these horizontal lines. But to make more horizontal lines serves to create a more certain measurement, whereby one may seek out further detail than I have previously shown and set down; and this is necessary for a diligent master. And if one were so certain of his hand, he would not need to make straight lines, but could simply set points, which is enough if he can mark them. However, he who makes fewer horizontal lines than I have set down will have less labor, but he will also achieve less. This is one kind of difference regarding the lines.

The other way to handle the horizontal lines through the body works just as it did earlier with the "reversed faces" <note>Dürer likely refers to his studies on foreshortening and perspective</note>. That is, you may shift all the horizontal lines from their positions throughout the entire body, either toward or away from each other. If one moves the lines close together, the parts between them become short; but where one moves them far apart, the parts between them become long. All of this concerns the words "long" and "short."

Now we shall turn to thickness and breadth. This change occurs on all the horizontal lines and also between them. Whatever you add to a specific line will make the figure wider at that place from the front and thicker from the side. But if you take away from it, the figure becomes narrower and thinner at that end. The same happens between all the horizontal lines, so that one can make everything on the figure larger or smaller. Yet you should make nothing too long, short, thick, thin, wide, or narrow. And without doubt, if you are skillful, you may do wonderful things through these aforementioned methods...

<summary>Dürer discusses the distinction between accidental variation and intentional artistic choice, encouraging artists to manipulate proportions while respecting the limits of nature, unless creating fantasy "dream-works." He provides technical instructions on how to use horizontal coordinate lines to lengthen, shorten, thicken, or thin a figure.</summary>

<keywords>artistic intent, nature vs fantasy, dream-work, horizontal lines, proportions, measurement, copperplate engraving, aesthetics, beauty and ugliness</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer's exploration of human variety. Having established that no two things are ever identical, he now argues that intentional variation in proportion is the key to depicting different types of beauty and character, while acknowledging that ultimate "truth" in beauty belongs to God alone.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>T ij</sig>

...create a change in form and bring about great inequality between one another. From this it is found that some acquire broad shoulders, thin flanks, and narrow hips, while others are the opposite. Thus some acquire a short torso and long legs, and others the reverse. Some have straight bodies, arms, and legs, while others have crooked ones. Thus, through measurement <term>messung: the systematic application of proportions</term>, nature becomes recognizable in the human form. From these things mentioned above, one finds the reason for how to create both lovely and ugly things. Therefore, whoever wishes to undertake such a work should know in his intention what he wants to create.

For this reason, I have indicated the descriptions above so that a person might be primarily thoughtful before beginning his work. It is necessary for whoever wishes to show his skill in his art to put forward the best that he can, which is suitable for that same work. But it should be noted that an understanding, practiced artist <term>künstner: a person of skill and knowledge, more than a mere craftsman</term> can demonstrate his great power and art more in a coarse, peasant-like figure than some can in their large works. These strange words will be understood only by the powerful artists: that I speak the truth.

From this it happens that what one man draws with a pen in a single day on a half-sheet of paper, or carves into a small piece of wood with his small chisel <note>original: "eyselein," a small iron tool for engraving or carving</note>, becomes more artistic and better than another's large work upon which he labored for a whole year with the highest diligence. And this gift is wonderful, for God often gives it to one person to learn and understand how to make something so good that no equal to him can be found in his own time, nor had there been any for a long time before him, and no one will soon follow him.

We see an example of this in the times of the Romans, when they were in their splendor. We still see the ruins <note>original: "drümer" – the physical remains or fragments of classical antiquity</note> of what was made by them, the likes of which, in terms of art, is rarely found in our own works today. If we ask, however, how we should make a beautiful image, some will speak according to human judgment <term>vrteyl: subjective aesthetic opinion or consensus</term>, while others will not agree. And I, too, do not have a certain knowledge of what is right. Who then can make us certain of this? For I believe that no person lives who could conceive of the most beautiful end even in the smallest living creature, to say nothing of a human being, who is a special creation of God to whom other creatures are subject.

I admit that one person may conceive of and make a prettier image, and show good natural reasons for it to a simple mind, better than another—but not to the absolute end that it could not be even prettier. Such perfection does not enter into the human mind. God alone knows such things; whomever He reveals it to, that person would also know it. Truth alone contains within it what the most beautiful human form and measure <term>maß: the ideal proportion</term> might be, and no other.

People deliberate about such things and have countless different judgments, seeking many ways to reach them. However, one achieves ugliness much more easily than beauty. Regarding the error that we now have among us, I do not know how to steadfastly describe what measure could finally and rightly be called "pretty." But I would gladly help as much as I can so that the coarse deformity of our work might be cut away and avoided—unless it were the case that someone, with special diligence, wished to create a deformed thing. 

Now we return, as mentioned before, to human judgment. They consider a certain form beautiful at one time, and at another time they choose a different one instead. Now, if these same people require a work from a master, the master should be so skillful that he can satisfy their desire; thus he is to be praised. For this, he must have a powerful command of his practice if he is to fulfill their will. It would be useful to him if he understood in his mind which was the right measure and no other, so that he could also demonstrate the same with his work.

**Vocabulary:**
**Form**, **Inequality**, **Measurement**, **Artist**, **Roman times**, **Beautiful image**, **Judgment**, **Right measure**

<summary>Dürer discusses how varied proportions define beauty and ugliness, arguing that true artistic skill can be seen even in simple sketches or "coarse" subjects. He concludes that while artists strive for the "right measure," ultimate perfection in beauty is a divine secret unknown to humans.</summary>
<keywords>human proportion, artistic genius, Roman art, subjectivity of beauty, divine creation, artistic judgment, aesthetics, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>This page follows Dürer's discussion on how measurement allows an artist to recognize nature. He moves from the theoretical possibility of perfection to the practical necessity of consistency, geometry, and meticulous observation in creating a unified human figure.</meta>

But it seems impossible to me if someone says he knows how to show the best measure in the human form; for lies are within our perception, and darkness sits so firmly within us that even our fumbling fails. But he who proves his work through **geometry** <term>Geometria: the mathematical study of shapes, which Dürer considered the only objective "truth" in art</term> and shows the fundamental truth—him the whole world should believe. For there one is captured by proof, and it is right to hold such a man, as one gifted by God, for a master in such matters. The reasons for his proofs are to be heard with longing, and it is even more joyful to see his works. 

Since we cannot reach the very best <note>meaning: absolute perfection or the "ideal" human form</note>, should we then abandon our learning entirely? We do not accept such a shameful thought, for men have both good and evil before them; therefore, it befits a rational man to undertake the better path. 

And so that we return to how a better image <term>bild: a figure, statue, or painted representation</term> might be made, we must first arrange the whole figure well and magnificently with all its limbs, and thereafter ensure that each individual limb is made with careful consideration, in the very smallest things as well as in the largest. We do this so that we might draw out the beauty that is given to us, in order to come closer to the right goal. 

As was mentioned before, a human is a piece gathered from many different parts, and as each part has its own particular nature, one must take very diligent heed of all such things and what might corrupt them, so that such errors are avoided. We must cling diligently to the right natural property and not depart from it, according to our ability. 

To make something praiseworthy to look upon requires great diligence and high attention. For when one first undertakes the head—as has been described previously in the other booklets—one sees what strange roundness it has. Likewise with other things: what strange lines everything requires, which can be drawn by no ruler, but must be drawn only from point to point <note>Dürer refers here to "freehand" organic curves that geometry can measure but a simple straight-edge cannot replicate</note>. And thus, the forehead, cheeks, nose, eyes, mouth, and chin should be made with their proportions <note>original: "inhalt," referring here to the "content" or structural volume of the features</note>, so that the very smallest things are well-formed and good; thus, the whole assembly shall compare well together. 

Thereafter, the neck should rhyme well with the head, being neither too short nor too long, nor too thick or thin. Furthermore, take heed to diligently [form] the chest, belly, the back and buttocks, the legs, feet, arms, and hands with all their proportions, so that the smallest little things are well-disposed and made in the best way. And these things should also be finished in the work in the cleanest and most diligent manner, and the very smallest wrinkles and veins <note>original: "runtzelein vñ ertlein," literally little wrinkles and little veins/earth-lines</note> should not be omitted as far as is possible. 

For it is not right to simply run over the surface or "clutter up" a thing, unless it is a case where one must have a figure very quickly; then one must be satisfied, provided that it still shows an indication of right understanding, and that the right intention is recognized despite the haste. 

The character should be uniform through the entire body, and in all figures, whether they be of a hard or soft nature, fleshy or lean. It should not be that one part is fat and the other is withered, as if you made fat legs and thin arms, or the reverse; or fat in the front and lean in the back, or the reverse. All things should rhyme together proportionately and not be falsely assembled, for harmonious things are most highly esteemed. For this reason, a consistent age should be shown in every figure in all its parts and limbs; the head should not be from a youth, the chest from an old man, and the hands and feet from a middle-aged man. And that the figure not [only] from the front...

<summary>Dürer argues that while absolute perfection is unattainable, artists must use geometry and consistent observation of nature to create harmonious figures. He emphasizes that every part of a body, down to the smallest vein, must match in age, weight, and character to be considered "truthful."</summary>
<keywords>geometry, human proportion, artistic truth, anatomy, consistency, beauty, wrinkles, veins, artistic discipline</keywords>

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s discourse on proportion, specifically focusing on consistency of character and the practical necessity of drawing as a foundation for all art.</meta>

...young behind, old, and also made in reverse; for if it is against nature, then it is bad. Therefore, it is proper that every image <term>bild: any figure, whether a painting, sculpture, or print</term> be of a consistent type throughout, either young, old, or middle-aged, thin or fat, soft or hard. In this way, you find grown youth to be smooth, even, and of full body; but age is uneven, gnarled, wrinkled, and the flesh is withered. To show such things first, before one lays hands on the work, it is helpful to first sketch out <term>auff reyß: to draw or draft with lines</term> how one wishes to have it, so that one may see the form beforehand to see if there is something to be improved. If you do this with diligence and good consideration, then you will not easily regret what you have made. Therefore, it is necessary for every artist to learn to draw <term>reyssen: the art of linear drafting or drawing</term> well, for it serves many arts beyond measure, and much depends upon it.

And even if someone has a good measure <note>original: "gutte maß," referring to the mathematical proportions of a figure</note> described before him, and one who cannot draw tries to make it, and moves with his unskillful hand through the length, thickness, and breadth of the figure, he will very soon ruin what he is supposed to make. But where one who has an understanding of drawing takes a well-described figure, he can assist in the sketching so that it becomes even better. And so that we might arrive at a good measure through which we may bring beauty in part into our work, it seems most useful to me that you take your measure from many living people. But seek out people for this who are considered beautiful, and copy that type with all diligence. For from many various people, something good can be gathered together by an understanding artist through all parts of the limbs; for one rarely finds a person who has all limb-measurements well-formed, for everyone has a defect. And although one should gather from many different people, one should nevertheless use one type of person for a single figure, as was mentioned before regarding consistency. So, in copying for a young figure, use only young people; for an old one, old; for a middle-aged one, middle-aged people. Do the same with thin, fat, soft, and hard people, strong or weak; use each type separately for separate figures. And whoever exerts himself in these things to search out each part of the human specifically, he will find more necessities for his work than he can carry out. And the human understanding can rarely grasp how to correctly copy the beauty in creatures; and although we cannot speak of the greatest beauty of a physical creature, we nevertheless find in visible creatures such an excessive beauty for our understanding that none of us can perfectly bring it into our work.

Furthermore, various types of people belong to various images; for this, you find two types of the human race, such as whites and Moors <note>original: "Morn." Dürer is discussing ethnic diversity and its artistic representation from a 16th-century perspective</note>. Between them, a difference is to be noted regarding the nature that is between them and us. The faces of Moors are seldom pretty <note>original: "huͤbsch." Dürer reflects the Eurocentric aesthetic standards of the 1500s</note> because of their flat noses and thick mouths; likewise, their shins, knees, and feet are too gnarled and not as good to look at as those of whites, as are their hands. But I have seen some who were otherwise so well-formed and elegant in their whole body that I have not seen or could not imagine anything better shaped, being of such very good nature in arms and all things, as good as they could possibly be. Thus, one finds among the races of men all kinds of types which are useful for many kinds of figures, to be looked at according to their complexion <term>complexion: referring to the "Four Humors" or physical constitution/temperament</term>. Thus, the strong and hard have a defect in their body like lions, but the weak have a softer defect and are not as tormented <note>meaning: the muscles appearing less strained or knotted</note> as the strong. Therefore, it is not fitting to give a very strong figure a very soft defect, or a slender figure a very hard...

<summary>Albrecht Dürer argues for artistic consistency, insisting that a figure must be uniformly young, old, or of a specific "type" to remain natural. He emphasizes that beauty is found by synthesizing the best parts of many individuals and briefly discusses ethnic diversity and physical constitution (complexion) in art.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, drafting (reyssen), human proportion, aesthetic consistency, composite beauty, ethnic diversity, complexion, 16th-century art theory</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer's philosophical and technical instructions on human proportion. He transitions from the practicalities of drawing to the deeper theoretical relationship between the artist, nature, and the divine source of beauty.</meta>

...deficiency; even though one must give some leeway to the thin and the fat in images, yet they may fairly be used in all kinds of variations of figures henceforth wherever one wishes. But life in nature allows us to recognize the truth of these things; therefore, look at them diligently, guide yourself by them, and do not depart from nature to follow your own whim, thinking that you might find something better on your own, for you would be misled. For truly, art is embedded in nature; whoever can draw it out <note>original: "herauß kan reyſſenn," meaning to extract or draw forth through the act of drafting</note> possesses it. If you acquire it, it will grant you much in your work, and through Geometry you can prove much of your work. Whatever we cannot prove, however, we must leave to good intention and human judgment; yet experience <term>erfarung: practical experience or empirical knowledge gained through observation</term> does much in these things.

But the more closely your work conforms to life in its form, the better your work appears; and this is true. Therefore, never intend to do something better than what God has given the power to work in His created nature; for your ability is powerless against God's creation. From this it is concluded that no person can ever make a beautiful image <term>bildnuß: a representation, figure, or portrait</term> out of their own mind alone, unless they have well-stored their mind by copying many things; then it is no longer called one’s own, but has become acquired and learned art, which grows together and brings forth fruit of its own kind. From this, the gathered secret treasure of the heart becomes manifest through the work, and the "new creature" <note>original: "newe creatur," referring to the artistic invention or the mental concept that the artist "creates" internally before bringing it to life</note> that one shapes in one’s heart in the form of a thing. 

This is the reason that a well-practiced artist does not need to copy living models for every single figure, for he pours out enough of what he has gathered from the outside into himself over a long time. Such a person finds it easy to create in his work, but very few reach this understanding; instead, there are many who, with great effort, create much that is incorrect. Therefore, whoever has attained good practice through right understanding is well able to make something good without any model present <note>original: "an allen gegen wurff," literally "without any object thrown against" or placed before the artist</note>, as far as our ability allows; yet it will always be better if he uses life as a model while copying. But for the unpracticed, it is impossible, for these things do not happen by chance. 

It also happens—though rarely—that someone through great experience and a long time of diligent practice becomes so certain that, from his own understanding attained with great toil, he might produce something better without a model in front of him <note>original: "auſſerhalb eins gegen geſichts," meaning without a face or subject to look at</note> than another who places many living people before him to copy, because that other person lacks understanding. Therefore, we must take great care and prevent deformity and unseemliness from creeping into our work; for this reason, we should avoid making useless things in figures that are otherwise meant to be beautiful, for that is a defect. 

Take a comparison from the blind, the lame, the withered cripples, the limping, and the like; such things are all ugly because of their deficiency. Likewise, excess is also to be avoided, such as if one wanted to give someone three eyes, three hands, or three feet. But the more one leaves out all the ugliness of the aforementioned things and instead makes straight, strong, bright, and necessary things that all people commonly love, the better that work becomes; for such things are considered beautiful. But beauty <term>hübſcheyt: beauty or comeliness</term> is so contained within human beings, and our judgment of it is so doubtful, that we might find two people who are both very beautiful and lovely, and yet neither is like the other in any single piece or part, neither in measure nor in kind. We also do not understand which is more beautiful, so blind is our knowledge; therefore, when we give judgment on it, it is uncertain. Yet in some parts, one may still surpass the other, even if it is unknown to us.

<summary>Dürer argues that true art is extracted from nature rather than invented by the artist, though a master may eventually create from a "secret treasure of the heart" built through years of observation. He defines beauty as the avoidance of both deficiency (deformity) and excess, while admitting that human judgment of beauty remains subjective and imperfect.</summary>
<keywords>nature, artistic inspiration, Geometry, God as creator, beauty (hübſcheyt), experience (erfarung), artistic training, deformity, subjectivity</keywords>

--- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer's discourse on the training of an artist. Having previously established that art is rooted in nature and geometry, Dürer now moves to the versatility required of a master and the relationship between theory (understanding) and practice (the hand).</meta>

From this it follows that no powerful artist <term>künstner: a master of skill and knowledge</term> should devote himself to only one style, but rather that he should be practiced in many ways and knowledgeable in every kind of style. From this, it follows that he might create whatever kind of figure <note>original: "geschlecht der bild"</note> is requested of him. Based on the aforementioned theories, one may know how to make an angry, a kind, or any other kind of figure, and each figure can be made well for its own sake. Thus, if someone comes to you and wants a treacherous Saturnine <note>original: "Saturninisch" — referring to the influence of the planet Saturn, traditionally associated with a gloomy, melancholic, or even deceptive temperament</note> or Martial <note>original: "Martialisch" — referring to Mars, associated with war, aggression, and physical strength</note> image, or one that represents Venus <note>original: "Venerem" — Latin for Venus; here meaning a figure reflecting beauty, grace, and love</note> which should look lovely and charming, you would easily know—through the aforementioned teachings, if you are practiced in them—what measure and style you should use for it. Thus, through measurement, every kind of human character can be shown from the outside: those who are of a fiery, cheerful <note>original: "lustig," likely referring to the Sanguine temperament</note>, watery, or earthly nature. For the power of art, as said before, masters every work. And true artists recognize in an instant what is a powerful work; and from this, a great love arises in the one who understands it. True fellows know this well and know what is the right practice in it, for knowledge is truthful, but "opinion" <note>original: "meynung" — subjective belief or whim as opposed to objective artistic principles</note> often deceives. Therefore, let no one believe in themselves too much, so that they do not become errant and fail in their work. For this reason, it is very useful for anyone dealing with such things to see various kinds of good images, often those made by famous good masters, and also to hear others speak of them. Yet, you should always take note of their errors and think of how to improve them. And do not let yourself be talked into only one style that a single master uses, as mentioned before. For everyone likes to make things like themselves, whatever pleases them. But if you hear many perspectives, take the best out of them for your own use; for error is found in almost all opinions. Therefore, however well we make a work, it could always be made better; just as with people, however handsome one is found to be, a more beautiful one can still be found. But let everyone take up what is more certain—whether he learns it from someone else or finds it himself from life. Yet let everyone beware of learning from those who speak well of the matter and yet have always produced blameworthy, incompetent works with their hands—of whom I have seen many. For if you follow them, they will mislead you, as their work and their lack of skill testify. For it is a very different thing to speak of a matter and to actually make it. Nevertheless, it is not to be rejected if an unlearned person tells a truth; one should not disbelieve it just because of who said it. For it is possible that a peasant <note>original: "bawer"</note> might tell you the error in your work, but he cannot inform or teach you how you should improve that same error.

Everyone who has never learned anything in this art before and wishes to draw a beginning from this little book must read this with diligence and learn to understand what he reads, taking on less at once. He should practice himself well in those same things so that he can master them, and only then begin to make something else. For understanding <term>verstandt: the intellectual grasp of principles</term> must begin to grow along with practice <note>original: "gebrauch"</note>, so that the hand can do what the will in the mind desires. From this, over time, grows the certainty of art and of practice. For these two must be together; for one without the other is worth nothing. One should also note that although a common man recognizes the better from the worse, no one understands how to judge a work more perfectly than a knowledgeable artist who has often proven such through his own work.

<summary>Dürer argues that a true artist must be versatile enough to depict various temperaments (Saturnine, Martial, etc.) and warns against following personal "opinion" over objective artistic truth. He emphasizes that mastery requires the equal development of intellectual understanding and manual practice.</summary>
<keywords>artistic versatility, planetary temperaments, four humors, theory vs practice, artistic judgment, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 263 ---
<meta>This page concludes the third book of Albrecht Dürer’s treatise on human proportion. Following the previous discussion on how to depict various temperaments and characters through geometry, Dürer now addresses the practical application of these rules. He explains how a master transitions from rigorous measurement to intuitive "eye-measure," and defends the universal truth of his proportional systems regardless of the scale of the artwork.</meta>

Now, one might ask: "Who wants to always endure the toil and labor of such long decoration? See how he measures a single image with so much effort, yet it often happens that in a short time one must create twenty or thirty different figures." In such a case, my meaning is not that one should measure all of his things at all times. But once you have learned to measure well, and have gained understanding along with practice—so that you can make a thing out of "free certainty" and know how to do justice to every subject—then it is not always necessary to measure every single thing. For your acquired art <term>kunst: here meaning a combination of theoretical knowledge and skill</term> provides you with a good **eye-measure** <note>original: "augen maß" — the ability to judge proportions accurately by sight alone</note>. Then, the practiced hand becomes obedient. The power of art drives error from your work and prevents you from creating falsehoods, because you understand them; through your knowledge, you become undaunted and entirely master of your work. Thus, you do not make a single useless stroke or blow. This agility means you do not have to deliberate for long when your head is "filled with art." Through this, your work appears skillful, lovely, powerful, free, and good, and it will be praised by everyone, for **correctness** <note>original: "gerechtickeyt" — implying both accuracy and a sense of aesthetic "justice" or balance</note> is mixed into it.

But if you have no true foundation, it is not possible for you to make anything correct or good, even if you had the greatest manual dexterity in the world. Such "freedom" is more of a pitfall that leads you astray. Therefore, no freedom should exist without art, just as art remains hidden without practice. They must be joined together as said before. It is therefore necessary to learn how to measure with true artistic skill. He who can do this well creates marvelous things. For the human form cannot be traced simply with rulers or compasses; rather, it must be drawn from point to point, as previously described. Outside of right measurement, no one will create anything good.

Now, it may happen that some will take these described measurements of figures and apply them to a large work, and if they fail through their own lack of skill, they will lay the blame on me. They might say that my drawings work in small things, but are misleading in large works. This cannot be so. Either the small is right and the large is good, or the small is bad and the large is worth nothing at all. One cannot split the truth in this matter. A circle drawn with a compass remains round whether it is small or large; the same is true of a square. Therefore, every **proportion** <note>original: "proportz"</note> remains true to itself whether it is large or small. It is just as in music, where one **octave** <note>original: "octaff"</note> relates to another—one high, the other low—yet it remains the same note.

Furthermore, it should be noted that a previously made image can be distorted in very many ways and paths so that it is no longer recognized, and yet in doing so, nothing is truly added to or taken away from its original measurements <note>This likely refers to Dürer’s experiments with "transformation" or anamorphic grids, where a figure is stretched or compressed while maintaining its internal logic</note>.

->End of the Third Book.<-

<summary>Dürer concludes his third book by explaining that rigorous measurement eventually leads to intuitive mastery, or "eye-measure." He argues that proportional rules are universal and scale-invariant, comparing them to musical octaves.</summary>

--- Page 264 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the Fourth Book of Dürer’s treatise. Having previously established the static proportions and "eye-measure" for various human types, he now moves from the rigid, upright models to the mechanics of motion and how to manipulate these figures into lifelike poses.</meta>

# The Fourth Little Book shows how and 
## where one should bend the previously described figures.

Since much has been described in the previous three books about how figures are made, altered, and may be transformed through various differences, it is now necessary that one knows where they should be bent and turned in their limbs, according to how one wants them, as far as is possible. For figures that are as stiffly straight as they were previously described would be of no use, and they would be unpleasant to look at. And no matter how good a limb may be, if one gives it a bad gesture <note>original: "geberd" — posture or carriage of the body</note>, it will be spoiled. Conversely, a lesser limb can be considered worthy through a good gesture. But as to how one should speak of the limbs and how they wonderfully fit into one another—those who deal with <term>Anatomia: the study of the internal structure of the body</term> know this; I will let them speak of it, for I do not wish to speak further of it here, except for what I see as necessary. 

The figures are to be bent through the entire **spine** <note>original: "ruckgrad"</note> from the neck down to the hip on all **transverse lines** <term>transverse lines: the horizontal reference lines Dürer uses in his diagrams to mark key anatomical points</term> and in between. However, they are to be bent more forward than backward, and this bending is used in the hindmost quarter of the body against the back on all transverse lines and in between, except that the thigh with its joint is bent in the middle of the transverse line where the hip ends. Bend the knee also in the middle of its transverse line. Bend the foot at the ankles of the shin bone, and the toes at their joints. And if needed, the foot may also be bent slightly through its middle. Bend the arm at the shoulder joint, the elbow, the wrist, and all fingers at their joints. But when viewing the figure from the front, it is to be bent to both sides from the neck down through the entire spine. To make this easier to note, I have placed small triangles and rings at the main points where they are to be bent in the preceding figures.

Now it is further necessary that one speaks of such bending distinctly and understandably. Therefore, it should be noted: everything that belongs to such bending must be observed with special diligence, so that one can bend and pose every thing earnestly or gracefully. For first, one must use a grim posture for anger, and a friendly one for love. And therefore, understand correctly in your practice what belongs to and accompanies bending. For this, note these six following distinctions, and pay close attention to these words in all bending.

<image-desc>A vertical curly brace groups six terms describing different types of anatomical bending or movement. To the left of the brace is the explanatory text "These are the distinctions".</image-desc>

->These are the distinctions<-

->Bent<-
->Crooked<-
->Turned<-
->Twisted<-
->Stretched / Contracted<-
->And Pushed.<-

---

These six aforementioned words all occur in a human being, more or less, according to how they move. And these six types of distinctions—what I mean by each and how they are to be understood—I will explain through lines as follows.

<summary>Dürer introduces the fourth book by explaining the necessity of bending and posing figures to make them lifelike and expressive. He identifies the specific anatomical joints for movement and introduces six core terms for different types of bodily motion.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, spine, joints, movement, posture, transverse lines, bending, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 265 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s introduction to the Fourth Book. After explaining why stiff figures are undesirable, he now defines the three geometric principles—bending, curving, and turning—that allow an artist to manipulate a static figure into a lifelike pose.</meta>

First, I will address the **bent** <term>bent: *gepogen*, referring here to angular movement at joints like the elbow or knee</term>. Place a straight line *a. b.* and give it two segments <note>original: "glider" — literally 'limbs' or 'links'</note> or two points, *c. d.* At these two points *c. d.*, bend the line back and forth as you wish. However, the lengths between *a. c.*, *c. d.*, and *d. b.* always remain straight; you see the diagram of this drawn below.

Two vertical representations of a line segment. The left diagram shows a straight vertical line with points labeled a, c, d, b from top to bottom. The right diagram shows a zigzag line connecting the same points a, c, d, and b, demonstrating how a line can be 'bent' while maintaining the lengths of its segments.

Secondly, let this be called **curved** <term>curved: *Krümbt*, referring to a continuous arc or "crookedness" rather than a sharp angular bend</term>. I again set a line *a. b.* This may be drawn with a compass <note>original: "zirckel"</note> or drawn curved by hand—curving backward at the top and forward at the bottom, or in the opposite direction. In short, let this line be drawn curved according to each person's needs. This is shown here below in these three lines.

Three curved lines labeled 'a' at the top and 'b' at the bottom. The first line on the left is a simple arc. The middle and right lines are S-curves (ogees) of varying curvature, illustrating different ways a line can be 'crooked' or curved.

Thirdly, let this be called **turning** <term>turning: *Wenden*, referring to rotation or pivoting around a center point</term>. When two lines—one being *a. b.* and the other *c. d.*—lie upon one another, they appear as a single line. Next, place a point *e* in the middle. At this point *e*, turn one line at the top or bottom toward whichever side you wish. And although their four ends <note>original: "örter" — literally "places," referring to the endpoints of the lines</note> *a. c.* and *b. d.* move away from each other, the two lines still remain joined at the point *e*, as is drawn in the following figure.

Two diagrams illustrating 'Wenden' (turning or rotation). The left diagram shows two vertical lines positioned exactly on top of each other, labeled 'a' and 'c' at the top, and 'd' and 'b' at the bottom. The right diagram shows the two lines intersecting at a central point 'e', forming an 'X' shape. The endpoints are labeled 'a', 'c' at the top and 'd', 'b' at the bottom.

<summary>Dürer defines three fundamental types of movement—bending at joints, curving of the spine or limbs, and rotation at a pivot point—to provide a geometric basis for artistic posing.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, bending, curving, turning, rotation, Albrecht Dürer, Underweysung der Messung, anatomical movement</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Dürer’s geometric analysis of movement. Having previously defined angular bending, curving, and pivoting, he now concludes his list of transformations with winding, stretching, and shifting—the final tools an artist needs to transform a static, "stiff" diagram into a dynamic human figure.</meta>

B ij <note>This is a "signature mark," used by early printers to keep the pages of a book in the correct order during binding.</note>

Fourthly, let this be called **winding** <term>winding: *winden*, describing a helical or spiral movement like the threads of a screw</term>. This occurs when two lines, *a. b.* and *c. d.*, are wound around each other in a screw-like fashion, as the following figure informs you.

A diagram showing two lines intertwined in a spiral or double-helix pattern, crossing each other in the center. The endpoints of the lines are labeled with the letters 'c' and 'a' at the top and 'b' and 'd' at the bottom.

Fifthly, let this be called **stretched and contracted** <term>stretched and contracted: *gestreckt und gekrüpfft*, referring to the lengthening of a limb through extension or its shortening through compression</term>. This occurs when a line is lengthened by being drawn out, or shortened by being folded into itself. I can give you no better way to understand this than by placing two lines next to each other: a long one, *a. b.*, and a short one, *c. d.*

A diagram featuring two vertical straight lines of different lengths. The left line is longer and labeled 'a' at the top and 'b' at the bottom. The right line is shorter and labeled 'c' at the top and 'd' at the bottom.

Sixthly, let this be called **shifting** <term>shifting: *geschoben*, the lateral displacement or sliding of parts relative to one another</term>. As you already know, when two lines lie exactly on top of one another, they appear as one line. But when they are shifted away from each other so that they no longer touch, then there are two. From this, parallel lines <note>original: "par linien"</note> or other lines that converge may be drawn, shifted either up or down—that is, one may push or slide them however one wishes. This is indicated below by the two lines *a. b.* and *c. d.*

A diagram consisting of two sets of vertical lines illustrating shifting. The left set shows two parallel vertical lines close together, labeled 'a' and 'c' at the top and 'b' and 'd' at the bottom. The right set shows two vertical lines that are parallel at the top but diverge slightly towards the bottom, also labeled 'a' and 'c' at the top and 'b' and 'd' at the bottom.

<summary>Dürer completes his geometric theory of motion, defining how lines—representing human limbs—can be spiraled, lengthened, shortened, or displaced to create lifelike poses.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, Albrecht Dürer, winding, stretching, shifting, screw-line, human proportion, artistic theory</keywords>

--- Page 267 ---
<meta>This page introduces the first three of six geometric transformations that Dürer uses to manipulate forms. While the previous page (B ij) concluded with winding, stretching, and shifting, this text establishes the foundational movements: bending at joints, continuous curving, and pivoting at a center point. Together, these six principles allow a draughtsman to "pose" a geometric figure as if it were a living body.</meta>

Firstly, I will take the **bent** <term>bent: *gepogen*, referring here to a line broken into segments at specific angles, much like a hinged limb</term>. Set a straight line *a. b.* and give it two joints or points *c. d.* At these two points *c. d.*, bend the line back and forth however you wish. However, the lengths between *a. c.*, *c. d.*, and *d. b.* must always remain straight; you can see its figure drawn <note>original: *auffgerissen*, literally "torn open," a period term for a technical drawing or architectural plan</note> below.

<image-desc>Two diagrams of lines. On the left, a straight vertical line is marked with points a (top), c, d, and b (bottom). On the right, the same line is shown bent at points c and d into a zig-zag shape while the individual segments remain straight.</image-desc>

Secondly, let this be called **curved** <term>curved: *Krümbt*, a continuous, fluid line as opposed to an angular bend</term>. Again, I set a line *a. b.* which may be drawn with a compass <term>compass: *zirckel*, the essential tool for geometric precision</term> or drawn curved by hand—curving backward at the top and forward at the bottom, or vice versa. In short, let this line be drawn curved according to each person's necessity. This is shown here below in these three lines.

<image-desc>Three diagrams of curved lines. The first on the left is a simple curve marked a at the top and b at the bottom. The middle and right lines are S-curves (ogees) of differing curvature, also marked a and b.</image-desc>

Thirdly, let this be called **turning** <term>turning: *Wenden*, a pivoting or intersecting motion where two lines share a central axis</term>. When two lines—one being *a. b.* and the other *c. d.*—lie upon one another, they appear as a single line. Next, place a point *e.* in the middle. At this point *e.*, turn one line at the top or bottom toward whichever side you wish. And although their four ends <note>original: *örter*, literally "places" or "locations," used here to mean endpoints</note>—*a. c.* and *b. d.*—move away from one another, the two lines still remain joined on top of each other at point *e.*, as is drawn in the following figure.

<image-desc>Two diagrams illustrating intersecting lines. On the left, two lines overlap perfectly, labeled with points a and c at the top, e in the middle, and b and d at the bottom. On the right, the same two lines intersect at point e to form an X shape, with the endpoints labeled a, c, b, and d.</image-desc>

<summary>Dürer defines the first three methods of geometric transformation—bending at points, curving, and pivoting—providing diagrams to show how straight lines can be manipulated into complex shapes.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, line transformations, bending, curving, pivoting, Albrecht Dürer, technical drawing, proportion</keywords>

<sig>B ij</sig>

Fourthly, let it be called **winding** <term>winding: *winden*, referring to a helical or spiral twisting like the threads of a screw</term>, where two lines, *a. b.* and *c. d.*, are wound around one another in the manner of a screw <note>original: *schrauffens weyß*</note>, just as the following figure informs you.

<image-desc>A diagram to the right of the text illustrates "winding." Two lines, labeled 'a' and 'c' at the top and 'b' and 'd' at the bottom, are intertwined in a spiral or screw-like double helix pattern.</image-desc>

Fifthly, let it be called **stretched and shrunk** <term>shrunk: *krüpfft*, referring to the shortening of a line through compression or contraction</term>. This occurs when a line is pulled apart to be lengthened, or curled into itself and shortened. I can give you no better way to understand this than by placing two lines side-by-side: a long one, *a. b.*, and a short one, *c. d.*

<image-desc>A diagram to the right of the text illustrates "stretched" and "shrunk" (compressed). It shows two vertical lines side-by-side: a long line on the left labeled 'a' (top) and 'b' (bottom), and a shorter line on the right labeled 'c' (top) and 'd' (bottom).</image-desc>

Sixthly, let it be called **shifted** <term>shifted: *geschoben*, a sliding displacement</term>. As you already know, when two lines lie directly on top of one another, they appear as a single line. However, when they are shifted apart so that they no longer touch, they become two. From this, parallel lines <term>parallel lines: *bar linien*, literally "pair lines," an early term for lines that maintain an equal distance</term> or other lines that converge may be drawn, shifted up or down—pushed or slid however one wishes. This is indicated below by the two lines *a. b.* and *c. d.*

<image-desc>A diagram to the right of the text illustrates "shifted" (pushed). It features two pairs of vertical lines. The left pair shows two lines 'a-b' and 'c-d' positioned extremely close together to appear as one. The right pair shows the same two lines shifted apart to be clearly distinct.</image-desc>

<summary>This page concludes the explanation of six geometric transformations, detailing how lines can be wound into spirals, lengthened or shortened, and shifted to create parallel or diverging forms.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, winding, stretching, shrinking, shifting, parallel lines, technical drawing, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 269 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of geometric transformations—bending, curving, turning, winding, stretching/shrinking, and shifting—moving from their application on simple lines to their complex application on the human body and artistic figures.</meta>

You should know that just as these aforementioned things occur with lines, they also occur in a flat object; and just as they happen in flat objects, they also happen in every soft body <term>body: *corpus*, referring to a three-dimensional form</term> that is wide, thick, and mobile. For such a body can be bent, curved, turned, wound, stretched, shrunk, and shifted in the same way as lines, because no body exists without lines. Therefore, all the things determined above can be used in a body just as they are in lines. Now note for what purposes these six aforementioned distinctions are to be used in the human figure.

First, the word **bent** <term>bent: *gebogen*</term> is used for human limbs—though not meaning that the bones themselves are bent, for they would break; for this reason, I have placed the figure earlier showing that the lines between the points [the joints] should remain straight.

Secondly, **curving** <term>curving: *krümmen*</term> is to be used in the human body within the veins and the flesh; for when a person leans to one side, backwards or forwards, then the veins and the flesh curve either inward or outward.

Thirdly, **turning** <term>turning: *wenden*</term> is used in human limbs; such as when someone turns their head around, and likewise the torso, the arms, thighs, and most parts of the limbs may be turned in this way.

Fourthly, **winding** <term>winding: *winden*</term> is used in the human body in the flesh and veins; for if you have turned a part forward, then all soft things wind from one end to the other, whether it be in the neck, the torso, the arms, or the thighs.

Fifthly, note that **stretching** <term>stretching: *strecken*</term> and **shrinking** <term>shrinking: *krüpffen*, referring to the compression or bunching of tissue</term> are to be used in the human body. Stretching makes a part thin, but shrinking makes it thick; take heed of this. If you stretch out an arm, the veins pull tight and stretch the flesh lengthwise, so the arm becomes thinner. But if you bend the arm, the flesh bunches up <note>original: *krüpfft sich*</note> over itself, and the arm becomes thicker; it is the same in other parts of the body as well.

Sixthly, **shifting** <term>shifting: *schieben*</term> is used in the human body sometimes in the limbs, such that one part slides in front of, behind, or next to another; or when one limb is bent toward another, or the body is curved, the flesh then shifts outward or inward or to one side.

These six different things, as stated above, may well all happen at once in a human body through movement. Thus the limbs bend, turn, and shift, and alongside this, the flesh and other parts of the body curve, wind, stretch, and shrink. Thus one thing pushes the other, as is commonly found in nature.

From these described things, you may give your figures <term>figures: *bildern*, referring here to artistic representations or statues</term> whatever posture <term>posture: *geberd*, or gesture</term> you wish, according to what serves your purpose, provided only that it is consistent with nature. For it is especially to be noted that anything excessive looks bad; therefore, one should do neither too much nor too little of these things, or the posture will become either too fierce or too sluggish—unless someone purposefully intended to make bold or graceful <note>original: *schleyffrige*, literally "slippery," here meaning smooth or flowing</note> figures. This is to be used for men, women, children, horses, and other creatures.

It is to be known that all my previously described figures, when they are bent here and there, do not remain at the same thickness and width in all parts or at all ends, because movement takes from one part and gives to another; therefore, these things change. The clearest way to learn this is through much drawing from living people <note>original: *ab machens lebendiger menschen*</note>, for there one sees how all things happen. Therefore, let everyone take care that he does not lead his work astray.

<summary>Dürer explains how the six geometric transformations applied to lines also apply to the anatomy of the human body, specifically how movement affects flesh, veins, and limbs. He emphasizes that artists must observe nature closely to ensure that gestures and changes in body thickness remain realistic.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, artistic theory, movement, transformation, bending, winding, stretching, life drawing, nature</keywords>

--- Page 270 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how to manipulate a human figure in space. Having defined the types of movement (bending, turning, etc.) on the previous page, the author now provides a practical geometric method for sculptors and painters to represent these movements by encasing body parts in simplified geometric solids.</meta>

<sig>B iij</sig>

Likewise, if one wishes to bend a figure, there are many ways, but one that is particularly useful for sculptors <term>sculptors: *bildhawer*, literally "image-hewers"</term> is to take the principal parts of the body between the horizontal lines of the total proportions and place or "set" them into star-shaped or angular bodies <term>bodies: *corpus*, referring here to three-dimensional geometric solids like blocks or polyhedra</term>. This method is used for **bossing** <term>bossing: *bossiern*, the process of modeling a figure in a soft material like wax or clay before carving</term>, as they call it. Just as the head is set into such a block, so too are the neck and the torso, piece by piece, down to the waist; from there to the end of the hip; then the thighs down to the knee; likewise the shins and the feet. The same is done with the arms. For in every angular body, all parts, points, and lines of the physical form may be very easily indicated through vertical and horizontal parallel lines to find where each feature belongs. However, such "star-blocks" do not always need to be perfectly square or cubic, but may be elongated and shaped according to the requirements of the body, with their corners directed so that they provide a help to the understanding.

But to demonstrate how one may bend and turn the previously described figures as mentioned before, I will take for my instruction the first described male figure from the first book. I will begin first with the head, leaving it upright, but turning it to one side. To do this, do as follows: Take the "pressed-down" ground plan <note>This refers to a top-down view or architectural footprint of the head.</note> of the face and turn the front part toward you and slightly to one side, so that the **squaring** <term>squaring: *vierung*, the square frame or grid used to plot the head's dimensions</term> of the ground plan stands almost diagonally. As the flattened square is marked at its four corners with the numbers 1, 2, 3, and 4, you must project vertical lines upward from these four points as far as you need. Thus, you will find how wide this turned square becomes when viewed diagonally.

Afterward, cut across these four vertical lines with a long horizontal line marked **r. y.** and place the upright, forward-facing face within its own square grid next to the ground plan. Then, transfer all the horizontal lines of the forward-facing face across the four vertical lines that were drawn upward from the four corners of the ground plan. In this new grid, created by the horizontal lines across the length, you will find the positions for the forehead, eyebrows, eyes, nose, ears, mouth, and chin. Then, project vertical parallel lines upward from the ground plan—from all corners and edges of the nose, eyes, mouth, chin, ears, forehead, neck, head, and whatever else is necessary—into the upper grid, between the horizontal lines where each thing is to be found. Where the locations and endpoints of each feature are met, one should then draw the shape of the face. In this way, the **foreshortening** <term>foreshortening: *fürwendung*, the representation of an object as shorter or narrower than it is to create the illusion of depth</term> will be quite correct. This requires great diligence to do properly. To show this more clearly, I have drawn such a turned face below.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for manipulating the human head in three-dimensional space. While the previous page focused on turning the head to the side, Dürer now moves to a more complex operation: tilting the head backward (foreshortening) and projecting that movement from a profile view into a frontal view using a coordinate grid.</meta>

In the same way as I turned the aforementioned face to one side, I will now, from the side, point the chin upwards and tilt the head backwards, and then see how it appears from the front. This is how it is to be done: Take the square <note>original: *vierung*, referring here to the geometric frame or bounding box of the head</note> of the side-viewed <note>original: *nebensichtigenn*</note> face with all its contents, and place it at the back on the lower corner upon a horizontal line <term>horizontal line: *zwerch lini*</term> **x-y**. At the bottom, with the front corner at the chin, direct it upwards. After that, draw at the upper corner of this square a horizontal line **k-ß** against the lower line **r-n**. Between these two horizontal lines, the two corners of the side-viewed square must be captured diagonally <note>original: *vber ort*, a geometric term meaning across the corners or at an angle</note>.

Thereafter, create on the side of the profile face, between the two horizontal lines, the upward-turned face in a front view <note>original: *fürsichtig*</note> in its width, just as was described before. To do this: Draw between the two horizontal lines with two vertical lines <term>vertical lines: *auffrechten linien*</term> **a-b** the width of the front-facing face, and draw within it the other vertical lines **c, d, e, f, g, h, i, k** as they were described before, between which all features are divided according to their width.

Afterwards, using a transfer <note>original: *vbertrag*, the method of projecting points from one drawing to another using parallel lines</note> from the side-viewed tilted face with horizontal lines, pull everything across into the square of the front-viewed face, starting from the height of the head...

<summary>Dürer explains the geometric method for drawing a head tilted backwards by projecting points from a tilted profile grid into a frontal grid. This demonstrates his pioneering use of descriptive geometry to handle complex foreshortening in art.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, foreshortening, human proportion, geometry, profile view, frontal view, coordinate system, Albrecht Dürer</keywords>

--- Page 272 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical explanation of how to project the human head using a coordinate system. Having previously established how to tilt the head back, he now explains how these same principles of intersecting lines and "ground-plans" (perspective grids) apply to various angles, including downward tilts and the entire human figure.</meta>

...of the forehead, eyebrows, nose, ears, mouth, chin, neck, and whatever else is necessary. From these crossed, intersecting lines <term>intersecting lines: *durch schnydenen linien*</term>, you will be instructed where you should draw the contour lines of all the features in the face differently than you did before. Therefore, pay close attention when drawing the shape of the face to where all things fall. And in the same way as these methods are used for the upright face, they are also used for the downward-tilted face <note>original: *vndersich biegenn*, referring to the head bowing forward</note>.

But to make this clearer to understand, I have drafted below both types of faces: the upright and the downward-tilted. These two—the profile <note>original: *nebensichtige*</note> and the frontal <note>original: *fürsichtige*</note> faces—whether upright or tilted, may also be turned to one side. In both cases, a transformation <note>original: *verendung*</note> occurs that would look strange in the drawing. For this, however, the flattened ground-plans <term>ground-plans: *nider gedruckten grundt*, the two-dimensional grids used to map three-dimensional objects in perspective</term> must serve both parts, just as they now fall from the upright and tilted faces. Great skill must be used in such a shifting of the ground-plans, for these things reveal themselves very accurately within them. And so one may proceed with all kinds of previously described faces, of both men and women—and not only with the face, but with the entire body.

And in the same way as the head is placed into a flattened ground-plan, the whole body may also be placed into a flattened ground-plan where it is required. But before this is done, one should first tilt the figure and place it in a proper pose <term>pose: *bossen*, from the Italian *bozza*, referring here to the physical attitude or "stance" of a figure</term> just as one wishes to have it. Only then should one project downward from all points and ends with upright, parallel lines <term>parallel lines: *bar linien*</term> into the flattened ground-plan.

<summary>Albrecht Dürer explains how his geometric "ground-plan" method allows artists to accurately draw the human head and body from any angle, including difficult foreshortened views. He emphasizes that the same grid-based projection used for the face can be applied to the entire human figure in various poses.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, foreshortening, Albrecht Dürer, human proportion, geometry, ground-plan, profile view, frontal view, anatomy</keywords>

--- Page 273 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical instructions on the geometric construction of the human figure. Having just explained how to tilt the head in isolation, he now expands these principles to "bending" the entire body—both male and female—using horizontal projection lines to ensure anatomical consistency across different views.</meta>

And so that one may understand more clearly how the figures may be bent and how they appear when made so, I will first bend the previously described man of seven head-lengths at the points where he is to be bent, as mentioned before. And take the head of the previously described man bowed downward <note>original: *vndersich gepuckt*</note> and place the profile view <note>original: *nebensichtig*</note> onto the frontal figure; thus his face will be turned toward his left side. But the frontal face I place upon the profile body, for a single man must be drafted <term>drafted: *auffgerissen*, the process of laying out the geometric construction of a figure</term> in two ways. 

After that, I bend him above the waist <note>original: *weychenn*, referring to the soft part of the torso between the ribs and the hip</note> toward his left side; thus the right shoulder becomes higher than the left. I also bend the back straight along several transverse lines <term>transverse lines: *zwerch linien*, the horizontal grid lines used to map proportions</term> so that the body is not tilted with a completely straight line, and I stretch his left arm slightly in front of the body. But below the belt, I bend his hip slightly toward his left side and let him stand with his left foot under his head. His right leg, however, I stretch slightly toward his right side, and below the knee, I bend the shin slightly backward; thus he must stand on the toes of this foot.

Once the frontal man is first drafted, one then draws the man from the side using the transfer <term>transfer: *vbertrag*, the method of projecting points from one view to another across a grid</term> through the horizontal lines that clearly show where everything finds its place. Within these, one must draw the contour lines anew and differently than they were before.

And in the same way, I also bend the woman of seven head-lengths who belongs with the man described above; however, I do it in the opposite direction, for I direct her face upward. Yet, it is also turned toward the left side, as with the man before. And just as the man is bent toward his left side, I bend the woman toward her right side, but with a feminine gesture <note>original: *geberd*</note>, and herein I use the transfer from one view into the other, as previously mentioned. 

And I have drafted these above-described figures in the following pages for you to follow, demonstrating that anyone may use these things differently and according to their own pleasure. And note especially that, along with the bending, you may turn the parts within any horizontal line as much as is possible toward whichever side you wish. Understand it thus: if the man is bent with his left shoulder down toward the hip, you may also turn that same shoulder backward and the right one forward, so that the torso moves along with them. This can be done in all parts of the body; you may use all of this even further than what is described here.

<summary>Dürer explains how to "pose" or bend the human figure by manipulating geometric construction lines, demonstrating how to maintain proportional accuracy when turning the torso, shoulders, and limbs in both male and female figures.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, Proportionslehre, human proportion, figure drawing, geometry, anatomy, frontal view, profile view, transverse lines, projection, artistic technique</keywords>

--- Page 274 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s instructions on posing the human figure. Having just finished the specific placement of the seven-head-tall figure, he now moves to the "ten-head-length" proportions—his tallest, most slender model—to demonstrate how to pivot and balance the body from a side view (profile) to ensure realistic movement.</meta>

Now that I have shown a little of how to bend the frontal figures <term>frontal figures: *fürsichtigen bild*</term> to both sides, I will hereafter teach how to bend them from the side view, both forward and backward. To do this, one must first undertake the making of the figure from the side. For this reason, just as I previously used the thick, strong man and his wife from the first book, I will now also bend the last man and woman of ten head-lengths <note>Dürer uses "head-lengths" as the standard unit of proportion; a ten-head figure is exceptionally tall and slender compared to his more common eight-head models.</note> from that same book, so that one may see the difference between those that are too thick and short and those that are too long and thin.

First, I place this aforementioned ten-head-tall man standing straight on his right foot, but I pull his left thigh backward—so that a portion of the buttocks moves with it—and bend the shin backward at the knee, so that he must stand on the toes of that same foot. After that, I begin at the horizontal line of the navel and bend his spine backward from point to point in an arc. Then, at the height of the pit of the neck <note>original: *halsgrübleins*, referring to the suprasternal notch at the base of the throat</note>, I come forward again so that I leave the vertical line <term>vertical line: *auffrecht lini*, the central axis or plumb line used to maintain a figure's center of gravity</term> upon which the man stands behind, and then I bend his neck well forward along with the head, which is inclined downward and hangs forward; for the back left foot and the right arm, which is stretched backward, ensure that he remains in balance <note>original: *in der wag*, literally "in the scale" or balanced.</note>.

However, I will bend the woman next to her previously described husband in an opposite manner in several respects, namely as follows: from the waist <note>original: *weychē*, the soft part of the torso between the ribs and the pelvis</note> upward, I bend her backward from end to end as far as the nape of the neck, and I also stretch her right arm backward. After that, again from the waist down to the end of the hip, I bend her buttocks backward. Then, from the hip joint, I push her upper right leg with the knee forward of the vertical line on which the figure stands. But below the knee, I push the shin backward; thus the woman stands in balance because I stretch her left foot forward so that she stands on its toes.

Afterward, using the transfer method <term>transfer: *vbertrag*, the geometric process of projecting points from one view (like a profile) to another (like a frontal view) to maintain consistent proportions</term> with the horizontal parallel lines, I draw all the high and low points of the changed parts from the profile of the bent woman's body into the frontal figure. Then I create all the widths of the body as described before and bend her hip slightly toward the right side; thus a different form is produced from it.

After that, I draw the contour lines of the woman again into the straight-drawn, foreshortened lines <note>original: *verkersten linien*, lines adjusted to show the effect of perspective or twisting</note>, as you see drafted <term>drafted: *auffgerissen*</term> hereafter. I write all this to you as an example so that you may bend the figures to your liking in ways other than what is shown here; for one may pose them sitting, lying, or in whatever way life allows, using them in all sorts of ways. And just as I have applied this method of bending to these two figures—the thick-short one and the long-thin one—so may all kinds of previously described figures be bent and treated as described before.

->A decorative calligraphic monogram consisting of two large interlaced circular loops, resembling stylized letters like 'S' and 'D' or 'C' intertwined.<-

He emphasizes that these geometric principles allow an artist to depict the human body in any natural pose, such as sitting or lying down.</summary>
