--- Page 295 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical manual on human proportion, moving from static measurements to the geometric principles of movement—how to manipulate the "blocks" of the human body in space.</meta>

Next, I will look a little further into how I might bend, shift, turn, twist, stretch, and shrink <note>original: "krupffen"; meaning to compress or shorten an object in drawing</note> the figures, and through practical application make this properly understood, just as the bending and turning has already been sketched out previously.

And to reach such an understanding more easily, I will make use of two **quadrangular bodies** <note>original: "gefierte corpus"; Dürer uses these to represent segments of the body, like the torso or parts of a limb</note> which shall stand one upon the other. And note, in German, a **quadrangular plane** <note>original: "gefierte ebne"; a two-dimensional square or rectangle</note> should be understood as distinct from a quadrangular body. But it should be considered how these bodies might be handled—how one may pull them apart, stretch, or shrink them—likewise dealing with all other aforementioned things; this I will thus put into practice.

In place of the <term>limbs<gloss>glidmaß</gloss></term>, we will place two quadrangular bodies one upon the other, as stated above, and mark each corner of the two quadrangular planes where the bodies meet, so that they may be understood distinctly. Therefore, mark the four corners of the bottom plane of the upper body with **a, b, c, d**, and the four corners of the top plane of the lower body with **1, 2, 3, 4**. And let the mutual middle or <term>center<gloss>centrum</gloss></term> of both be **o**, where they lie upon one another. This point **o** remains constant, no matter how one turns the other things this way or that.

Now I will sketch a <term>ground plan<gloss>nidergedruckten grund</gloss></term> <note>literally a "pressed-down ground," referring to a bird's-eye view or floor plan</note> of the two bodies standing upon each other beneath them, and mark the corners with letters and numbers, just as the vertical lines falling down from the <term>elevation<gloss>obertrag</gloss></term> <note>the front-facing or standing view in a technical drawing</note> indicate in the ground plan. Thus, **a** and **1** meet in one corner, **b** and **2** in the second, **c** and **3** in the third, and **d** and **4** in the fourth, as I have sketched here alongside.

Now we will turn the upper body slightly to one side, so that no corner remains directly above the other. Do this in the ground plan so that corner **a** moves away from corner **1**, **b** from **2**, **c** from **3**, and **d** from **4**. If you then proceed upward from the shifted corners with vertical lines through the elevation, the upper quadrangular body will appear as it has been turned, as I have sketched next to the nearest ground plan.

This turning or rotating may be used for all transverse lines that I have drawn through the human limbs at every <term>joint<gloss>glenck</gloss></term>, as much as nature will naturally allow.

And it is from this principle that the previously described turned face was made.

<summary>Dürer explains the geometric method for rotating parts of the human body by representing limbs as stacked boxes. By using a ground plan and vertical projections (elevation), he demonstrates how to mathematically calculate the appearance of a limb when it is twisted at a joint.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, geometry, human proportion, rotation, ground plan, elevation, anatomy, perspective, technical drawing</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of geometric block manipulation. On the previous page, Dürer established the base coordinates (a, b, c, d and 1, 2, 3, 4) and the central pivot point 'o'. ->D iij<-

Now, once the previously constructed <note>original: "gefüert"</note> **body** <note>original: "corpus"</note> has been turned to one side, and the corners are moved away from each other, we will furthermore also bend it to one side while it is so turned, so that it does not remain upright. And afterward, we shall lay it again into a <term>ground plan<gloss>nidergedruckten grund</gloss></term> <note>literally a "pressed-down ground," referring to a floor plan or a bird's-eye view used in perspective drawing</note>. However, so that the upper corners of the body may be recognized when one wishes to lay them into a ground plan, we will mark them with **e, f, g, h**, just as the four lower corners were designated with **a, b, c, d**. And when you do this as stated above, it will look like what I have <term>sketched<gloss>auffgerissen</gloss></term> here below, shown from the <term>transfer<gloss>vbertrag</gloss></term> <note>the process of projecting points from a side or front view onto the ground plan</note> with the vertical lines indicated in the ground.

<column-break/>

Now that this body on top of the other has been turned in two ways and bent to one side, we will now, for the third time, bend it once more forward at the top. Yet, let the point **o** always remain fixed in both parts of the bodies, unless it should happen that at times it is necessary to do otherwise for certain things. In this way, the turning and the bending to the side and forward happen together. Afterward, lay all of that into a ground plan through the transfer, as mentioned before; then it will look like what I have sketched below.

<summary>Dürer explains how to geometrically plot a rectangular block that is simultaneously twisted and tilted in two directions (sideways and forward). He introduces a labeling system (e, f, g, h) for the upper corners to help the artist track these points when projecting them onto a two-dimensional ground plan.</summary>

--- Page 297 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical demonstration of manipulating geometric blocks to represent human movement. Following the previous page's focus on tilting and twisting a single block, Dürer now explores the interaction between two stacked blocks—representing segments of the torso or limbs—and how their rigid geometry begins to transition into the organic, "soft" forms of the human body.</meta>

And as was indicated before, when two **squared** <note>original: "gefierte"</note> **bodies** <note>original: "corpora"</note> are placed one upon the other—where the upper body is turned and bent to one side, and may also be bent forward or backward—so too may the lower body be handled. As one wishes, one may let one remain stationary and manipulate the other, or work with both of them together as the specific use requires. 

But when such things are <term>sketched<gloss>auffgerissen</gloss></term> <note>Dürer uses this term for technical projection drawing</note>, their sides, corners, and planes intersect quite strangely in the <term>elevation<gloss>auffrechte grund</gloss></term> and <term>ground plan<gloss>nidergedruckten grund</gloss></term>, just as you see here before your eyes. For at certain points, the bodies merge into one another, while on the opposite side, they move out or away from each other. 

And when such a process is applied to soft things <note>referring to pliable materials or the organic tissues of the human body</note>, some parts stretch, while conversely, other parts crumple and fold over one another. From this, it happens in a finished work that whatever is taken from one part is added to the other, so that one may <note>original: "die lucken auß büßt"</note> fill the gaps <note>This describes Dürer's method of "volume conservation," where he compensates for the voids or overlaps created by the geometric boxes to create a natural-looking anatomical form</note>.

<image-desc>A perspective drawing of two stacked cubes. The top cube is skewed and tilted to the right, intersecting the lower cube at an angle. Points on the top face are labeled with letters 'e', 'b', 'f', 'g'. The intersection points and corners are labeled with numbers '1', '4', '9', '3', '2'. The sides of the lower cube are labeled 'a' and 'c'.</image-desc>

Now it is also to be noted that, in the same way the previously described bodies are turned and bent using straight lines, one may also curve all the lines, sides, and surfaces in a soft body however one wishes. For a clearer understanding of this, I will once more sketch here below the two previously drawn bodies—just as they were turned and bent before—and curve several of their side lines and planes, so that these drawn lines may make the principle clear to you.

<image-desc>A technical diagram showing the ground plan or projection of the two skewed cubes from the previous image. It displays overlapping geometric shapes (quadrilaterals) with various points labeled with letters 'a', 'b', 'c', 'd', 'f', 'g' and numbers '1', '2', '3', '4', '9'. A central point is marked with a small circle.</image-desc>

<summary>Dürer explains how to manipulate two stacked geometric blocks to simulate complex body movements, noting how their intersections must be adjusted when translating rigid shapes into "soft" organic forms. He introduces the concept of curving these geometric lines to better represent the natural contours of the human figure.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, perspective drawing, anatomical modeling, elevation, ground plan, volume compensation, organic forms, projection</keywords>

--- Page 298 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical explanation of how to manipulate geometric volumes—referred to as "bodies"—to simulate the complex movements of the human form. He transitions from simple bending to the concept of "winding" (torsion), explaining how these movements change the relationship between the ground plan and the elevation of a drawing.</meta>

D ij
<meta>Catchword: mey</meta>

As was previously understood regarding the two **squared bodies** <note>original: "gefierten corpora"</note> and the many various ways of bending, curving, and other movements, there remains yet to be understood the **winding** <note>original: "winden"; here referring to torsion or twisting along an axis</note>. This winding is applied to the side lines from corner to corner, and through it, the square planes of the body twist. It is quite a similar matter to what was said before regarding curving, except that this winding moves in a **screw-like fashion** <note>original: "schrauffens weyß"</note>. You may use such winding according to your will, for through it, nothing remains as it was before. If you then lay this altered upper square with its winding into the <term>ground plan<gloss>grundt</gloss></term>, the flattened ground plan will appear different than before, just as I have sketched below.

For as often as any object that is <term>elevated<gloss>auffgezogen</gloss></term> is shifted, it creates a different ground plan. And let this also be noted: if one wishes to **twist** a thing, it must be done in the flattened ground plan and then elevated. But whatever one **bends** back and forth must be done in the elevation and then laid back into the flattened ground plan.

<image-desc>A technical diagram of a vertical rectangular box shown in perspective. The structure is depicted as being "wound" or twisted, with its vertical edges spiraling slightly. Various points on the structure are labeled with letters (a, b, c, e, f, g) and numbers (1 through 9) to indicate geometric coordinates and the mapping of the "auffgezogen" (elevated) form.</image-desc>

And although I have used these two squared bodies until now, I also intend for other bodies to be used in this same manner. For it happens that one requires long or short, narrow, wide, thick, or squared bodies, or other bodies with more sides, corners, and planes. Those many-sided bodies should be made suitable for the shape they belong to; therefore, they are made with unequal planes, sides, and angles—sometimes large at the top and small at the bottom, and vice versa. Thus, it may well happen that no part of the body is identical to another. He who works in such tasks will find that I do not mention this in vain. However, these prepared bodies are to be handled in the same way as the squared ones mentioned before. Furthermore, this method is to be used with the **entire human body** <note>original: "gantzen menschlichen leyb"</note>, whether it is circumscribed within a many-sided body or not. This practice may be applied to many other things as well. For every physical creature, once it has been sketched out, may have its limbs twisted or bent along the <term>transverse lines<gloss>zwerch linien</gloss></term> <note>These are the cross-sectional lines Dürer uses to define the volume of a limb</note> to one side, backward, or forward. Within these, you may enclose them in square or many-sided bodies and give a figure any pose you wish to lay or set. Therefore, as mentioned before, if one wishes, they may place a previously described figure into squared

or many-sided bodies, and after the figure is enclosed, bend and twist it according to one's mind. From this are found the stretches, knots, curves, and windings, as indicated before, through which the right way is shown. Such a way or method <note>The text ends mid-word with the catchword "mey," which likely begins the word "meynung" (opinion/method) or "meyner" (my) on the following page.</note>

mey

<summary>Dürer explains the geometric principle of "winding" (torsion) and how it differs from simple bending. He describes how complex human movements can be modeled by enclosing body parts in various multi-sided geometric volumes and manipulating them in both ground plan and elevation.</summary>
<keywords>winding, torsion, ground plan, elevation, squared bodies, many-sided bodies, human anatomy, technical drawing, transverse lines</keywords>

--- Page 299 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s instructions on using geometric solids to map the human body. Having previously discussed torsion and "winding," he now explains how these technical methods assist sculptors in accurately removing material from stone or wood by visualizing the body within a series of stacked, squared blocks.</meta>

This information is not useless for **sculptors** <note>original: "Bildhawern"</note> who are beginning to learn, and who wish to make something out of wood or stone, so that they may follow a thing correctly and precisely. For through this, one finds a straight path on all sides to carve through the **angular bodies** <note>original: "ecketté corpora"; 3D geometric shapes used as a guide</note> to remove what needs to go, so that one does not carve away too much or leave too much behind. However, a knowledgeable and well-practiced man does not always need this, unless it is a very large work. But one who is artistically gifted in his understanding **points** <note>original: "punctirt"; refers to the pointing technique used by sculptors to transfer dimensions from a model to a block</note> the previously described figure and then bends it; and only then does he redraw the form lines from point to point into the new change brought about by the shifting. It is difficult to write about these things and even more tedious to read and learn them because of the many words, points, and signs. However, there are many more advantageous ways to use these things; for if that were not so, these things would be very difficult and hard to make. Therefore, whoever searches in these matters will daily find a more direct path, through which these things become easier every day. Thus, it is good for one to have a master to provide daily guidance; but to learn a thing without a master is sixfold the effort and labor.

Now I will put part of what was shown above into practice. For my example, I will take the **nine-heads-long man** <note>referring to a canon of proportions where the body height is equal to nine times the length of the head</note> from the second book, whose head has been lengthened so that he is no longer exactly nine heads long. I will enclose his form lines with nothing but squared or angular bodies, viewed from the side and from the front. Yet these bodies become high or low because of the **transverse lines** <note>horizontal cross-sections</note> that divide them, and the bodies vary because of the human form contained within them. I will divide this entire man's length into ten angular bodies using nine **cuts** <note>original: "schnydten"; cross-sectional slices</note>; no two of these will have the same form. And these shall be the cuts: the transverse line of the chin; the fleshy part of the shoulder; the pit of the throat; the breasts; the flanks; the hip joint; the end of the hip; the bend of the knee; and the bottom of the ankle bone. But so that this may be understood, I have first sketched this aforementioned man with his form lines, and thereafter enclosed him with the straight lines of the squared bodies as seen from the side and front. Thus he stands upright, straight and without any bend, stretching his right arm out from him and letting the left hang down, as you see. But thereafter, on the next page, I will sketch only the squared bodies as they are stacked upon one another, and leave out the man’s form lines. Then I will take these squared bodies as they stand upon one another, within which the figure is embodied, and through a transfer, I will lay them into a **flattened ground plan** <note>original: "nider geduckté grund"; a top-down geometric projection</note>, just as was done before with the head. I have sketched this below the upright version as it appears.

Following this, I have further sketched what form the body takes in each **cut** <note>cross-section</note> as its circumference should be drawn when one looks at its squared plane. This is sought and found from the figure's side and front views; then one sees what is "missing" from the squared plane in each cut. One should also note that where two squared bodies stand upon one another at a cut, the **outline** <note>original: "vmbziehung"</note> of the body on the bottom plane of the upper body is the same as the outline of the body on the top plane of the lower body. For the sake of marking, I have also drawn cross-lines with straight lines into these squared cuts, where the body is individually outlined. This is so that when the squared planes are turned or shifted, the outline of the body can be correctly redrawn. For it often happens that the squared planes become narrow or come to look like **rhombuses** <note>original: "rautens weyß"</note>; in that case, the rounding of the body must be redrawn correctly within them. And just as the squared plane becomes a rhombus, the cross-lines within it must be drawn proportionately. And where you wish to redistribute things that were previously wide but have become narrow, you must use the **transformer** <term><gloss>verkerer</gloss></term> <note>a specialized geometric tool described in Dürer's first book for distorting and scaling proportions</note> from the first book. Thereafter, I have again sketched in a flattened ground plan these outlined body lines as they occur in the cuts outside of the squared planes, showing how they fall upon one another, to demonstrate how much one part overlaps the other.

<vocab>Sculptors, bodies, angular, head-lengths, cuts/sections, outline/contour, transformer</vocab>

Now I wish to examine further how the **squared bodies** <note>original: "gevierten corpora," referring to the anatomical segments treated as geometric blocks</note> of the torso are shifted and no longer remain straight upon one another, and how the outlined shape of the body changes in the drawing on every **plane** <note>original: "plano"</note> in the **cross-section** <note>original: "schnyd"</note>. To demonstrate this clearly, I will take the squared body between the cross-section of the **breasts** <note>original: "tütlein," literally "little teats," used here as an anatomical landmark for the chest</note> and the **flanks** <note>original: "weychen," the soft part of the waist between the ribs and hips</note>. First, I place it upright from the side on a **horizontal line** <note>original: "zwerch lini"</note> and mark the four corners of the breast section: front **.a.b.** and back **.c.d.** Then mark the section of the waist: front **.1.2.** and back **.3.4.** I then place these beneath in a **flattened ground plan** <note>original: "nidergedruckten grundt," what we would today call a plan view or bird's-eye projection</note>, which I also mark with the same letters and numbers as above so that one corresponds to the other.

Afterward, take the side-view upright that stands on the horizontal line and bend it forward at the mark of the spine, and then drop its altered flattened ground plan beneath it, always placing the letters and numbers in their corners. Thus, this body is once bent forward and creates a new ground plan. Once this flattened ground plan is made, then take this plan and turn it toward you from the front at the chest, well onto the right side below the aforementioned horizontal line. Then, from all corners of the letters and numbers of the rotated ground plan, move upward toward and across the horizontal line. Afterward, from the forward-bent upright body, draw lines from all corners of the letters and numbers using a **transfer horizontal** <note>original: "vbertrag zwerch lini"</note> through the vertical lines that were drawn upward from the letters and numbers of the rotated ground plan. Mark these corners where the vertical and horizontal lines of the letters and numbers meet, and thus each corner of the body is found.

Afterward, draw the side lines from corner to corner, and you will see how the body has turned to one side. Thus the aforementioned body is bent forward and to one side. Now take the body that is bent forward and turned to one side, and bend it at the mark of the spine on the horizontal line toward the right side. Then proceed from all corners of the letters...

Now I will further examine how the squared <term>volumes<note>original: "corpora"; referring here to the three-dimensional blocks or segments Dürer uses to simplify the human form</note></term> of the body are shifted and no longer remain straight upon one another, and how the outlined shape of the body changes in a <term>draft<note>original: "auffreyssen"; the process of technical drawing or plotting proportions</note></term> across every plane in the cross-section. To demonstrate this clearly, I will take the squared volume between the cross-section of the **nipples** and the **flanks**. First, I place it upright according to its side on a horizontal line, and mark the four corners of the nipple-section as front **a.b.** and back **c.d.** Then I mark the cross-section of the flank as front **1.2.** and back **3.4.** I lay this beneath in a <term>ground plan<note>original: "nidergedruckten grundt"; a flattened, bird's-eye view of the shape's footprint</note></term>, which I also mark with its letters and numbers as above, so that one corresponds to the other.

Next, take the side upright view that stands on the horizontal line and bend it according to the curve of the **spine** forward and downward. Then, drop its altered ground plan beneath it, always placing the letters and numbers in their respective corners. In this way, this volume is bent forward and creates a new ground plan. Once this flattened ground plan is made, take this plan and turn it toward you from the front at the chest, specifically toward the right side beneath the aforementioned horizontal line. 

Then, draw lines upward from all the corners of the letters and numbers of the rotated ground plan toward and beyond the horizontal line. After that, from the forward-leaning upright volume, draw horizontal lines from all the corners of the letters and numbers so that they intersect the vertical lines coming up from the rotated ground plan. Mark these corners where the vertical and horizontal lines of the letters and numbers meet; in this way, every corner of the volume is found.

Then, draw the side lines to join corner to corner, and you will see how the volume has turned to one side. Thus, the aforementioned volume is bent forward and to one side. <meta>This page continues Albrecht Dürer's technical instructions on how to project three-dimensional volumes onto a two-dimensional plane. On the previous page, Dürer began explaining how to shift and tilt a geometric block representing a segment of the human torso.</meta>

...and numbers of the currently projected, forward-tilting, and side-bent <term>volume<note>original: "corpus"; Dürer uses these geometric blocks to simplify the complex shapes of the human body for perspective drawing</note></term> with vertical lines downward. After that, using the <term>transfer method<note>original: "vbertrag"</note></term>, draw horizontal lines from all the corners of the previously shifted <term>ground plan<note>original: "nidergedruckten grundes"</note></term> through the vertical lines where they intersect at their corresponding letters and numbers. In those same points, connect the sides of the volume in the ground plan; in this way, you will find everything correctly positioned. 

Then, take the thicknesses and widths from the side and the front of the newly created cross-sections of the volume, both at the top and the bottom, and draw the <term>grid lines<note>original: "gitterten linien"</note></term> back into them using the <term>transformer<note>original: "verkerer"; a geometric grid or device Dürer designed to mechanically calculate perspective and distortion</note></term>. After that, draw the contour lines through the grid as they should wrap around the cross-section, in both the projected profile and the ground plan, just as I have drafted in the following illustrations. 

And just as I handle this single volume, so must it be done with all volumes throughout the entire figure. For if one later wishes to represent the figure—once it has been moved—in a <term>foreshortened<note>original: "verkürzt vnd ab gestolln"; literally "shortened and stolen away," a vivid historical description of the optical illusion of depth</note></term> painting, it is necessary to have such a draft beforehand from which the painting can be made.

--- Page 306 ---
<meta>This page continues Dürer’s technical demonstration of how to rotate and tilt a geometric block in three-dimensional space using two-dimensional projections. He moves from general theory to a specific example using the "volume of the head."</meta>

As has been shown earlier in a large <term>volume<note>original: "corpus"</note></term> and block of the torso—specifically the segment between the level of the nipples and the waist—one can manipulate and move through the shifting of all the squared volumes of the body, which normally stand straight atop one another along a vertical line. From this, I will proceed to show its practical use; therefore, we will first bend and turn the squared volumes of the figure one by one, however we wish, and re-apply all the necessary elements previously described. We may pose the figure however we like; another artist might choose a different arrangement.

As previously shown, it must be noted that every squared volume looks different in the <term>projection<note>original: "auffreyssenn"; the elevation or upright drawing</note></term> when it is shifted, appearing differently in both the <term>elevation<note>original: "auff gezognen"</note></term> and the <term>ground plan<note>original: "nidergedruckten grund"</note></term>. For first, a volume is bent either forward or backward; next, one might turn it to a side; and thirdly, tilt it to a side. Therefore, through the aforementioned foundations, these positions are properly sought and correctly found. If I repeat some things already mentioned, it is because necessity requires it, so that I may proceed from the established principles to all other similar matters. I could have left it at the previously shown squaring of the body, but I felt it was not sufficiently clear. 

Therefore, I will begin with the very start of the figure—the squared <term>volume of the head<note>original: "corpus des haubts"</note></term>—and however I handle this, I will do the same for all the other blocks of the figure. Now I begin by labeling the four upper corners of the head's volume with **a, b, c, d** and the four lower corners with **1, 2, 3, 4**, so that **a** aligns with **1**, **b** with **2**, **c** with **3**, and **d** with **4**. I view this first from the side and shift the volume of the head well forward from the vertical line on which the figure stands, bending it forward. From this, I must find its ground plan to determine how much the upper part overlaps the lower. Once this is done, I label it with an **A**. 

Next, I take this first ground plan and place its front toward me, so that I am looking at it from the front, and then I turn its front slightly toward its right side. I then extend vertical lines upward from all the corners marked with letters and numbers. I place the side view of the squared volume of the head—just as it was bent downward—beside these vertical lines. Then, using the <term>transfer method<note>original: "vbertrag"</note></term>, I draw <term>horizontal lines<note>original: "zwerch linien"</note></term> from all the corners of the letters and numbers of the side volume **A** through the vertical lines. Where the corresponding letters and numbers intersect, you will find the corners: the top ones marked with letters and the bottom ones with numbers. 

When you connect these points, you will see how the side-tilted volume—which has now been turned to one side—appears from a <term>frontal view<note>original: "fürwertig"</note></term>, and how its ground plan lies beneath it. This second part of the projection is labeled with a **B**. Now you must also know how this part **B** appears from the side in both the elevation and the ground plan. It is always necessary to know what shape an object takes in a projection when viewed from both the side and the front. The volume is now bent forward and turned to one side, as you see in the elevation and ground plan of **B**, but this has been drawn from a frontal perspective. We must now also know what shape this takes from the side in a projection. Proceed as follows: 

Take the previous side-turned ground plan that you just viewed from the front; turn it again and look at it directly and correctly from the side. Once more, using the transfer method, draw lines upward from all the corners of the letters and numbers of this ground plan. Then, place the previous frontal volume—bent and turned as it is—beside these vertical lines. From all the corners of the letters and numbers of that frontal elevation, draw horizontal lines using the transfer method.

<summary>Dürer explains the step-by-step geometric process of tilting and rotating the "head block" of a human figure. He demonstrates how to use vertical and horizontal projection lines to translate a tilted side view into a frontal perspective.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, perspective, human proportion, projection, ground plan, elevation, Albrecht Dürer, technical drawing, volume of the head</keywords>

--- Page 307 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing the "squared volumes" of the human body in perspective. Dürer is explaining how to project points from a ground plan to an elevation to show a head that is simultaneously tilted, turned, and bent.</meta>

...into the vertical lines that are drawn upwards from the <term>ground plan<note>original: "nider gedruckten grund"; literally "pressed-down ground," referring to the top-down perspective view</note></term>. At the points where the lines of the letters and numbers intersect with one another, place the corresponding letters and numbers. Then, draw the lines connecting these letters and numbers together. In this way, you will see how the turned <term>volume<note>original: "corpus"</note></term> is drawn from the side and how it appears in the ground plan; mark this section with a **C**. 

Now, from this, you must find the third step: so that the <term>volume</term> is inclined forward, turned to one side, and additionally bent to one side. This process was indicated before, but proceed as follows: take the upright <term>volume</term> **B** and bend it to the right side, just as it was previously turned to the right side. Leave the ground plan lying beneath it as it was, though it must now be altered, because the four upper corners—**a, b, c, d**—must fall toward the right side. Consequently, the sides of the lower foundation—**1, 2** and **3, 4**—will become narrower, as will the two upper sides **a, b** and **c, d**. 

This is done as follows: from the <term>transfer<note>original: "vbertrag"; the method of projecting points from one drawing to another</note></term>, draw vertical lines down from the four upper corners **a, b, c, d** into the ground plan. There you will see where you should intersect and extend the lines **a, b** and **c, d** in the ground plan. This is indicated by the lines of the letters drawn downward from the elevation. One proceeds similarly with lines drawn down from the corners of the numbers of the upright foundation; these will also show you how much narrower the sides of the ground plan—**1, 2** and **3, 4**—become compared to how they were before. Thus, you see how much the upper part in the ground plan shifts to one side according to the upright portion; mark this part with a **D**.

Fifthly, you must also know how this would appear from the side in an <term>elevation<note>original: "auffreyssen"; an architectural or technical drawing showing a vertical projection</note></term>. To do this: take this ground plan **D** and position it to be viewed from the side, just as the ground plan **C** was positioned before. From all the corners of the letters and numbers, project lines upward using the transfer. Afterward, draw <term>horizontal lines<note>original: "zwerch linien"</note></term> through the upright ones from volume **D**. Where the letters and numbers meet one another with their lines, place points and add their corresponding letters and numbers. Then, draw the lines together. These will show you how the <term>volume</term> appears from the side in the elevation. Mark this with an **E**. 

Thus, the first two parts—the front view and the side view—are ready for something to be made from them.

Afterward, take the second squared <term>volume</term> in which the neck is formed. Handle it exactly as you did with the aforementioned volume of the head. However, bend it slightly less forward, and similarly turn it slightly less to the right, and bend it less to the right side than the upper volume of the head is bent. Mark its four corners with **a, b, c, d** and the lower ones with **1, 2, 3, 4**. 

In this way, you will see in the <term>elevation</term> how the foundations are drawn up or pressed down, especially in the last two that are to be used. You should also do this in the subsequent <term>volumes<note>original: "corpora"</note></term>: always bend the lower volumes less forward, turn them less to the right, and bend them less to the right than the one placed above it. This is done so that all these parts are used in a proper, skillful succession. Through this, you can see how the body winds to the right side, inclining both to the side and forward. 

However, when you come to the fifth squared volume—between the level of the <term>nipples<note>original: "tütlein"</note></term> and the <term>waist<note>original: "weychen"; the soft part of the torso or flanks</note></term>—let that stand upright, though turn it slightly to the right. Prepare its necessary foundations; for this, one needs no more than three types of foundations: the side-view first for the sake of the ground plan, which is then turned forward a second time and drawn up. After that, one makes the third foundation, showing how it is turned...

<summary>Dürer provides a step-by-step geometric method for projecting a tilted and turned head into a two-dimensional drawing using ground plans and elevations. He also explains that as one moves down the body (neck to waist), the degree of rotation and tilt should decrease to create a naturalistic "winding" or contrapposto effect.</summary>
<keywords>perspective projection, elevation, ground plan, squared volumes, human proportion, geometry in art, Albrecht Dürer, torso anatomy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for constructing anatomical volumes in perspective. The previous page ended while describing the projection of lines from an elevation into a ground plan to represent a complex, tilted pose.</meta>

...is to be seen on the side. I also mark these three <term>ground plans<note>original: "grund"; here referring to the top-down perspective view or footprint of a three-dimensional object</note></term> with the letters **C**, **D**, and **E**. I do not begin with **A**, as I did above, so that in the following practical application, the <term>volumes<note>original: "corpus"; Dürer treats parts of the human body as geometric blocks to simplify the rendering of perspective and rotation</note></term> **D** and **E** will always align with one another throughout the entire image. 

Furthermore, if you wish to turn a distorted <term>ground plan</term>—one that is already angled forward—onto its side, then enclose that same distorted shape within a proper <term>square<note>original: "vierung"; a bounding box or framing square used as a geometric reference</note></term>. Then, turn the square along with the object onto its side; in this way, you will not lose your orientation. Alternatively, you may determine the position using a vertical line: see whether the sides of the object form a wide or narrow angle against the drawn line, and apply this to the side view, just as you previously did for the front view using a <term>horizontal line<note>original: "zwerchlini"</note></term>. 

Next, take the sixth <term>squared volume<note>original: "geſiert corpus"</note></term>, located between the cut of the <term>waist<note>original: "weychen"; literally the "soft parts" or flanks of the torso between the ribs and the pelvis</note></term> and the **navel**. Shift or bend the bottom of it forward at the level of the navel, and create its <term>ground plan</term>. This first plan shall be the final one in the sequence; therefore, I mark it with the letter **E**. The <term>forward-facing ground plan<note>original: "fürſichtigen grund"</note></term>, however, shall be marked with **D**. This <term>volume</term> requires no more than these two ground plans, because it is bent only once. 

Afterward, take this <term>side-view ground plan<note>original: "nebēſichtigē nider gedrucktē grund"</note></term> with all its markings and turn it so that it is viewed from the front. Then, draw this ground plan upward again and proceed with the <term>transfer<note>original: "vbertrag"; the method of projecting points from one view to another to create a coherent three-dimensional representation</note></term> as previously described; thus, all things will be found just as clearly indicated before.

<margin>The squared volume belongs to the head.<note>Dürer's method involves breaking the human figure into "squared volumes" or cuboids. While the main text discusses the torso and navel, this marginal note reminds the reader that the same geometric principle applies to the head.</note></margin>

--- Page 313 ---
<meta>This page serves as the conclusion to Albrecht Dürer’s "Four Books on Human Proportion," published posthumously in 1528. It includes Dürer's own closing remarks followed by an editorial note regarding his death and the publication process.</meta>

Whoever now undertakes and follows this instruction of mine, first forming a correct <term>proportion</term><note>Dürer refers here to the mathematical relationship between the different parts of the human body, a central theme of his work.</note> through measurement, then placing them in an orderly fashion and establishing them in the ground plan, then drawing them up and bringing them into <term>perspective</term>, and thus skillfully transferring them into the painting or work—he will soon realize what usefulness may follow for him from this. Without a doubt, he will find much more than is indicated or reported here. Notwithstanding that this instruction of mine may be considered difficult in some places, it is nonetheless true that what is hard to understand will not be learned without effort and diligence. And with that, my gracious lord, I will for this time bring my writing to an end; and if God grants it in His time, I shall write further on what belongs to the art of painting, so that such art might not rest upon practice alone, but may also, in time, be learned and understood through a correct and orderly foundation, to the praise of God and for the use and pleasure of all art lovers.

A decorative calligraphic flourish or knot consisting of two interlocking circular loops.

Although the pious and artistic Albrecht Dürer wrote these four books, he only revised and corrected the first one. Before he could reach the other three, the swiftness of death overtook him <note>Dürer died on April 6, 1528, just before the full work could be published.</note>, so that he was unable to review them. It is quite possible that, had he been given time, he might have changed, increased, or decreased certain things. Nevertheless, his good friends considered it more useful that the other three books also be issued uncorrected, rather than allowing them to remain suppressed—regardless of whether there was anything in them to be improved (though they do not believe there was). If God had granted him a longer life, he would have brought to light many more wonderful, rare, and artistic things, serving especially the art of painting in landscapes, colors, and the like. He also specifically intended to describe and publish a longer and clearer perspective than he had done before. But God, who ordains all things for the best, prevented this not without cause; may His name be praised and honored eternally.

->Printed in Nuremberg by Hieronymus Formschneyder<-
->at the expense of Albrecht Dürer's bereaved widow in the year since<-
->the birth of Christ, 1528, on the last day of October.<-

A decorative calligraphic flourish or knot, identical to the one above, consisting of two interlocking circular loops.

<summary>This page concludes Dürer's landmark treatise on human proportion, featuring his final exhortation to artists and an editorial note explaining that the work was completed by his friends after his death in 1528.</summary>
<keywords>Albrecht Dürer, Nuremberg, Hieronymus Formschneyder, Proportion, Perspective, 1528, Painting, Art Instruction, Posthumous publication</keywords>

<meta>This page contains the official Imperial Privilege granted by Emperor Charles V to Agnes Dürer, the widow of Albrecht Dürer. It follows the editorial note about Dürer’s death, providing the legal protection necessary for his widow to publish his final, unfinished work on human proportion without the threat of intellectual theft or unauthorized reprinting.</meta>

<sig>Z iiij</sig>
<meta>catchword: ver</meta>

<image-desc>A large, elaborate ornamental calligraphic initial 'W' at the top left of the page, featuring intricate swirls and flourishes typical of 16th-century manuscript or early print decoration.</image-desc>

**W**E CHARLES the fifth, by the grace of God elected Roman Emperor, at all times
increaser of the Empire <note>original: "merer des Reychs." A traditional title for the Holy Roman Emperor, from the Latin *semper Augustus*.</note>, etc. In Germany, in Spain, both Sicilies, Jerusalem, Hun-
gary, Dalmatia, Croatia, etc. King; Archduke of Austria, Duke of Burgundy,
etc. Count of Habsburg, Flanders, and Tyrol, etc. We publicly acknowledge with this letter
and make known to everyone: Whereas the honorable, our dear and devout Agnes, the
surviving widow of the late Albrecht Dürer, has brought before our Imperial government in the Holy
Empire how the aforementioned Albrecht, her husband, brought together several books on <term>human
proportion</term><note>The mathematical study of the proportions of the human body, which Dürer revolutionized.</note> out of true art and <term>perspective</term> by his own diligence and invention, with no
small effort, and had the intention to bring these same books into print,
so that they might have been useful and beneficial to those who love such art; and yet,
the same Dürer died recently and before the completion of such art books; and now she,
the widow, has had some of these books cut into forms <note>This refers to the woodblock printing process, where images and text were carved into wooden blocks.</note> and is willing to print them.
However, some have presumed to take the art discovered by her husband and some of
such human proportion behind her back through improper ways, bringing it into print
alongside others under their own title, and as if they, and not Dürer, had discovered it.
Furthermore, she has reason to fear (as often happens when something is discovered with such diligence,
effort, and labor, and brought into print for the common good, that
others immediately reprint it and thus seek and conduct their own profit in an unfair way, to the
detriment of the inventor) that if she has the discovered art of her late husband
printed, others would immediately reprint it to her significant disadvantage, if this were
not prevented. And thereupon she has humbly called upon us and asked us to
appear with gracious provision and help. Therefore, so that the aforementioned art and
invention of Dürer is not withheld, and also so that the aforementioned widow
is not defrauded or brought to harm in this matter, We have given the same widow
this following <term>privilege</term><note>A "freyheyt" or privilege was an early form of copyright protection granted by a monarch.</note>. We give it to her also herewith by Roman Imperial power, knowingly
and by virtue of this letter, in such a way that no one, whoever they may be, shall bring into print,
or publish, or sell the aforementioned discovered art and books of the late Albrecht Dürer,
or any excerpt from them, in his own name and as if he had made them, or otherwise,
without the consent of the said widow. Also, that no one shall reprint the aforementioned books, art, and proportion
of his said widow, if she has brought them or has had them brought into print, for ten years
immediately following the publication of the same print, nor sell them so reprinted.
And thereupon We command all and every one of Our and the Empire's subjects,
and especially all painters, book-printers, image-printers or art-printers, carriers, and sellers, and each
one in particular, by Roman Imperial power, and under the penalty of ten marks of <term>pure gold</term><note>original: "lötigs golts." Gold of a specific standard of purity.</note>,
half to be paid to Our Imperial chamber and the other half to the aforementioned widow,
without fail; also upon the loss of such prints and reprints, We herewith earnestly
will that no one shall claim for himself the aforementioned late Albrecht Dürer's discovered art books,
nor any excerpt or appendix of the same, as if he had made them, nor otherwise bring them
into print or publish them. Furthermore, that no one shall reprint or carry to the market or sell
the aforementioned books and art, which the said widow has published or would publish in print,
within the established time of ten years, in any way, as
you collectively and individually hold the aforementioned penalty dear, and wish to avoid the seizure of your prints and reprints, which prints and reprints the said widow may thus [take], or those whom she has authorized...

<summary>This page contains an official Imperial Privilege granted by Emperor Charles V to Agnes Dürer, protecting her late husband's work on human proportion from piracy and intellectual theft for a period of ten years.</summary>
<keywords>Charles V, Agnes Dürer, Albrecht Dürer, copyright, printing privilege, human proportion, Holy Roman Empire, book printing, intellectual property</keywords>

--- Page 316 ---
<meta>This page continues from the legal documents following Albrecht Dürer's death. While the previous page focused on the Imperial Privilege granted to his widow Agnes to protect his intellectual property, this page shifts to a deeply personal tribute. It features an elegy and epitaphs written in Latin by Willibald Pirckheimer (1470–1530), a prominent Nuremberg lawyer, humanist, and Dürer's closest lifelong friend.</meta>

# Elegy of Willibald Pirckheimer
## on the death of Albrecht Dürer.

Albrecht, you who were so closely joined to me for so many years,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;and who were the greatest part of my soul, <note>original: "maxima pars animae." This is a classical expression of deep friendship, famously used by Horace to describe Virgil.</note>
With whom I was able to share sweet conversations 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In safety, and pour out words into a faithful heart. 
Why do you, unhappy one, suddenly leave your mourning friend, 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And hurry away with a swift foot, never to return? 
I was not permitted to touch your beloved head, nor your right hand, 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Nor to speak the final "farewell" in sadness. 
But you, as a man, had already committed your weary limbs to the sickbed, 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;When death, hastening, suddenly snatched you away. 
Alas, deceitful hopes, and a mind unaware of the evils to come! 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How quickly all things suddenly fall with a swift slip. 
Favorable fortune had given everything to Dürer: 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Genius, beauty, and faith along with honesty. 
But again, death took everything away with a swift and daring stroke; 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Yet that wicked death was not strong enough to take away your praise. 
For Dürer’s virtue remains, as does his renowned fame; 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;They shall shine as long as the bright stars in the sky. 
Go, glory! Go, you who are not the least honor of our nation; <note>original: "gentis." Pirckheimer refers to the German people, positioning Dürer as a national hero of the arts.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;With **CHRIST** as your guide, seek the heavenly realms. 
There you shall always rejoice in honor that is not in vain, 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Bearing worthy rewards, happy for your merits. 
While we, fragile ones, wander in the shadow of death, 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And glide wretchedly in an unstable boat, <note>A metaphor for the uncertainty and fragility of human life.</note>
But the grace of Christ will be present, preserving us; 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;With Him as our leader, we shall enter upon the happy journey. 
Meanwhile, in our sadness, we shall pour out tears for our friend; 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Nothing can be sweeter to those who are so afflicted. 
And prayers shall be added, which might appease the highest Thunderer, <note>original: "tonantem." A classical epithet for Jupiter, here used by the humanist Pirckheimer to refer to the Christian God.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If pious vows have any power at all. 
And so that nothing is missing from the tomb, we shall scatter scents: 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Narcissus, violets, lilies, garlands, and roses. 
Meanwhile, happy one, rest in blessed sleep; 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For a good man sleeps in Christ—he does not die.

### Epitaph for the same

After Albrecht had adorned the world with his painting, 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And all things were filled with such graceful art, 
"Now only heaven remains," he said, "to be painted on high"; 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Soon, leaving the earth, he sought the bright stars.

### Another

Genius, honesty, integrity, prudence, virtue, 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Art, piety, and faith: here they lie entombed.

<note>Vocabulary: Bilibaldus Pirckeymherus (Willibald Pirckheimer), Albertus Durerus (Albrecht Dürer), elegy, epitaph, elegiac couplets (the poetic meter used here).</note>

<summary>This page contains a moving funeral elegy and two shorter epitaphs written by the humanist Willibald Pirckheimer for his best friend, Albrecht Dürer. The text blends Christian piety with classical Roman poetic forms to immortalize Dürer's character and artistic genius.</summary>
<keywords>Willibald Pirckheimer, Albrecht Dürer, elegy, epitaph, funeral poetry, Humanism, friendship, Renaissance art, grief, Latin poetry</keywords>

->Another tribute by the same author<-

->Albrecht, if life could be returned through tears,<-
->this body would not be covered by this common earth.<-
->But because the fates are not moved by tears, nor by weeping,<-
->through mourning we only pay our funeral rites.<- <note>original: "inferias." These were the sacrifices or honors offered to the souls of the deceased in Roman tradition.</note>

->Sacred to his Blessed Memory<-

To the memory of Albrecht Dürer, a most excellent man / and the most perfect painter of his age / who was not only the first among the Germans to enrich and illuminate the art of painting / and to restrain it to a stricter law, <note>original: "seueriorem legem." This refers to Dürer’s efforts to base art on mathematical principles and the study of human proportion.</note> / but also began to commend it to posterity through his writings / for which reason / but especially for his composed character / his singular prudence and modesty / he was most dear to his fellow citizens of Nuremberg / and indeed to all foreigners / most welcome to the Divine Maximilian / and to his grandson Charles, <note>Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor.</note> / Emperors / as well as to Ferdinand, King of Hungary and Bohemia / who supported him with a large and annual stipend and pursued him with the highest favor. He died, however, not without the great grief of his friends, on the sixth of April, <note>original: "viii. idus Aprilis." Following the Roman calendar, the 8th day before the Ides of April is April 6th.</note> in the year 1528, at the age of 57.

->Willibald Pirckheimer<-
->placed this for his most upright friend.<-

# Correction of several words that were overlooked in printing.

**D** On the first leaf <note>original: "blat." In early printing, pages were often counted by the leaf (front and back) rather than individual page numbers.</note> of the third line/ and the foot prepared in front/ read: is prepared in front.

### Another by the same author <note>original: "Aliud ab eodem." This indicates that the following poem is also by Willibald Pirckheimer, who authored the preceding tributes in the book.</note>

->If life could return through tears, O Albrecht,<-
->this body would not be covered by this lowly earth.<-
->But because the Fates are moved neither by tears nor weeping,<-
->through our mourning, we only perform your funeral rites.<-

### Dedicated to his Blessed Memory <note>original: "M. Beato. S." This is an abbreviation for *Memoriae Beatae Sacrum* (Sacred to his Blessed Memory).</note>

To the memory of Albrecht Dürer/ a man of the highest character/ and the most perfect painter of his age/ who was not only the first among the Germans to advance and bring light to the art of painting/ and to bring it under a stricter rule <note>A reference to Dürer’s pioneering work in applying mathematical laws, geometry, and proportions to German art.</note>/ but also began to commend it to posterity through his writings/ for which reason/ and especially for his disciplined manners/ his wisdom and singular modesty/ he was most dear to his fellow citizens of Nuremberg/ and indeed to all foreigners/ He was most welcome to the Divine Maximilian <note>Emperor Maximilian I (reigned 1493–1519), Dürer's greatest patron.</note>/ and to his grandson Charles <note>Emperor Charles V (reigned 1519–1556).</note>/ the Emperors/ as well as to Ferdinand, King of Hungary and Bohemia <note>Ferdinand I, the brother of Charles V.</note>/ who supported him with a large annual stipend and treated him with the highest favor. He passed away, however, to the great longing of his friends, on the 6th of April <note>original: "viii. idus Aprilis." Using the Roman calendar, the 8th day before the Ides of April is April 6th.</note>, in the year 1528, at the age of 57.

->Willibald Pirckheimer<-
->placed this monument for his most upright friend.<- <note>original: "M. P.," an abbreviation for *Monumentum Posuit*.</note>

<image-desc>A calligraphic flourish or vignette at the bottom of the text, composed of two symmetrical swirling loops joined in the center, creating a decorative horizontal knot-like ornament.</image-desc>

<summary>This page features a moving tribute and formal epitaph for Albrecht Dürer, written by his friend Willibald Pirckheimer. It celebrates Dürer not only as a master painter and theorist but also as a man of great character who was esteemed by the Holy Roman Emperors.</summary>

<meta>This page serves as a formal memorial or epitaph for Albrecht Dürer, concluding the "Four Books on Human Proportion." It follows the technical errata list and provides a solemn, scholarly tribute to Dürer's life and legacy, likely written by his close friends Beatus Rhenanus and Willibald Pirckheimer.</meta>

### Another by the same author <note>original: "Aliud ab eodem"; this refers to Beatus Rhenanus, a prominent German humanist scholar who likely composed this short poem.</note>

> If life could return through tears, Albrecht,
> This body would not be covered by this common earth.
> But because the fates are not moved by tears or weeping,
> We pay our funeral rites only through mourning.

->Beatus Rhenanus<- <note>original: "M. Beatus. S." An abbreviation for "Magister Beatus [Rhenanus] Salutem."</note>

To the memory of Albrecht Dürer, a most excellent man and the most perfect painter of his age. He was not only the first among the Germans to expand and illuminate the art of painting and to bind it to a stricter law <note>original: "ad ſeveriorem legem reſtrinxit"; a reference to Dürer’s application of mathematical and geometric rules to art</note>, but he also began to commend it to posterity through his writings. For these achievements—and especially for his disciplined character, singular prudence, and modesty—he was most dear to his fellow citizens in Nuremberg, and indeed to all foreigners. He was also most highly esteemed by the Divine Maximilian and his grandson Charles, Emperors <note>Holy Roman Emperors Maximilian I and Charles V</note>, as well as by Ferdinand, King of Hungary and Bohemia, who supported him with a generous annual stipend and treated him with the highest favor. He died, however, to the great grief of his friends, on the 6th of April <note>original: "viij. idus Aprilis"; using the Roman calendar, the 8th day before the Ides of April is April 6th</note>, in the year 1528, at the age of 57.

->Willibald Pirckheimer<- <note>A wealthy lawyer and humanist who was Dürer's closest lifelong friend.</note>
->placed this for his most upright friend.<- <note>original: "amico integerrimo. M. P." where M.P. stands for "Monumentum Posuit" (placed this monument).</note>

<image-desc>A large, elaborate calligraphic flourish (paraph) featuring two main interlocking horizontal loops forming a stylized figure-eight, with several delicate swirling extensions.</image-desc>

<summary>This page contains a Latin epitaph and a formal biographical tribute to Albrecht Dürer, highlighting his role in elevating German art through mathematical principles and his high standing among the imperial elite.</summary>
